Root systems, Tits cones and imaginary cones of Brink-Howlett groupoids
Matthew Dyer, Harrison Gimenez

Abstract
. We extend the basic theory of the groupoids introduced by Brink and Howlett in their study of normalizers of parabolic subgroups of Coxeter groups, by studying both their abstract root systems and root systems realized in real vector spaces. Such root systems have some properties formally analogous to those of root systems of Borcherds-Kac-Moody Lie algebras; in particular, some contain imaginary simple roots. Further, positive roots correspond to certain reflection subgroups. We also extend the most basic properties of the Tits cone and imaginary cone of Coxeter groups to corresponding cones defined for Brink-Howlett groupoids. The results linearize the study of certain classes of reflection subgroups of Coxeter groups in a similar way as root systems of Coxeter groups linearize the study of reflections.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
20F55; 17B22, 20L05

Introduction

Let
(W,S)
be a Coxeter system with standard root system
\Phi
. The subgroups generated by subsets of the set
S
of
simple reflections are called standard parabolic subgroups, and
their conjugates are called parabolic subgroups. Both types of
subgroups are important in the study of Coxeter groups and in areas in which Coxeter groups find application, such as in the
structure and representation theory of semisimple algebraic
groups and Kac-Moody groups and Lie algebras.

In their study
[
9
]
of normalizers of standard parabolic subgroups, Brink and Howlett studied the components of a
groupoid in which the objects are the subsets of the simple roots and a morphism
J\to K
is
given by an element
w\in W
satisfying
w(J)=K
. We will call
a component (or union of components) of this groupoid a Brink Howlett groupoid.

This paper considers various notions of root systems of Brink-Howlett groupoids. We describe both abstract root systems
and root systems realized as subsets of real vector spaces.
The most interesting and useful of these root systems have a set of roots based at each object
J
of the groupoid, with the positive
roots based at
J
corresponding to a set of corank one reflection overgroups (in
W
) of the standard parabolic
subgroup
W_{J}
with
J
as its set of simple roots. The study of these root systems leads to study of versions “relative to
J
” of various basic facts about Coxeter systems which were previously known in the case
J={\emptyset}
. For example, Tits theorem that a finite subgroup of a Coxeter group is contained in a finite parabolic subgroup is generalized to the statement that a finite index overgroup of
W_{J}
in
W
is contained in a parabolic subgroup of
W
which is also a finite index overgroup of
W_{J}
.

Such root systems
in real vector spaces linearize the study of certain classes of
reflection subgroups in a similar way as root systems linearize
the study of reflections. We give a
direct elementary proof that a Brink-Howlett groupoid has
certain favorable lattice theoretic properties (in regard to its
weak orders, defined by inclusion of inversion sets in its root system) which may be summarized by the statement that
it underlies a principal rootoid (see e.g.
[
19
]
) and to prove that the Brink-Howlett generators of Brink-Howlett groupoids are the specialization to those groupoids of the standard (simple) generators of principal rootoids. This makes the extensive (but largely unpublished) general theory of principal rootoids directly applicable to the study of Brink-Howlett groupoids, and provides interesting and important examples for that general theory.

Some of the root systems we consider have certain features formally
analogous to those of root systems of Borcherds-Kac-Moody
Lie algebras (see
[
32
]
). In particular, they may contain imaginary simple
roots, in a natural sense. In the case of root systems realized in
real vector spaces, inclusion of such imaginary simple roots in
the root system is necessary in order to ensure that each
positive real root is expressible as a non-negative linear
combination of simple roots and thereby to generalize the basic notion of root coefficient.

We also define, for suitable root systems of Brink-Howlett groupoids in real vector spaces, natural analogues of the Tits cone and imaginary cone of a Coxeter group. The definitions involve both real and imaginary roots. The basic fact that the stabilizer of a subset of the fundamental chamber of the Tits cone of a Coxeter group is a standard parabolic subgroup is generalized to the fact that
the “pointwise stabilizer groupoid” of a subset of a fundamental chamber of a Brink-Howlett groupoid’s Tits cone is another Brink-Howlett groupoid, which one may view as an analogue for Brink-Howlett groupoids of a standard parabolic subgroup.

This paper emphasizes basic results and phenomena for Brink-Howlett groupoids for which there are no extensions known (or in some cases, possible) to principal rootoids in general. The focus is mainly on imaginary roots and realizations of the root systems in real vector spaces. The results suggest many natural directions for further study of Brink-Howlett (and other) groupoids with root systems, and applications to the study of Coxeter groups.

1.
Overview of results

1.1.
Coxeter groups and root systems

Let
V
be a real vector space.
For subsets
X
and
Y
of
V
, we write
X+Y:=\{\,x+y\mid x\in X,y\in Y\,\}
,
cX:=\{\,cx\mid x\in X\,\}
and
-X:=(-1)X
. For
X\subseteq V
, let
\operatorname{span}(X)
denote the linear span of
X\cup\{0\}
and
\operatorname{cone}(X)
denote the convex cone in
V
spanned by
X\cup\{0\}
.

As a general convention, in writing a sum
\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}
of elements
(a_{i})_{i\in I}
of an abelian group (e.g. a vector space), we tacitly assume that
a_{i}=0
for almost all (i.e. all but finitely many)
i\in I
. When such a sum is, more specifically, an
R
-linear combination
\sum_{i\in I}r_{i}m_{i}
of a family of elements
(m_{i})_{i\in I}
of some left
R
-module
M
over a ring
R
, we assume more strongly that
r_{i}=0
for almost all
i\in I
. In both cases, empty sums have value
0
, by convention.
In particular, for
X\subseteq V
,

\operatorname{span}(X)=\{\,\sum_{\alpha\in X}c_{\alpha}\alpha\mid c_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}\text{ \rm for all $\alpha\in X$}\,\}

and

\operatorname{cone}(X)=\{\,\sum_{\alpha\in X}c_{\alpha}\alpha\mid c_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\text{ \rm for all $\alpha\in X$}\,\}.

As references for elementary background we use from convex geometry, see for instance
[
39
]
,
[
6
, Ch II, §2]
and
[
2
]
.

1.2.

By a
real quadratic space
, we shall mean in this paper a pair
(V,B)
consisting of a real vector space
V
and a symmetric bilinear form
B\colon V\times V\to\mathbb{R}
.

Fix a real quadratic space
(V,B)
.
Let
\mathrm{O}=\mathrm{O}(V,B)
denote the
orthogonal group
of
(V,B)
, which is the group of automorphisms of
(V,B)
. The elements of
\mathrm{O}
are the invertible linear maps
g\colon V\to V
which preserve the form
B
, in the sense that
B(gx,gy)=B(x,y)
for all
x,y\in V
. An element
\alpha
of
V
is said to be
non-isotropic
if
B(\alpha,\alpha)\neq 0
. For non-isotropic
\alpha\in V
, write
\alpha^{\vee}:=\frac{2}{B(\alpha,\alpha)}\alpha
and define the
reflection

s_{\alpha}\in O
by
s_{\alpha}(v)=v-B(v,\alpha^{\vee})\alpha
for
v\in V
.

1.3.

Let
(\Phi,\Pi)
be a based root system in
(V,B)
in the sense of
[
24
, 2.3]
, with positive roots
\Phi^{+}
and associated Coxeter system
(W,S)
. This means that:

(i)

(V,B)
is a real quadratic space

(ii)

\Pi\subseteq V
satisfies
\operatorname{cone}(\Pi)\cap\operatorname{cone}(-\Pi)=\{0\}
.

(iii)

for all
\alpha\in\Pi
, one has
\alpha\not\in\operatorname{cone}(\Pi\setminus\{\alpha\})
and
B(\alpha,\alpha)=1
.

(iv)

S:=\{s_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in\Pi\}
and
W:=\langle S\rangle
is the subgroup of
O
generated by
S
.

(v)

\Phi:=\{w\alpha\mid w\in W,\alpha\in\Pi\}
satisfies

\Phi=\Phi^{+}\cup\Phi^{-}
where
\Phi^{+}:=\Phi\cap\operatorname{cone}(\Pi)
and
\Phi^{-}:=\Phi\cap\operatorname{cone}(-\Pi)
.

These conditions imply that
\Pi
is
positively independent
. That is, if
\sum_{\alpha\in\Pi}c_{\alpha}\alpha=0
with
c_{\alpha}\geq 0
for all
\alpha\in\Pi
, then
c_{\alpha}=0
for all
\alpha
.

See
[
24
]
,
[
4
]
and
[
22
]
for more details. For example,
\Phi
could be the standard root
system of
(W,S)
, with simple roots
\Pi
forming a basis of
V
and
B
the standard symmetric bilinear form on
V
, as
described in
[
5
]
,
[
31
]
,
[
3
]
and
[
1
]
. We assume the
reader is familiar with basic facts concerning
Coxeter systems and their root systems, as may be found in those references.

1.4.

Let
T:=\{wsw^{-1}\mid w\in W,s\in S\}=\{\,s_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in\Phi\,\}
. Elements of
\Pi
,
\Phi
,
\Phi^{+}
,
\Phi^{-}
,
T
and
S
are called

simple roots
,
roots
,
positive roots
,

negative roots
,
reflections
and
simple
reflections
, respectively.

By definition, the
rank
of the Coxeter system
(W,S)

is
|S|
. From
[
20
]
, for example, the rank is the minimum of the cardinalities
of sets of reflections which generate
W
. Unless
otherwise stated, Coxeter systems are not assumed in this paper to be of
finite rank.

The
standard length function
of
(W,S)
is denoted

\ell
or
\ell_{(W,S)}
.
The map
\alpha\mapsto s_{\alpha}\colon\Phi^{+}\to T
is a
bijection.
For
w\in W
, the
left inversion set

\Phi_{w}

of
w
is

(1.4.1)

\Phi_{w}:=\Phi^{+}\cap w(-\Phi^{+}).

Define

(1.4.2)

N(w):=\{\,s_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in\Phi_{w}\,\}=\{t\in T\mid\ell(tw)<\ell(w)\}.

If
x,y\in W
, we have

(1.4.3)

N(xy)=N(x)+xN(y)x^{-1},\qquad\Phi_{xy}=(\Phi_{x}\setminus(-x\Phi_{y}))\cup(x(\Phi_{y})\setminus-\Phi_{x})

where
+
denotes symmetric difference on the power set
\mathcal{P}(T)
of
T
(that is,
A+B=(A\cup B)\setminus(A\cap B)=(A\setminus B)\cup(B\setminus A)
for
A,B\subseteq T
) . Further,

(1.4.4)

\ell(x)=|N(x)|=|\Phi_{x}|,\qquad\text{\rm if $x\in W$}.

1.5.

The
weak (right) order
of
W
is the partial order
\leq
on
W
defined by

x\leq y\iff\Phi_{x}\subseteq\Phi_{y}\iff N(x)\subseteq N(y)\iff\ell(y)=\ell(x)+\ell(x^{-1}y),

for
x,y\in W
. One has

x\leq xy\iff\Phi_{x^{-1}}\cap\Phi_{y}={\emptyset}\iff N(x^{-1})\cap N(y)={\emptyset}\iff\ell(xy)=\ell(x)+\ell(y).

The poset
(W,\leq)
is a complete meet semilattice (see
[
3
, Theorem 3.2.1]
).

1.6.

We say loosely that the pair
(\Phi,\Pi)
, with
\Pi\subseteq\Phi
regarded as subsets of the real vector
space
V
equipped with its bilinear form
B
and
W
-action,
is a
realized root system
of
(W,S)
. There may be uncountably many pairwise non-isomorphic (in the natural
sense) realized root systems of a fixed (finite rank) Coxeter
system
(W,S)
, even restricting to those for which
\Pi
is a basis of
V
.

On the other hand, one may regard
(\Phi,\Pi)
more abstractly
as a pair consisting of a
W\times\{\pm 1\}
-set
\Phi

together with a subset
\Pi
of
\Phi
and a specified bijection

\alpha\mapsto s_{\alpha}\colon\Pi\to S
. As such, it depends
only on
(W,S)
, up to the natural notion of isomorphism of
such pairs. We shall call such a pair an
abstract root
system
of
(W,S)
. Two well-known descriptions of the
(unique up to isomorphism) abstract root system directly in
terms of the Coxeter system
(W,S)
are recalled in
2.5
–
2.6
.

Many of the results discussed in
1.7
–
1.43
are of a purely algebraic and combinatorial nature, in that they can
be formulated in terms of the abstract root system of
(W,S)
, though we do not always do so when possible; however,
currently known proofs of many of them require use of realized root systems. Later results of this section typically depend in an
essential way on a choice of realized root system.

1.7.

The subgroups
W_{J}:=\langle\,J\,\rangle
of
W
, for
J\subseteq S
, are called
standard
parabolic subgroups
. Conjugates
wW_{J}w^{-1}
, where
w\in W
and
J\subseteq S
, of standard parabolic subgroups are called

parabolic subgroups
.

In this and similar notation,
we use indexing by sets of reflections, or by the corresponding sets of positive roots, interchangeably. For example,
if
\Gamma\subseteq\Pi
, then
W_{\Gamma}:=W_{J}
where
J:=\{\,s_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in\Gamma\,\}
.
More generally, for any subset
\Gamma
of roots, we write
W_{\Gamma}=W_{R}=\langle\,R\,\rangle
where
R=\{\,s_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in\Gamma\,\}
.

A subset
J
of
\Pi
, the corresponding subset
\{\,s_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in J\,\}
of simple reflections, or the
subgroup
W_{J}
itself, is said to be
spherical
if
W_{J}
is finite.
In that case, we denote the
longest element
of
W_{J}
as
w_{J}
; it satisfies
(w_{J})^{2}=1
,
N(w_{J})=W_{J}\cap T
and
w_{J}J=-J
.

1.8.
Brink-Howlett groupoids

Recall that a
groupoid
is a small category
G
in which every morphism is invertible. Such a groupoid is determined by its set

\mathrm{Ob}(G)
of objects, the hom-sets
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{b}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.44pt{b}\kern 6.22598pt{a}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{b}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.44pt{b}\kern 6.22598pt{a}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{b}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-5.4658pt{b}\kern 3.85262pt{a}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{b}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-4.36505pt{b}\kern 2.75186pt{a}}}}:=\mathrm{Hom}_{G}(a,b)
for
a,b\in\mathrm{Ob}(G)
and composition maps

(g,f)\mapsto gf\colon\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{c}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.4576pt{c}\kern 6.22598pt{b}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{c}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.4576pt{c}\kern 6.22598pt{b}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{c}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-5.47836pt{c}\kern 3.85262pt{b}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{c}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-4.37761pt{c}\kern 2.75186pt{b}}}}\times\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{b}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.44pt{b}\kern 6.22598pt{a}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{b}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.44pt{b}\kern 6.22598pt{a}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{b}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-5.4658pt{b}\kern 3.85262pt{a}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{b}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-4.36505pt{b}\kern 2.75186pt{a}}}}\to\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{c}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.4576pt{c}\kern 6.22598pt{a}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{c}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.4576pt{c}\kern 6.22598pt{a}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{c}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-5.47836pt{c}\kern 3.85262pt{a}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{c}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-4.37761pt{c}\kern 2.75186pt{a}}}}

subject to suitable (associativity, unit and inverse) conditions.

A groupoid
H
is said to be
connected
if it has at least
one object and there is a morphism
a\to b
for any objects
a
and
b
of
H
. The
inclusion-maximal connected subgroupoids
H
of
G
are called the
connected components
, or
components
, of
G
.

By a
representation
of the groupoid
G
in a category
C
, we mean a functor
F\colon G\to C
. In case
C
is a
concrete category, given by a faithful “underlying set” functor
U\colon C\to\mathrm{Set}

(which is frequently suppressed from the notation)
to the category of sets, we often abbreviate
gx:=(U(F(g)))(x)\in U(F(b))
for
g\in\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{b}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.44pt{b}\kern 6.22598pt{a}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{b}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.44pt{b}\kern 6.22598pt{a}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{b}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-5.4658pt{b}\kern 3.85262pt{a}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{b}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-4.36505pt{b}\kern 2.75186pt{a}}}}
and
x\in U(F(a))
. Then

F
is characterized by “action maps”

(g,x)\mapsto gx\colon\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{b}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.44pt{b}\kern 6.22598pt{a}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{b}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.44pt{b}\kern 6.22598pt{a}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{b}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-5.4658pt{b}\kern 3.85262pt{a}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{b}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-4.36505pt{b}\kern 2.75186pt{a}}}}\times U(F(a))\to U(F(b))

subject to suitable (associativity and unit) conditions.

1.9.

In their study
[
9
]
of normalizers of standard parabolic subgroups, Brink and Howlett implicitly defined a groupoid
\widehat{G}
as follows.

The set of objects of
\widehat{G}
is
\operatorname{ob}(\widehat{G}):=\mathcal{P}(\Pi)
, the power set of
\Pi
. For two subsets
J
and
K
of
\Pi
, a morphism
J\to K
in
\widehat{G}
is by definition a triple

(K,w,J)
where
K=w(J)
; we may write such a morphism simply as
w\colon J\to K
or even just
w
if
J
and/or
K

are understood. Composition of morphisms is induced by multiplication in
W
:
(L,v,K)(K,w,J)=(L,vw,J)
.

We call
\widehat{G}
the
full Brink-Howlett groupoid
of
(W,S)
. Brink and Howlett mainly studied the connected
components of
\widehat{G}
. By a
Brink-Howlett groupoid
(of
(W,S)
), we mean any groupoid
G
which is a union of
components of
\widehat{G}
.

Example 1.10
.

Let
J\subseteq\Pi
and
\alpha\in\Phi^{+}\cap J^{\perp}
, where for any subset
U
of
V
,

U^{\perp}:=\{\,v\in V\mid B(v,u)=0\text{ \rm for all $u\in U$}\,\}

Then
(J,s_{\alpha},J)
is a morphism in
\widehat{G}
. Even for

W
of type
B_{2}
, this morphism does not in general lie in the subgroupoid generated by morphisms
(J,s_{\beta},J)
with
\beta\in\Pi\,\cap\,\alpha^{\perp}
.

1.11.

Brink and Howlett
[
9
]
showed that Brink-Howlett groupoids have several Coxeter group-like
features, including an analogue of the presentation of Coxeter groups by simple generators subject to braid relations.
They also showed that for any object
J
, a certain subgroup of the vertex group of morphisms
J\to J
(containing in particular
all morphisms discussed in Example
1.10
) is a Coxeter
group with root system naturally constructed from a realized
root system
\Phi
of
(W,S)
. However, Brink and Howlett’s paper does not develop notions of root systems of Brink-Howlett groupoids.

Similar Coxeter group-like groupoids also appear, with root systems, in the literature as Coxeter (and Weyl) groupoids (see
for instance
[
10
]
and
[
27
]
), although the Brink-Howlett groupoids of infinite Coxeter groups are not of that type in general.

Rudiments of a more general framework (rootoids, especially principal rootoids) including the Brink Howlett groupoids, and Coxeter and Weyl groupoids, are indicated in

[
15
]
–
[
16
]
and
[
19
]
. The main novelties in the theory of rootoids are its lattice- and category-theoretic underpinnings, and consequent closure of classes of
rootoids under various natural category-theoretic constructions.

The underlying groupoid of a principal rootoid admits a
canonical presentation by generators and relations generalizing those of Coxeter groups, Coxeter groupoids and
Brink-Howlett groupoids.
A principal rootoid also admits a canonical analogue of the abstract root system of a Coxeter group, but, even if finite, does not in general have a “realized root system” in a real vector
space, because of the existence of non-realizable oriented matroids (see
[
19
]
). Finite, complete, principal rootoids admit suitable “oriented matroid root systems” by

[
19
]
, but it is an open question whether (or under what conditions) infinite principal rootoids do.

Our first main goal in this paper is to interpret the standard abstract root system of a Brink-Howlett groupoid (which can be
shown to be the standard root system of the corresponding
principal rootoid, though we do not define this) directly in terms of
the associated Coxeter system. We show that positive roots
correspond to certain reflection subgroups, and the standard abstract root system may be naturally extended in various ways
(e.g. allowing analogues of imaginary roots and non-reduced root systems) to
abstract root systems with positive roots corresponding to larger and more natural classes of reflection subgroups. Our second
main goal is to study realizations of these abstract root systems in real vector spaces. We obtain a satisfactory realization for some
of these root systems, including the standard abstract root system,
and those we consider with only real roots. Finally, we extend the
definitions and some basic properties of Tits and imaginary cones of Coxeter groups to these realized root systems.

1.12.
Signed groupoid-sets

We shall not discuss rootoids in this paper, but will make use of the very closely related notion of rootoidal signed groupoid-sets. These
are not as well behaved category-theoretically as rootoids but are more concrete and better adapted for purposes here. Signed groupoid-sets
are discussed in
1.13
–
1.20
and Section
3
; for more detail and proofs, see
[
19
]
. For basic
terminology and facts concerning ordered sets and lattices as used in this paper, see for example
[
11
]
.

1.13.

The category
\mathbf{Set}_{\pm}
of
definitely signed sets
has as its objects sets
X
with a specified free action of the
sign group

\{\pm 1\}=\{\pm\}
and a distinguished set
X^{+}
of orbit
representatives, called
positive elements
of
X
. Let
X^{-}:=-X^{+}=\{\,-x\mid x\in X^{+}\,\}
. The
sign
of
x\in X
is
\epsilon\in\{\pm\}
where
x\in X^{\epsilon}
.
Morphisms of definitely signed sets are

\{\pm\}
-equivariant maps, not necessarily preserving positive elements, with composition by composition of underlying functions.

A
signed groupoid-set
is by definition a pair
(G,X)
where
G
is a groupoid and
X\colon G\to\mathbf{Set}_{\pm}
is a functor. We refer to
(G,X)
, or just
X
for short, as a
signed
G
-set
.
Since
\mathbf{Set}_{\pm}
is
a concrete category, we may (and usually do) describe signed

G
-sets by their action maps

(g,x)\mapsto gx\colon\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{b}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.44pt{b}\kern 6.22598pt{a}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{b}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.44pt{b}\kern 6.22598pt{a}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{b}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-5.4658pt{b}\kern 3.85262pt{a}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{b}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-4.36505pt{b}\kern 2.75186pt{a}}}}\times X(a)\to X(b),

which in particular satisfy
g(-x)=-gx
. Here
gx:=(X(g))(x)
. Elements of
X(a)
,
X(a)^{+}
and

X(a)^{-}
respectively are called
roots
,
positive roots
and
negative
roots
of
X
(
at

a
or
based at

a
). We say a root in
X(a)
has
a
as its

base
(tacitly assuming in doing so that the sets
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}X^{{\kern-6.71323pt\kern 7.10211pt}}_{{\kern-9.80331pt{a}\kern 7.10211pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}X^{{\kern-6.71323pt\kern 7.10211pt}}_{{\kern-9.80331pt{a}\kern 7.10211pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}X^{{\kern-4.05513pt\kern 4.44402pt}}_{{\kern-6.40518pt{a}\kern 4.44402pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}X^{{\kern-2.78542pt\kern 3.1743pt}}_{{\kern-5.13547pt{a}\kern 3.1743pt}}}:=X(a)
for
a\in\operatorname{ob}(G)
are pairwise disjoint, as we do by convention unless otherwise stated).

1.14.

There is a natural notion of morphism of signed groupoid-sets (see
[
15
]
–
[
16
]
but we shall not use it in this paper. However, the notion of a morphism of signed
G
-sets
(for fixed
G
) defined below will be used extensively.

Let
(G,X)
and
(G,Y)
be signed
G
-sets, where
G
is a groupoid.
A morphism
\nu\colon(G,X)\to(G,Y)
is defined to be a natural transformation

\nu\colon X\to Y
of functors
G\to{\mathbf{Set}}_{\pm}
for which the components
\nu_{a}\colon X(a)\to Y(a)
for
a\in\operatorname{ob}(G)
, which are morphisms of signed sets, are also
positivity
preserving
in the sense they restrict to maps
X(a)^{+}\to Y(a)^{+}
. This gives rise to a category of signed
G
-sets, and thereby provides a natural notion of
isomorphism
of signed
G
-sets.

We say the above morphism
\nu
is an
embedding
of signed
G
-sets if each component
\nu_{a}
with

a\in\operatorname{ob}(G)
is injective as a map of sets. We then write
(G,X)\hookrightarrow(G,Y)
.

We say that
(G,X)
is a
signed
G
-subset
of
(G,Y)

if there is an embedding
\nu\colon(G,X)\hookrightarrow(G,Y)
such that for each object
a
of
G
, the underlying function of
the component
\nu_{a}\colon X(a)\to Y(a)
is an inclusion map
(so in particular,
X(a)\subseteq Y(a)
).
By abuse of notation, we then write
(G,X)\subseteq(G,Y)
.
If there is an embedding
(G,X)\hookrightarrow(G,Y)
, then
(G,X)
is isomorphic to a signed
G
-subset of
(G,Y)
.

1.15.

Let
(G,X)
be a signed groupoid-set. For any subgroupoid
H
of
G
, one has a signed groupoid-set
(H,X_{|H})
, called the restriction of

(G,X)
to
H
, where the functor
X_{|H}\colon H\to{\mathbf{Set}}_{\pm}
is the restriction of
X\colon G\to{\mathbf{Set}}_{\pm}
. The restriction of
(G,X)
to a connected component
H
of
G
is
called a (connected) component of
(G,X)
.

Example 1.16
.

Regard the Coxeter group
W
as a one-object groupoid
W_{\bullet}
with object
\bullet
and

W
as the group of automorphisms of that object. Then the abstract root system
\Phi
of
(W,S)
naturally gives rise to a
signed
W_{\bullet}
-set
(W_{\bullet},\Phi_{\bullet})
; here,
\Phi_{\bullet}
is the functor taking the object
\bullet
of

W_{\bullet}
to the signed set
\Phi
, and a morphism
w
of
W_{\bullet}
(i.e. an element
w
of
W
) to the
\{\pm\}
-equivariant action by
w
on
\Phi
.

1.17.

Signed groupoid-sets are of greatest interest when they satisfy stringent additional
conditions, discussed below, abstracted from those satisfied in
example
1.16
. See
Section
3
,
[
15
]
and
[
19
]
for more details.

Let
(G,X)
be a signed groupoid-set. For an object
a
of
G
, the
left star
of
a
is the set
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-5.81378pt{a}\kern 3.85262pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-4.71303pt{a}\kern 2.75186pt}}}=\dot{\bigcup}_{b\in\operatorname{ob}(G)}\,\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt{b}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt{b}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-5.81378pt{a}\kern 3.85262pt{b}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-4.71303pt{a}\kern 2.75186pt{b}}}}
of morphisms of
G
with codomain
a
. Here, we regard distinct
\hom
-sets of a
category as pairwise disjoint, as is a common convention.

The
left inversion set

X_{g}
of
g\in\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-5.81378pt{a}\kern 3.85262pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-4.71303pt{a}\kern 2.75186pt}}}
is the set

(1.17.1)

X_{g}:=\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}X^{{\kern-6.71323pt\kern 7.10211pt}}_{{\kern-9.80331pt{a}\kern 7.10211pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}X^{{\kern-6.71323pt\kern 7.10211pt}}_{{\kern-9.80331pt{a}\kern 7.10211pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}X^{{\kern-4.05513pt\kern 4.44402pt}}_{{\kern-6.40518pt{a}\kern 4.44402pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}X^{{\kern-2.78542pt\kern 3.1743pt}}_{{\kern-5.13547pt{a}\kern 3.1743pt}}}^{+}\cap g(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{b}}}}X^{{\kern-6.71323pt\kern 7.10211pt}}_{{\kern-9.31613pt{b}\kern 7.10211pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{b}}}}X^{{\kern-6.71323pt\kern 7.10211pt}}_{{\kern-9.31613pt{b}\kern 7.10211pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{b}}}}X^{{\kern-4.05513pt\kern 4.44402pt}}_{{\kern-6.0572pt{b}\kern 4.44402pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{b}}}}X^{{\kern-2.78542pt\kern 3.1743pt}}_{{\kern-4.78749pt{b}\kern 3.1743pt}}}^{-}),\quad g\in\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt{b}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt{b}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-5.81378pt{a}\kern 3.85262pt{b}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-4.71303pt{a}\kern 2.75186pt{b}}}}

of
positive roots based at
a
which are made negative by
g^{-1}
. Also define

(1.17.2)

\widehat{X}_{g}=\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}X^{{\kern-6.71323pt\kern 7.10211pt}}_{{\kern-9.80331pt{a}\kern 7.10211pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}X^{{\kern-6.71323pt\kern 7.10211pt}}_{{\kern-9.80331pt{a}\kern 7.10211pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}X^{{\kern-4.05513pt\kern 4.44402pt}}_{{\kern-6.40518pt{a}\kern 4.44402pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}X^{{\kern-2.78542pt\kern 3.1743pt}}_{{\kern-5.13547pt{a}\kern 3.1743pt}}}^{+}+g(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{b}}}}X^{{\kern-6.71323pt\kern 7.10211pt}}_{{\kern-9.31613pt{b}\kern 7.10211pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{b}}}}X^{{\kern-6.71323pt\kern 7.10211pt}}_{{\kern-9.31613pt{b}\kern 7.10211pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{b}}}}X^{{\kern-4.05513pt\kern 4.44402pt}}_{{\kern-6.0572pt{b}\kern 4.44402pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{b}}}}X^{{\kern-2.78542pt\kern 3.1743pt}}_{{\kern-4.78749pt{b}\kern 3.1743pt}}}^{+})

where
A+B:=(A\setminus B)\cup(B\setminus A)=(A\cup B)\setminus(A\cap B)
denotes symmetric difference. Essentially by definition, the map
g\mapsto\widehat{X}_{g}
defines a

1
-coboundary for
G
, so we have the
1
-cocycle property

(1.17.3)

\widehat{X}_{gh}=\widehat{X}_{g}+g(\widehat{X}_{h}),

for morphisms
g,h
with the composite
gh
defined.
Observe that we have

(1.17.4)

X_{g}=\widehat{X}_{g}\cap\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}X^{{\kern-6.71323pt\kern 7.10211pt}}_{{\kern-9.80331pt{a}\kern 7.10211pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}X^{{\kern-6.71323pt\kern 7.10211pt}}_{{\kern-9.80331pt{a}\kern 7.10211pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}X^{{\kern-4.05513pt\kern 4.44402pt}}_{{\kern-6.40518pt{a}\kern 4.44402pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}X^{{\kern-2.78542pt\kern 3.1743pt}}_{{\kern-5.13547pt{a}\kern 3.1743pt}}}^{+},\qquad\widehat{X}_{g}=X_{g}\,\dot{\cup}-X_{g}.

Note also that for any morphism
\rho\colon(G,X)\to(G,Y)
of signed groupoid sets and any
a\in\operatorname{ob}(G)
and

g\in\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-5.81378pt{a}\kern 3.85262pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-4.71303pt{a}\kern 2.75186pt}}}
, we have
\rho_{a}(X_{g})\subseteq Y_{g}
, with equality for all
a
if
\rho_{a}(X(a))=Y(a)
for all
a
.

1.18.

The
weak (right) preorder
of
(G,X)
based at
a
is the pre-order
(that is, reflexive, transitive relation)
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.22745pt{a}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.22745pt{a}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\leq^{{\kern-5.69846pt\kern 6.08734pt}}_{{\kern-8.04851pt{a}\kern 6.08734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\leq^{{\kern-4.60957pt\kern 4.99846pt}}_{{\kern-6.95963pt{a}\kern 4.99846pt}}}
on
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-5.81378pt{a}\kern 3.85262pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-4.71303pt{a}\kern 2.75186pt}}}
defined by

g\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.22745pt{a}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.22745pt{a}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\leq^{{\kern-5.69846pt\kern 6.08734pt}}_{{\kern-8.04851pt{a}\kern 6.08734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\leq^{{\kern-4.60957pt\kern 4.99846pt}}_{{\kern-6.95963pt{a}\kern 4.99846pt}}}h\iff X_{g}\subseteq X_{h},\qquad g,h\in\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-5.81378pt{a}\kern 3.85262pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-4.71303pt{a}\kern 2.75186pt}}}.

We say
(G,X)
is
faithful
if for any object
a
of
G
and any morphisms
g,g^{\prime}\in\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-5.81378pt{a}\kern 3.85262pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-4.71303pt{a}\kern 2.75186pt}}}
, one has
X_{g}=X_{g^{\prime}}
if and only if
g=g^{\prime}
. Equivalently,
(G,X)
is
faithful
if and only if for each
a\in\operatorname{ob}(G)
, the weak preorder

(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-5.81378pt{a}\kern 3.85262pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-4.71303pt{a}\kern 2.75186pt}}},\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.22745pt{a}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.22745pt{a}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\leq^{{\kern-5.69846pt\kern 6.08734pt}}_{{\kern-8.04851pt{a}\kern 6.08734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\leq^{{\kern-4.60957pt\kern 4.99846pt}}_{{\kern-6.95963pt{a}\kern 4.99846pt}}})
is a partial order, which is then
called the
weak order
of
(G,X)
at
a
. One says that
(G,\Lambda)
is
interval finite
if it is faithful and for any

a\in\operatorname{ob}(G)
, all closed intervals in the weak order
(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-5.81378pt{a}\kern 3.85262pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-4.71303pt{a}\kern 2.75186pt}}},\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.22745pt{a}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.22745pt{a}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\leq^{{\kern-5.69846pt\kern 6.08734pt}}_{{\kern-8.04851pt{a}\kern 6.08734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\leq^{{\kern-4.60957pt\kern 4.99846pt}}_{{\kern-6.95963pt{a}\kern 4.99846pt}}})
are finite.

We say that
(G,X)
is
rootoidal
if it is faithful and for each
a\in\mathrm{Ob}(G)
,

(i)

(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-5.81378pt{a}\kern 3.85262pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-4.71303pt{a}\kern 2.75186pt}}},\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.22745pt{a}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.22745pt{a}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\leq^{{\kern-5.69846pt\kern 6.08734pt}}_{{\kern-8.04851pt{a}\kern 6.08734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\leq^{{\kern-4.60957pt\kern 4.99846pt}}_{{\kern-6.95963pt{a}\kern 4.99846pt}}})
is a complete meet semilattice (i.e. any non-empty subset
P
of
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-5.81378pt{a}\kern 3.85262pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-4.71303pt{a}\kern 2.75186pt}}}
has a meet
\bigwedge P
).

(ii)

if
P\subseteq\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-5.81378pt{a}\kern 3.85262pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-4.71303pt{a}\kern 2.75186pt}}}
and
q\in\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-5.81378pt{a}\kern 3.85262pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-4.71303pt{a}\kern 2.75186pt}}}
are such that

X_{p}\cap X_{q}=\emptyset
for each
p\in P
, and if the join
p^{\prime}:=\bigvee P
in
(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-5.81378pt{a}\kern 3.85262pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-4.71303pt{a}\kern 2.75186pt}}},\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.22745pt{a}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.22745pt{a}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\leq^{{\kern-5.69846pt\kern 6.08734pt}}_{{\kern-8.04851pt{a}\kern 6.08734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\leq^{{\kern-4.60957pt\kern 4.99846pt}}_{{\kern-6.95963pt{a}\kern 4.99846pt}}})
of
P
exists, then
X_{p^{\prime}}\cap X_{q}=\emptyset
.

The condition (ii) is called the
Join Orthogonality Property (JOP)
of
(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-5.81378pt{a}\kern 3.85262pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-4.71303pt{a}\kern 2.75186pt}}},\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.22745pt{a}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.22745pt{a}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\leq^{{\kern-5.69846pt\kern 6.08734pt}}_{{\kern-8.04851pt{a}\kern 6.08734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\leq^{{\kern-4.60957pt\kern 4.99846pt}}_{{\kern-6.95963pt{a}\kern 4.99846pt}}})
. It implies that

(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-5.81378pt{a}\kern 3.85262pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-4.71303pt{a}\kern 2.75186pt}}},\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.22745pt{a}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.22745pt{a}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\leq^{{\kern-5.69846pt\kern 6.08734pt}}_{{\kern-8.04851pt{a}\kern 6.08734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\leq^{{\kern-4.60957pt\kern 4.99846pt}}_{{\kern-6.95963pt{a}\kern 4.99846pt}}})
embeds as a down-set in a complete ortholattice.

1.19.

Rootoidal signed groupoid sets have many favorable properties, but additional “discreteness” conditions
are required in order to restrict to more “Coxeter-group-like” signed groupoid sets.

For the following definitions, assume that
(G,X)
is a faithful signed groupoid-set. A morphism
g\in\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-5.81378pt{a}\kern 3.85262pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-4.71303pt{a}\kern 2.75186pt}}}
is called

simple
if

|X_{g}|=1
and
atomic
if
g
is an atom of the poset

(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-5.81378pt{a}\kern 3.85262pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-4.71303pt{a}\kern 2.75186pt}}},\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.22745pt{a}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.22745pt{a}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\leq^{{\kern-5.69846pt\kern 6.08734pt}}_{{\kern-8.04851pt{a}\kern 6.08734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\leq^{{\kern-4.60957pt\kern 4.99846pt}}_{{\kern-6.95963pt{a}\kern 4.99846pt}}})
. A simple morphism is clearly atomic.

For any set of generators
C
of
G
, the
length

\ell_{C}(g)
of
g
is defined to be the minimal length of an expression of
g
as a product of elements of
C
and their
inverses; by convention,
\ell_{C}(g)=0
if
g
is an identity morphism.

We say that
(G,X)
is
principal
if it is generated by its set
R

of simple morphisms, and for any morphism
g
of
G
,

\ell_{R}(g)=|X_{g}|
.

We say that
(G,X)
is
preprincipal
if it is generated by its set
A
of atomic morphisms and for any
a\in\mathrm{Ob}(G)
,

g\in\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-5.81378pt{a}\kern 3.85262pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-4.71303pt{a}\kern 2.75186pt}}}
and
s\in\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}A^{{\kern-5.62503pt\kern 6.01392pt}}_{{\kern-8.71512pt{a}\kern 6.01392pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}A^{{\kern-5.62503pt\kern 6.01392pt}}_{{\kern-8.71512pt{a}\kern 6.01392pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}A^{{\kern-3.28612pt\kern 3.675pt}}_{{\kern-5.63617pt{a}\kern 3.675pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}A^{{\kern-2.23611pt\kern 2.625pt}}_{{\kern-4.58617pt{a}\kern 2.625pt}}}:=\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-5.81378pt{a}\kern 3.85262pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-4.71303pt{a}\kern 2.75186pt}}}\cap A
, one has either
X_{s}\subseteq X_{g}
or
X_{s}\cap X_{g}=\emptyset
.

1.20.

See Section
3
for more details of the following definitions and constructions.

A root in
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}X^{{\kern-6.71323pt\kern 7.10211pt}}_{{\kern-9.80331pt{a}\kern 7.10211pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}X^{{\kern-6.71323pt\kern 7.10211pt}}_{{\kern-9.80331pt{a}\kern 7.10211pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}X^{{\kern-4.05513pt\kern 4.44402pt}}_{{\kern-6.40518pt{a}\kern 4.44402pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}X^{{\kern-2.78542pt\kern 3.1743pt}}_{{\kern-5.13547pt{a}\kern 3.1743pt}}}
is said to be
real
if its sign is changed by the action of
g^{-1}
for some
g\in\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-5.81378pt{a}\kern 3.85262pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-4.71303pt{a}\kern 2.75186pt}}}
,
and
imaginary
otherwise. These notions give rise to signed
G
-subsets

(G,X^{{\mathrm{re}}})
and
(G,X^{{\mathrm{im}}})
of
(G,X)
. Two roots
\alpha,\beta
based at the same object
a
of
G
are said to be

parallel
if
g^{-1}\alpha
and
g^{-1}\beta
have the same sign as one another, for all
g\in\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-5.81378pt{a}\kern 3.85262pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-4.71303pt{a}\kern 2.75186pt}}}
.

Some of the results involve two endofunctors of the category of signed
G
-sets,
realification
, denoted

(G,X)\to(G,X^{{\mathrm{re}}})
on objects and
\nu\mapsto\nu^{{\mathrm{re}}}
on morphisms, and
compression
, denoted

(G,X)\to(G,X^{{\mathrm{c}}})
on objects and
\nu\mapsto\nu^{{\mathrm{c}}}
on
morphisms.
By definition, the positive (resp., negative) roots of
X^{{\mathrm{re}}}

based at an object
a
of
G
are the positive (resp., negative) real roots of
X
which are based
at
a
. The positive (resp., negative) roots of
X^{{\mathrm{c}}}
based at an object
a
of
G
are the parallelism classes of positive
(resp., negative) roots of
X
which are based at
a
.
Realification and compression commute and their composite,
real compression
, is denoted by

(G,X)\mapsto(G,X^{{\mathrm{rec}}})
on objects and
\nu\mapsto\nu^{{\mathrm{rec}}}
on morphisms. We say
X
is
real
if

X^{{\mathrm{re}}}\cong X
,
compressed
if
X^{{\mathrm{c}}}\cong X
and
real compressed
if
X^{{\mathrm{rec}}}\cong X
.

Realification and compression don’t change the underlying
groupoid of a signed groupoid set
(G,X)
or its weak preorders,
so in particular, they preserve rootoidality.
If
(G,X)
is preprincipal, then
(G,X^{{\mathrm{rec}}})
is real, compressed, principal, and the atomic generators of
(G,X)

are the simple generators of
(G,X^{{\mathrm{rec}}})
. The class of real, compressed, principal, rootoidal signed groupoid sets

(G,X)
has many features in common with the class of signed groupoid sets
(W_{\bullet},\Phi_{\bullet})
associated to
Coxeter systems
(W,S)
(and also analogues for Brink-Howlett groupoids and Coxeter and Weyl groupoids). The class
of preprincipal, rootoidal signed groupoid sets is an enlargement of that class which admits abstract analogues of
non-reduced root systems of finite Weyl groups and root systems (possibly with imaginary roots) of Coxeter groups of
Kac-Moody Lie algebras.

1.21.
Standard abstract root systems of Brink-Howlett groupoids

Let
G
denote a Brink-Howlett groupoid of
(W,S)

and
(W_{\bullet},\Phi_{\bullet})
denote the signed groupoid-set from Example
1.16
.
There is a functor

F\colon G\to W_{\bullet}
such that
F(J)=\bullet
for all
J\in\mathrm{Ob}(G)
and
F(J\xrightarrow{w}K)=w
. Composing the functor

\Phi_{\bullet}
with
F
gives a signed groupoid-set

(G,\Lambda),\qquad\Lambda:=\Phi_{\bullet}\circ F.

The following is a main result of this paper, proved in Section
3
. It is a consequence of more general facts, some discussed in
[
15
]
–
[
16
]
, but we provide a direct proof. Note that part (ii) follows from (i), by the discussion in
1.20
.

Theorem 1.22
.

Define the signed groupoid-set
(G,\Lambda)
as in
1.21
.

(a)

(G,\Lambda)
is a preprincipal, rootoidal signed groupoid-set with the set of Brink-Howlett generators of
G
as its set of atomic morphisms.

(b)

(G,\Lambda^{{\mathrm{rec}}})
is a principal rootoidal signed groupoid-set with the set of Brink-Howlett generators of
G
as its set of simple morphisms.

We call
(G,\Lambda^{{\mathrm{rec}}})
the standard signed groupoid-set (attached to
G
,
(W,S)
and
(\Phi,\Pi)
).

Example 1.23
.

Consider
(G,\Lambda)
as in
1.21
.
Consider an object
J\subseteq\Pi
of
G
. We have
\Lambda(J)^{+}=\Phi^{+}
and
\Phi_{J}^{+}\subseteq\Lambda(J)^{{\mathrm{im}},+}
since for any morphism
(K,w,J)
in
G
, one has
w(J)=K
by definition and therefore
w(\Phi_{J}^{+})=\Phi_{K}^{+}\subseteq\Phi^{+}=\Lambda(K)^{+}
. Suppose now that
W
is finite.
There is a morphism
(K,w_{S}w_{J},J)
in
G

where
K:=-w_{S}J\subseteq\Pi
. One has
w_{S}w_{J}(\Phi^{+}\setminus\Phi_{J}^{+})=\Phi^{-}\setminus\Phi_{K}^{-}
, which implies that
\Phi^{+}\setminus\Phi_{J}^{+}\subseteq\Lambda(J)^{\mathrm{re},+}
. This forces

\Lambda(J)^{{\mathrm{im}},+}=\Phi_{J}^{+}
and
\Lambda(J)^{\mathrm{re},+}=\Phi^{+}\setminus\Phi^{+}_{J}
.

The corresponding equalities need not hold for infinite
W
.
For example, one readily checks that if the Coxeter system
(W,S)
is universal
(that is, for any distinct
r,s\in S
, the product
rs
has infinite order), then for any non-empty subset
J
of
S
, one has

\Lambda(J)^{\mathrm{im},+}=\Phi^{+}
and
\Lambda(J)^{\mathrm{re},+}={\emptyset}
.

1.24.
Brink-Howlett generators

We shall construct in this paper other signed
G
-sets with real compression isomorphic to
(G,\Lambda^{{\mathrm{rec}}})
as in Theorem
1.22
. To begin, we recall the known explicit description of the Brink-Howlett generators, and related elements, of Brink-Howlett groupoids.

Let
(m_{r,s})_{r,s\in S}
denote the Coxeter matrix of
(W,S)
. By definition,
m_{r,s}:=\mathrm{ord}(rs)\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}\cup\{\infty\}
. Recall that the Coxeter graph is the undirected, edge-labelled (simple) graph with vertex set
S
and an edge joining
r,s\in S
if
m_{r,s}\geq 3
, that edge being labeled by
m_{r,s}
if
m_{r,s}\geq 4
.

Using the natural bijection
\alpha\mapsto s_{\alpha}\colon\Pi\to S
, the Coxeter graph of
(W,S)
will often be regarded
in this paper as an edge-labeled graph with vertex set
\Pi
. By a
component
of
\Pi
, we mean a connected
component of this graph, or sometimes just the vertex set
J
of that component. We may then refer to
\{\,s_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in J\,\}
as a component of
S
,
W_{J}
as a component
of
W
,

\Phi_{J}
as a component of
\Phi
,
\Phi_{J}^{+}
as a component of
\Phi^{+}
, etc. Similarly, we may refer to
components of other Coxeter groups, their simple reflections,
root systems, simple roots, and positive systems provided the sets of simple reflections and simple roots are clear from
context (as they are for any reflection subgroup of
(W,S)
, by
conventions in
1.28
). We refer to a component in any of these senses as being of finite, affine, indefinite, locally finite,
infinite type etc according to the type of
J
(see
[
32
]
and
[
25
]
).

The following facts, and the closely related Proposition
1.35
, are essentially due to Howlett and Deodhar.
See
[
29
]
,
[
12
]
,
[
9
]
, and also
[
25
]
for
W
of possibly infinite rank.

Lemma 1.25
.

Let
J\subseteq K\subseteq\Pi
. Then
|(W_{K}\setminus W_{J})\cap T|
is finite if and only if there are
finitely many components of
K
which meet
K\setminus J
, and they are all of finite type.
Assume that this is the case, and let
L\supseteq K\setminus J
be
the union of all such components, which is a spherical subset of
\Pi
. Let
w=\nu(K\setminus J,J):=w_{L}w_{L\cap J}\in W
.
Then:

(a)

\Phi^{+}_{K}\setminus\Phi^{+}_{J}=\Phi^{+}_{L}\setminus\Phi^{+}_{L\cap J}=\Phi_{w^{-1}}
.

(b)

(W_{K}\setminus W_{J})\cap T=(W_{L}\setminus W_{L\cap J})\cap T=N(w^{-1})
.

(c)

w{J}=(J\setminus L)\cup w(J\cap L)=(J\setminus L)\cup-w_{L}(J\cap L)\subseteq K
.

(d)

Writing
w{J}={J^{\prime}}\subseteq K
the element
\nu(K\setminus J^{\prime},J^{\prime})
of
W
is defined similarly as
\nu(K\setminus J,J)
, and it satisfies

\nu(K\setminus J,J)^{-1}=\nu(K\setminus J^{\prime},J^{\prime})
in
W
.

(e)

\nu(K\setminus J,J)=\nu(L\setminus J,L\cap J)
.

Remark 1.26
.

Whenever we use notation of the form
\nu(M,L)
in this paper, where
M
and
L
are subsets of the set of simple roots of a Coxeter system
(W,S)
, it is tacitly
assumed that
M\cap L={\emptyset}
and
|(W_{M\cup L}\setminus W_{L})\cap T|
is finite, so
\nu(M,L)
is defined by the preceding lemma.

1.27.

The triples
(J^{\prime},w,J)
, with
w=\nu(K\setminus J,J)
, defined as in Lemma
1.25
, for which
J
(or equivalently,
J^{\prime}
) are objects of a fixed
Brink-Howlett groupoid
G

are morphisms of
G
. Those for which, additionally,
K\setminus J
(or equivalently,
K\setminus J^{\prime}
) is a singleton set form a set of generators for
G
; we call them the

Brink-Howlett generators
of
G
. See
[
12
]
,
[
9
]
and Proposition
3.13
.
We frequently conflate a singleton set
\{x\}
with
x
when convenient for notational compactness, so if

K\setminus J=\{\alpha\}
, may write
\nu(\alpha,J):=\nu(\{\alpha\},J)
.

In fact,
G
is generated by its Brink-Howlett generators
[
12
]
, subject to relations of the following two types,
called
rank one relations
and
rank two relations
in
[
9
]
.

For any Brink-Howlett generator
(J^{\prime},\nu(K\setminus J,J),J)
of
G
as in Lemma
1.25
(c), there is a rank one relation
(J^{\prime},\nu(K\setminus J,J),J)^{-1}=(J,\nu(K\setminus J^{\prime},J^{\prime}),J^{\prime})
.

There is a rank two relation for each
morphism
(J^{\prime},w,J)
of
G
, with
w=\nu(K\setminus J,J)
where
|K\setminus J|=2
(or equivalently,
|K\setminus J^{\prime}|=2
). Such a morphism has exactly two expressions as a minimal
length product of Brink-Howlett generators, one with
\nu(\alpha,K)
as rightmost factor and the other with
\nu(\beta,K)
as rightmost factor, where
K\setminus J=\{\alpha,\beta\}
; the corresponding relation expresses the equality of those two expressions. See
[
9
]
.

1.28.
Maximal corank
k
overgroups of reflection subgroups

Roots of the signed groupoid-set in
Theorem
1.22
(b) will be seen to correspond to certain reflection
subgroups of
W
. We describe below salient properties of reflection subgroups and their root subsystems.

A
reflection subgroup
of
W
is by definition a subgroup

U=\langle\,U\cap T\,\rangle
of
W
which is generated by the
reflections it contains. Let
U
be a reflection subgroup of
W
. Then
U

has a set of
canonical Coxeter generators
(depending on
(W,S)
)

(1.28.1)

\chi(U)=\chi_{(W,S)}(U):=\{\,t\in T\mid N(t)\cap u=\{t\}\,\},

for which the set of
reflections in
U
is
U\cap T
. Further,
(U,\chi(U))
has a based root system
1
1

1

The technical utility of such functoriality properties of based root systems is one reason for using them as the framework for this paper rather than just considering standard based root systems. If
(\Phi,\Pi)
is a standard based root system,
(\Phi_{U},\Pi_{U})
is not in general standard.

(\Phi_{U},\Pi_{U})
with positive roots
\Phi_{U}^{+}
in
(V,B)
where
\Phi_{U}=\{\,\alpha\in\Phi\mid s_{\alpha}\in U\,\}
,

\Phi^{+}_{U}:=\Phi_{U}\cap\Phi^{+}
and
\Pi_{U}:=\{\,\alpha\in\Phi^{+}\mid s_{\alpha}\in\chi(U)\,\}
. In particular, if
U=W_{J}
is the standard parabolic subgroup generated by reflections in a subset
J
of simple roots, then
\Pi_{U}=J
.

Define the
corank

\corank_{W}(U)
of
U
in
(W,S)
to be the minimum
cardinality of a subset
T^{\prime}\subseteq W\cap T
such that

W=\langle\,(U\cap T)\cup T^{\prime}\,\rangle
. For
J\subseteq\Pi
, one has in particular
\Pi_{W_{J}}=J
and
\corank_{W}(wW_{J}w^{-1})=|\Pi\setminus J|
for any
w\in W
. See Proposition
2.11
.

We extend the definition of notions defined for Coxeter systems and their based root systems to arbitrary reflection subgroups
U
of
(W,S)
by regarding
U
as the Coxeter group underlying the Coxeter system
(U,\chi(U))
, for which one
uses the based root system
(\Phi_{U},\Pi_{U})
. In particular, this defines
\corank_{U}(U^{\prime})
for any reflection subgroups
U^{\prime}
and
U
of
W
with
U^{\prime}\subseteq U
; this corank is the minimum of the cardinalities of subsets
T^{\prime}
of
U\cap T
such that
U=\langle\,(U^{\prime}\cap T)\cup T^{\prime}\,\rangle
.

1.29.

For any subgroup
G
of
W
, we say that a subgroup
H
of
W
with
H{\,\supseteq\,}G
is an
overgroup
of
G
(in
W
). We say that
H
is an overgroup of
G
of
index
2
2

2

The term co-index may be preferred for consistency with corank.

[H:G]
(the index of
G
in
H
). If
H
and
G
are reflection subgroups of
W
, we say that
H
is a corank
n
reflection overgroup of
G
where
n:=\corank_{H}(G)
.

If
U
is a reflection subgroup of
W
and
W_{J}\subseteq U
, then

J\subseteq\Pi_{U}
; in that case,
\corank_{U}(W_{J})=|\Pi_{U}\setminus J|
. If the corank is equal to
k
, we say, according to the above, that
U
is a
corank
k
reflection overgroup
of
W_{J}
(in
W
).
For
k\in\mathbb{N}
, a
maximal corank
k
reflection overgroup
of
W_{J}
is by definition an inclusion maximal element of the inclusion-ordered poset of corank
k
reflection overgroups of
W_{J}
.

The following refinement of results in
[
17
]
(which concerned ranks of reflection subgroups) is important in this paper. It is proved and slightly refined in
2.32
–
2.42
.

Theorem 1.30
.

Let
J\subseteq\Pi
and
U
be a reflection overgroup of
W_{J}
. Assume that
\corank_{U}(W_{J})=k\in\mathbb{N}
.

(a)

If
U
is a parabolic subgroup of
W
, then
U
is a maximal corank
k
reflection overgroup of
W_{J}
.

(b)

Assume that
\Pi_{U}
is linearly independent.
Then the set of all corank
k
reflection overgroups of
W_{J}
which contain
U
has a maximum element. In particular,
U
is a reflection subgroup of a unique maximal corank
k
reflection overgroup of
W_{J}
.

(c)

Assume that
\Pi_{U}
is linearly independent
and that
\Phi_{U}=\Phi\cap\operatorname{span}(\Phi_{U})
.
Then
U
is a maximal corank
k
reflection overgroup of
W_{J}
. Let
I
be the set of all maximal, corank
k+1
reflection overgroups of
W_{J}
which contain
U
.
Then
\Phi\setminus\Phi_{U}=\dot{\bigcup}_{W^{\prime}\in I}(\Phi_{W^{\prime}}\setminus\Phi_{U})
.

1.31.
Roots and reflection subgroups

Note that reflections of
(W,S)
correspond bijectively to rank one reflection overgroups in
W
of the trivial group.
Theorem
1.33
below generalizes to a Brink-Howlett groupoid
G
the description in
2.5
of the abstract root system of
(W,S)
in terms of reflections, and is a main result of this work. Sets of corank one reflection overgroups of the standard parabolic subgroup
W_{J}
corresponding to each object
J
of
G
play an analogous role for
G
as the set of reflections plays for
(W,S)
. We first define relevant sets of reflection subgroups. See Example
4.1
, and Propositions
4.2
and
4.3
for some basic facts concerning them.

1.32.

For
J\subseteq\Pi
, define
R_{J}
to be the set of all corank one reflection overgroups of
W_{J}
. Let
N_{J}
denote the set of all
U\in R_{J}
such that
(U\setminus W_{J})\cap T
is finite.
Let
M_{J}
denote the set of all maximal corank one
reflection overgroups of
W_{J}
. Let
P_{J}
denote
the set of
all corank one reflection overgroups of
W_{J}
which are parabolic subgroups of
W
. Let
Q_{J}=P_{J}\cap N_{J}
.

For any
U\in R_{J}
, we have
J\subseteq\Pi_{U}
and
|\Pi_{U}\setminus J|=1
, so we may write

(1.32.1)

\Pi_{U}=J\dot{\cup}\{r_{U,J}\},\qquad r_{U,J}\in\Phi^{+}.

The following Theorem
1.33
, which is our main result on abstract root systems of Brink Howlett groupoids, is proved in Section
4
using
Lemma
1.25
, Theorem
1.30
, Proposition
1.35
and Theorem
1.43
.

Theorem 1.33
.

Let
G
be a Brink-Howlett groupoid
for
(W,S)
. Let
X
denote either
R
,
M
,
N
,
P
or
Q
.
Then there is a signed groupoid-set
(G,\Upsilon_{X})
as
follows.
For an object
J\subseteq\Pi
of
G
,

\Upsilon_{X}(J):=X_{J}\times\{\pm\}
where the signed set

X_{J}\times\{\pm\}
has
(X_{J}\times\{\pm\})^{+}=X_{J}\times\{+\}
with

\{\pm\}
-action
\epsilon(U,\eta)=(U,\epsilon\eta)
for
U\in X_{J}
and
\epsilon,\eta\in\{\pm\}
. For each morphism
(K,w,J)
of
G
, the map

\Upsilon_{X}(K,w,J)\colon X_{J}\times\{\pm\}\to X_{K}\times\{\pm\}
is

(U,\epsilon)\mapsto(wUw^{-1},(-1)^{\eta(w,U,J)}\epsilon),\qquad U\in X_{J},\quad\epsilon\in\{\pm\}

where
\eta(w,U,J)\in\mathbb{Z}
is equal to
1
if
(U\setminus W_{J})\cap T\subseteq N(w^{-1})
and to
0
otherwise (in which case
(U\setminus W_{J})\cap N(w^{-1})={\emptyset}
). Further:

(a)

Let
(U,\epsilon)\in{\Upsilon}_{X}(J)
. Then
(U,\epsilon)\in({\Upsilon}_{X}(J))^{\mathrm{re}}
if and only if
|\Phi^{+}_{U}\setminus\Phi_{J}^{+}|<\infty
if and only if
|(U\setminus W_{J})\cap T|<\infty
.

(b)

(G,\Upsilon_{Q})\subseteq(G,\Upsilon_{P})\subseteq(G,\Upsilon_{M})\subseteq(G,\Upsilon_{R})
and
(G,\Upsilon_{Q})\subseteq(G,\Upsilon_{N})\subseteq(G,\Upsilon_{R})
.

(c)

(G,\Upsilon_{R}^{{\mathrm{re}}})=(G,\Upsilon_{N})
and
(G,\Upsilon_{M}^{{\mathrm{re}}})=(G,\Upsilon_{P}^{{\mathrm{re}}})=(G,\Upsilon_{Q})
.

(d)

There are isomorphisms

(G,\Lambda^{{\mathrm{rec}}})\cong(G,\Upsilon_{X}^{{\mathrm{rec}}})\cong(G,\Upsilon_{Q})
.

1.34.

For notational compactness, we may write
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Upsilon^{{\kern-5.75003pt\kern 6.13892pt}}_{{\kern-9.43843pt{J}\kern 6.13892pt{X}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Upsilon^{{\kern-5.75003pt\kern 6.13892pt}}_{{\kern-9.43843pt{J}\kern 6.13892pt{X}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Upsilon^{{\kern-3.42223pt\kern 3.81111pt}}_{{\kern-6.19965pt{J}\kern 3.81111pt{X}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Upsilon^{{\kern-2.33334pt\kern 2.72223pt}}_{{\kern-5.11076pt{J}\kern 2.72223pt{X}}}}:=\Upsilon_{X}(J)
, and sometimes omit
X
if
X=R
, writing for instance
{\Upsilon}:={\Upsilon}_{R}
and

{\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Upsilon^{{\kern-5.75003pt\kern 6.13892pt}}_{{\kern-9.43843pt{J}\kern 6.13892pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Upsilon^{{\kern-5.75003pt\kern 6.13892pt}}_{{\kern-9.43843pt{J}\kern 6.13892pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Upsilon^{{\kern-3.42223pt\kern 3.81111pt}}_{{\kern-6.19965pt{J}\kern 3.81111pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Upsilon^{{\kern-2.33334pt\kern 2.72223pt}}_{{\kern-5.11076pt{J}\kern 2.72223pt}}}}:=\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Upsilon^{{\kern-5.75003pt\kern 6.13892pt}}_{{\kern-9.43843pt{J}\kern 6.13892pt{R}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Upsilon^{{\kern-5.75003pt\kern 6.13892pt}}_{{\kern-9.43843pt{J}\kern 6.13892pt{R}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Upsilon^{{\kern-3.42223pt\kern 3.81111pt}}_{{\kern-6.19965pt{J}\kern 3.81111pt{R}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Upsilon^{{\kern-2.33334pt\kern 2.72223pt}}_{{\kern-5.11076pt{J}\kern 2.72223pt{R}}}}
.

We next discuss additional facts which are of interest in relation Theorem
1.33
or are involved in its proof.
The first of these, as already mentioned, is essentially due to Howlett
[
29
]
and Deodhar
[
12
]
. It provides several equivalent descriptions of real roots in the root systems in Theorem
1.33
.

Proposition 1.35
.

Let
J\subseteq\Pi
, and
U
be a reflection overgroup of
W_{J}
, so
J\subseteq\Pi_{U}
. Then the following conditions are equivalent:

(i)

(U\setminus W_{J})\cap T
is finite

(ii)

\Phi_{U}\setminus\Phi_{J}
is finite.

(iii)

\corank_{U}(W_{J})=|\Pi_{U}\setminus J|
is finite and for each
\alpha\in\Pi_{U}\setminus J
, the component of
\Pi_{U}

containing
\alpha
is of finite type.

(iv)

the index
[U:W_{J}]
is finite.

The next result follows immediately from Theorem
1.33
and Proposition
1.35
.

Corollary 1.36
.

The set
\Lambda^{{\mathrm{rec}}}(J)^{+}
of positive real roots based at an object
J
of the standard signed groupoid-set
(G,\Lambda^{{\mathrm{rec}}})
of a Brink-Howlett groupoid
G
is in natural bijective correspondence with the set of finite index, corank one, parabolic overgroups of
W_{J}
in
W
.

1.37.

When the conditions in Proposition

1.35
hold, the element
\nu(\Pi_{U}\setminus J,J)\in U
is
defined in the Coxeter system
(U,\chi(U))
. Natural examples
of situations to which that proposition applies, with
U

standard parabolic in
W
, are given by the next proposition,
which is proved as Proposition

2.29
.

Proposition 1.38
.

Let
\alpha\in\Phi^{+}
. Fix
K\subseteq\Pi
with
B(\alpha,\beta)\geq 0
for all
\beta\in K
and let
J:=\{\,\beta\in K\mid B(\alpha,\beta)=0\,\}
. Then
[W_{K}:W_{J}]<\infty
.

1.39.
Special standard parabolic root subsystems

In the following, we regard
\Pi
as the vertex set of the Coxeter graph of
(W,S)
. By a component of a subset
J
of
\Pi
, we mean the vertex set of a component of the full subgraph the Coxeter graph on vertex set
J
. Let
J_{\mathrm{fin}}
be the union of all finite type components of
J
and
J_{\mathrm{inf}}
be the union of all other components (i.e. all finite components which are of affine or indefinite type, and all components which are infinite as sets) of
J
. Thus,
J=J_{\mathrm{fin}}\,\dot{\cup}\,J_{\mathrm{inf}}
.

In order to study a connected Brink-Howlett groupoid of
(W,S)
, Brink and Howlett replaced the groupoid by an isomorphic Brink-Howlett groupoid of a suitable standard parabolic subgroup
W^{\prime}
of
W
, to reduce to the case in which each object
J
satisfies
J_{\inf}={\emptyset}
(that is,
J=J_{\mathrm{fin}}
), and so that no root in
\Pi\setminus J
is joined in the Coxeter graph of
(W,S)
to infinitely many elements of
J
. To obtain particularly well-behaved realizations in real vector spaces of root systems of Brink-Howlett groupoids, it is necessary to refine this reduction by restricting the
W^{\prime}
action from
V
to a suitable subspace of
V
which contains
\Phi_{W^{\prime}}
.
This reduction and its effect on real root systems is described in Propositions
4.7
and
4.8
. The proofs of those results uses Proposition
1.41
, which is deduced from Proposition
1.38
.

1.40.

A
standard parabolic
root
subsystem of
\Phi
is by definition the root system
\Phi_{U}
of a standard
parabolic subgroup
U=W_{J}
, for some
J\subseteq\Pi
.

Proposition 1.41
.

Let
L\subseteq\Pi
.

(a)

If
L=L_{\mathrm{inf}}
, then
\Phi\cap L^{\perp}
is a standard parabolic root subsystem of
\Phi
.

(b)

\{\,\beta\in\Phi\mid\text{\rm$B(\beta,\alpha)\neq 0$ for only finitely many $\alpha\in L$}\,\}
is a standard parabolic root subsystem of
\Phi
.

1.42.
Relative Tits theorem

The following theorem, proved in Section
2
, is another of our main results. Its special case in which
J={\emptyset}
implies the theorem of Tits (
[
5
]
) that any finite subgroup of
W
is contained in a finite parabolic subgroup. The special case when the corank is one in the final statement of the theorem is particularly important for our applications.

Theorem 1.43
.

Let
J\subseteq\Pi
and
W^{\prime}
be an overgroup in
W
of
W_{J}
. Then the following conditions are equivalent:

(i)

The index
[W^{\prime}:W_{J}]
of
W_{J}
in
W^{\prime}
is finite.

(ii)

There is a parabolic subgroup
U{\,\supseteq\,}W^{\prime}
of
W
such that
[U:W_{J}]
is finite.

(iii)

There exist
w\in W
and
K\subseteq\Pi
so

L:=wJ\subseteq K
,
[W_{K}:W_{L}]
is finite and

W_{L}=wW_{J}w^{-1}\subseteq wW^{\prime}w^{-1}\subseteq W_{K}
.

If (i)–(iii) hold, then there is a unique inclusion-minimal element
U_{0}
of the set of parabolic subgroups
U
which satisfy (ii).
If, further,
W^{\prime}
is a reflection subgroup,
then
\corank_{U_{0}}(W_{J})=\corank_{W^{\prime}}(W_{J})
.

1.44.
Realized root systems

By a
realization
of a signed groupoid-set
(G,\Lambda)
, we mean a pair
({\mathcal{V}},\iota)
consisting of a representation
{\mathcal{V}}
of
G
in the category of real vector spaces together with a natural transformation
\iota\colon\Lambda\to{\mathcal{V}}
(from the functor
G\to\mathbf{Set}

underlying
\Lambda
to that underlying
{\mathcal{V}}
) such that the components
\iota_{a}\colon\Lambda(a)\to{\mathcal{V}}(a)
of
\iota
are injective
\{\pm\}
-equivariant maps, for all
a\in\operatorname{ob}(G)
.

The realizations of
(G,\Lambda)
correspond naturally to families of realizations of its components. We shall often write the real vector space
{\mathcal{V}}(a)
as
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}{\mathcal{V}}^{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-8.60402pt{a}\kern 5.90282pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}{\mathcal{V}}^{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-8.60402pt{a}\kern 5.90282pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}{\mathcal{V}}^{{\kern-3.28612pt\kern 3.675pt}}_{{\kern-5.63617pt{a}\kern 3.675pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}{\mathcal{V}}^{{\kern-2.23611pt\kern 2.625pt}}_{{\kern-4.58617pt{a}\kern 2.625pt}}}
.

To emphasize the distinction between them, we call
(G,\Lambda)
an
abstract
signed groupoid-set, and
(G,\Lambda,{\mathcal{V}},\iota)
a
realized
signed groupoid-set.
More informally, we call
\Lambda
an
abstract root system
of
G
and
(\Lambda,{\mathcal{V}},\iota)
a
realized root system
of
G
. This extends the terminology for root systems of Coxeter groups.

Example 1.45
.

Consider the signed
W_{\bullet}
-set
(W_{\bullet},\Phi_{\bullet})
in Example
1.16
.
There is a representation
{\mathcal{V}}_{\bullet}
of
W_{\bullet}
in the category of real vector spaces,
sending
\bullet
to be ambient real vector space
V
of
\Phi
, and each morphism
w
of
W_{\bullet}
to the linear map
V\to V
induced by the action of
w
.
There is a realization
({\mathcal{V}}_{\bullet},\iota)
of
(W_{\bullet},\Phi_{\bullet})
where
\iota\colon\Phi_{\bullet}\to{\mathcal{V}}_{\bullet}
is the natural transformation whose unique component
\iota_{\bullet}\colon\Phi\to V
is the inclusion map.

1.46.
Weak realizations

Define the notion of a
weak realization

({\mathcal{V}},\iota)
of a signed
G
-set
(G,\Lambda)
in the same way as a realization, except omitting the requirement that the components
\iota_{a}
be injective maps. Several natural constructions yield in general only weak realizations of signed groupoid-sets, and one could extend the framework of this paper to deal with such situations more effectively by incorporating structure of “root multiplicity” into parts of the development
(see
1.52
).

Consider a weak realization
({\mathcal{V}},\iota)
of
(G,\Lambda)
. Assume that for all
a\in\operatorname{ob}(G)
, one has

(1.46.1)

\iota_{a}(\alpha)\neq-\iota_{a}(\beta),\qquad\alpha,\beta\in\Lambda(a)^{+}.

Define the set
\widetilde{\Lambda}(a):=\iota_{a}(\Lambda(a))\subseteq{\mathcal{V}}(a)
. This becomes a signed set with
\{\pm\}
action
induced by that on
V
, and
\widetilde{\Lambda}(a)^{+}:=\iota_{a}(\Lambda(a)^{+})
as the set of positive elements.
The map
i_{a}\colon\Lambda(a)\to\widetilde{\Lambda}(a)
given by
restriction of
\iota_{a}
is a morphism of signed sets.
Let
j_{a}\colon\widetilde{\Lambda}(a)\to{\mathcal{V}}(a)
denote the inclusion map, which is
\{\pm\}
-equivariant.

For a morphism
g\colon a\to b
in
G
, define

\widetilde{\Lambda}(g)\colon\widetilde{\Lambda}(a)\to\widetilde{\Lambda}(b)
to be
the map obtained by restriction of
{\mathcal{V}}(g)\colon{\mathcal{V}}(a)\to{\mathcal{V}}(b)
. Then
\widetilde{\Lambda}(g)
is also a morphism of signed
sets. This defines a functor
\widetilde{\Lambda}\colon G\to{\mathbf{Set}}_{\pm}
, making
(G,\widetilde{\Lambda})
a signed
G
-set.
There is a natural transformation
i\colon\Lambda\to\widetilde{\Lambda}
with component
i_{a}
at
a
for each
a\in\operatorname{ob}(G)
. It gives a morphism
i\colon(G,\Lambda)\to(G,\widetilde{\Lambda})

of signed
G
-sets, which is an isomorphism if
({\mathcal{V}},\iota)
is
a realization.

There is a natural transformation
j\colon\widetilde{\Lambda}\to{\mathcal{V}}
(of set-valued functors) with component
j_{a}\colon\widetilde{\Lambda}(a)\to{\mathcal{V}}(a)
at
a\in\operatorname{ob}(G)
. It is easily seen that
(G,\widetilde{\Lambda},{\mathcal{V}},j)
is a realized signed groupoid set which we call
the
associated realized signed
G
-set
of the weakly realized
G
-set
(G,\Lambda,{\mathcal{V}},\iota)
.

Remark
.

The examples of (weak) realizations of signed groupoid-sets in which we are most interested satisfy convexity properties (to be studied in another work) which imply the condition (
1.46.1
). We do not impose corresponding conditions in the definition here, but simply observe that they hold in the examples considered.

1.47.
Realized root systems of Brink-Howlett groupoids

We now describe construction of (weak) realizations of abstract root systems of Brink-Howlett groupoids considered in Theorem
1.33
. The constructed realizations will generally depend, even up to the natural notion of isomorphism, not just on
(W,S)
but on the choice of a realized root system
\Phi
of
(W,S)
. We fix such a choice.

Recall that for
J\subseteq\Pi
and any corank one reflection overgroup
U\in R_{J}
of
W_{J}
, we define
r_{U,J}\in\Phi^{+}\setminus J
by

(1.47.1)

\Pi_{U}=J\dot{\cup}\{r_{U,J}\}.

Our approach to constructing realizations of signed groupoid sets
(G,\Upsilon_{X})
as in Theorem
1.33
is to represent an abstract root
(U,\epsilon)\in\Upsilon_{X}(J)
, where
J\in\operatorname{ob}(G)
, by
\epsilon r_{U,J}\in V
. This element of
V
, together with
J
, determines
(U,\epsilon)
uniquely by (
1.47.1
). However, Proposition
4.3
implies that this does not in general give a realization unless one replaces
V
by a suitable quotient vector space (depending on
J
in a manner compatible with the
G
-action)
and replaces
\epsilon r_{U,J}
by its image in the quotient.
It is then possible that the images in the quotient space of
r_{U,J}
and
r_{U^{\prime},J}
, where
U,U^{\prime}\in X_{J}
, could coincide even if
U\neq U^{\prime}
. In general, one only obtains weak realizations in this way (see Example
6.12
),
but surprisingly (see Remark
1.51
(2)), these induce a realization of the realification
(G,\Upsilon_{X}^{{\mathrm{re}}})
.

We introduce notation for Theorem
1.49
, which records basic properties of these weak realizations.

1.48.

Fix notation as in Theorem
1.33
, so

X
below denotes
R
,
M
,
P
,
N
or
Q
.

Let
J
be an object of
G
. Define the real vector space

{\mathcal{V}}(J):=V/\operatorname{span}(J_{\mathrm{fin}})
. Let
\pi_{J}\colon V\to V/\operatorname{span}(J_{\mathrm{fin}})
denote the canonical epimorphism
v\mapsto v+\operatorname{span}(J_{\mathrm{fin}})
. Define
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-3.69446pt\kern 4.08334pt}}_{{\kern-6.47188pt{J}\kern 4.08334pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-2.52779pt\kern 2.91667pt}}_{{\kern-5.3052pt{J}\kern 2.91667pt}}}:=\pi_{J}(\Pi\setminus J_{\mathrm{fin}})

and a map
\iota_{J}\colon{\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Upsilon^{{\kern-5.75003pt\kern 6.13892pt}}_{{\kern-9.43843pt{J}\kern 6.13892pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Upsilon^{{\kern-5.75003pt\kern 6.13892pt}}_{{\kern-9.43843pt{J}\kern 6.13892pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Upsilon^{{\kern-3.42223pt\kern 3.81111pt}}_{{\kern-6.19965pt{J}\kern 3.81111pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Upsilon^{{\kern-2.33334pt\kern 2.72223pt}}_{{\kern-5.11076pt{J}\kern 2.72223pt}}}}\to{\mathcal{V}}(J)
by
\iota_{J}(U,\epsilon)=\epsilon\pi_{J}(r_{U,J})
.

A morphism
(K,w,J)
of
G
satisfies

w(J)=K
and therefore
w(J_{\mathrm{fin}})=K_{\mathrm{fin}}
.
Let
{\mathcal{V}}(K,w,J)\colon{\mathcal{V}}(J)\to{\mathcal{V}}(K)
denote the linear map given by

\pi_{J}(v)\mapsto\pi_{K}(wv)
for all
v\in V
. This define a functor
{\mathcal{V}}
from
G
to the category of real vector spaces. There is a natural transformation

\iota\colon\Upsilon_{X}\to{\mathcal{V}}
(of the underlying functors functors from
G
to the category of sets) which has component
\iota_{J}
at
J\in\operatorname{ob}(G)
.

We maintain the above notation and assumptions till further notice. The following Theorem
1.49
is another main result of this paper, proved in Section
5
.

Theorem 1.49
.

(a)

F:=(G,\Upsilon_{X},{\mathcal{V}},\iota)
is a weakly realized signed groupoid-set.

(b)

F
is a realized signed groupoid-set if
\Pi
is linearly independent and
X=P
.

(c)

If
|J|=1
for all
J\in\operatorname{ob}(G)
, then
F
is a realized signed groupoid-set.

(d)

If
X
denotes
N
or
Q
, then
F
is a realized signed groupoid-set.

Remarks
.

(1) The proof of part (d) depends on an extension

[
18
]
to finite Coxeter groups of a
lemma due to Oshima
[
35
]
for finite Weyl groups.

(2) Theorem
1.49
(c)–(d) do not hold in general for
non-simply-laced finite Weyl groups
W
(even, say, of type

B_{2}
) if one uses the standard crystallographic root system
of
W
instead of the based root system as in this paper (with
all roots of unit square length); see Remark
2.44
.

(3) Theorem
1.49
(b) is false in general without the assumption there that
\Pi
is linearly independent (see Example
6.12
).

(4) Computations based on Theorem
1.49
(c), in the special case in which
(W,S)
is of rank three, were an important ingredient in the (unpublished) first proof of the main result of

[
17
]
.

Proposition 1.50
.

Let
J\in\operatorname{ob}(G)
.

(a)

For each
(U,\epsilon)\in\Upsilon_{X}(J)
, we have
\iota_{J}(U,\epsilon)=\epsilon\pi_{J}(r_{U,J})\neq 0
.

(b)

\pi_{J}(\operatorname{cone}(\Pi))=\pi_{J}(\operatorname{cone}(\Pi\setminus J_{\mathrm{fin}}))=\operatorname{cone}(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-3.69446pt\kern 4.08334pt}}_{{\kern-6.47188pt{J}\kern 4.08334pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-2.52779pt\kern 2.91667pt}}_{{\kern-5.3052pt{J}\kern 2.91667pt}}})
.

(c)

\iota_{J}(\mathchoice{\Upsilon^{{{+}}}_{{{X}}}}{\Upsilon^{{{+}}}_{{{X}}}}{\Upsilon^{{{+}}}_{{{X}}}}{\Upsilon^{{{+}}}_{{{X}}}}(J))\subseteq\operatorname{cone}(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-3.69446pt\kern 4.08334pt}}_{{\kern-6.47188pt{J}\kern 4.08334pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-2.52779pt\kern 2.91667pt}}_{{\kern-5.3052pt{J}\kern 2.91667pt}}})
.

(d)

\operatorname{cone}(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-3.69446pt\kern 4.08334pt}}_{{\kern-6.47188pt{J}\kern 4.08334pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-2.52779pt\kern 2.91667pt}}_{{\kern-5.3052pt{J}\kern 2.91667pt}}})\cap-\operatorname{cone}(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-3.69446pt\kern 4.08334pt}}_{{\kern-6.47188pt{J}\kern 4.08334pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-2.52779pt\kern 2.91667pt}}_{{\kern-5.3052pt{J}\kern 2.91667pt}}})=\{0\}
.

(e)

Suppose that
\alpha\in\Pi\setminus J
and
W_{J\cup\{\alpha\}}\in Q_{J}
. Then
\pi_{J}(\alpha)\in\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-3.69446pt\kern 4.08334pt}}_{{\kern-6.47188pt{J}\kern 4.08334pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-2.52779pt\kern 2.91667pt}}_{{\kern-5.3052pt{J}\kern 2.91667pt}}}
and
\pi_{J}(\alpha)\not\in\operatorname{cone}(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-3.69446pt\kern 4.08334pt}}_{{\kern-6.47188pt{J}\kern 4.08334pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-2.52779pt\kern 2.91667pt}}_{{\kern-5.3052pt{J}\kern 2.91667pt}}}\setminus\{\pi_{J}(\alpha)\})
.

(f)

If
\Pi
is linearly independent, then
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-3.69446pt\kern 4.08334pt}}_{{\kern-6.47188pt{J}\kern 4.08334pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-2.52779pt\kern 2.91667pt}}_{{\kern-5.3052pt{J}\kern 2.91667pt}}}
is linearly independent.

(g)

If
\Pi
is a basis of
V
, then
