\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-3.69446pt\kern 4.08334pt}}_{{\kern-6.47188pt{J}\kern 4.08334pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-2.52779pt\kern 2.91667pt}}_{{\kern-5.3052pt{J}\kern 2.91667pt}}}
is a basis of
\mathcal{V}(J)
.

Corollary 1.51
.

(a)

(G,\Upsilon_{X}^{{\mathrm{re}}})
has a realization.

(b)

The standard signed
G
-set
(G,\Lambda^{{\mathrm{rec}}})
of
G
has a realization.

(c)

The functor
{\mathcal{V}}
(from the groupoid
G
to the category of real vector spaces) is faithful.

(d)

The weakly realized signed groupoid set
(G,\Upsilon_{X},{\mathcal{V}},\iota)
satisfies the condition (
1.46.1
), so has an associated realized signed groupoid set
(G,\widetilde{\Upsilon}_{X},{\mathcal{V}},j)
.

1.52.

The preceding proposition and corollary show that the set
\mathchoice{\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{{{+}}}_{{{X}}}}{\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{{{+}}}_{{{X}}}}{\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{{{+}}}_{{{X}}}}{\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{{{+}}}_{{{X}}}}(J))

of
realized positive roots
based at
J

has convexity properties which are partly analogous to those of positive root systems of Coxeter groups, with
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-3.69446pt\kern 4.08334pt}}_{{\kern-6.47188pt{J}\kern 4.08334pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-2.52779pt\kern 2.91667pt}}_{{\kern-5.3052pt{J}\kern 2.91667pt}}}
playing a role (at the object
J
) similar to that of
\Pi
in
\Phi
. We do not have
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-3.69446pt\kern 4.08334pt}}_{{\kern-6.47188pt{J}\kern 4.08334pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-2.52779pt\kern 2.91667pt}}_{{\kern-5.3052pt{J}\kern 2.91667pt}}}\subseteq\mathchoice{\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{{{+}}}_{{{X}}}}{\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{{{+}}}_{{{X}}}}{\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{{{+}}}_{{{X}}}}{\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{{{+}}}_{{{X}}}}(J))
in general. However,
\mathchoice{\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{{{+}}}_{{{X}}}}{\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{{{+}}}_{{{X}}}}{\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{{{+}}}_{{{X}}}}{\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{{{+}}}_{{{X}}}}(J)
does contain

\{\,\pi_{J}(\alpha)\mid\alpha\in\Pi\setminus J,W_{J\cup\{\alpha\}}\in X_{J}\cap P_{J}\,\}\subseteq\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-3.69446pt\kern 4.08334pt}}_{{\kern-6.47188pt{J}\kern 4.08334pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-2.52779pt\kern 2.91667pt}}_{{\kern-5.3052pt{J}\kern 2.91667pt}}}

which we call the set of
realized simple roots
based at

J
. If
X_{J}{\,\supseteq\,}P_{J}
, then the set of realized simple
roots
based at
J
is
\pi_{J}(\Pi\setminus J)
, which is equal to
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-3.69446pt\kern 4.08334pt}}_{{\kern-6.47188pt{J}\kern 4.08334pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-2.52779pt\kern 2.91667pt}}_{{\kern-5.3052pt{J}\kern 2.91667pt}}}
if
J=J_{\mathrm{fin}}
.

The Brink-Howlett generators of
G
with codomain
J

correspond to the subset
\{\,\pi_{J}(\alpha)\mid\alpha\in\Pi\setminus J,W_{J\cup\{\alpha\}}\in Q_{J}\,\}\subseteq\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-3.69446pt\kern 4.08334pt}}_{{\kern-6.47188pt{J}\kern 4.08334pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-2.52779pt\kern 2.91667pt}}_{{\kern-5.3052pt{J}\kern 2.91667pt}}}
of
realized simple roots, which we refer to as the set of
real realized simple roots
based at
J
. The other (i.e. not real) realized simple roots at
J
will be be called
imaginary realized simple roots
at
J
.

Note that, especially in the case
X
is
R
or
N
, the realized root system
{\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-8.85509pt{J}\kern 5.55557pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-8.85509pt{J}\kern 5.55557pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-7.9441pt{J}\kern 5.55557pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-7.9441pt{J}\kern 5.55557pt}}}}
based at
J
may be
non-reduced
, in the sense that multiples
c\alpha
, where
c\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
,
c\neq 1
, of realized (even simple) roots
\alpha
may also be realized roots. See Proposition
6.14
.

Remark
.

In case
F
in Theorem
1.49
is only a weakly realized signed
groupoid-set, it may be useful to regard each realized root

\alpha\in\iota_{J}(\mathchoice{\Upsilon_{{{X}}}}{\Upsilon_{{{X}}}}{\Upsilon_{{{X}}}}{\Upsilon_{{{X}}}}(J)))
as having an
associated multiplicity, defined as the cardinal number

|\{\,(U,\epsilon)\in\Upsilon_{X}(J)\mid\iota_{J}(U,\epsilon)=\alpha\,\}|
of its fiber for the map

\iota_{J}
. Our results imply that real roots for
G
all have multiplicity equal to
1
in this sense (provided that one uses based root systems with all
roots of unit square length). However, we do not develop this idea further.

1.53.
Bilinear form

Let
L\subseteq\Pi
be such that all components of
L
are of finite type. Then the restriction of
B
to
\operatorname{span}(L)
is positive definite and the subspaces
\operatorname{span}(L)
and
L^{\perp}
of
V
are
B
-orthogonal with trivial intersection. The direct sum of these two subspaces consists of all elements
v\in V
such that
B(v,\alpha)\neq 0
for only finitely many
\alpha\in L
(see Section
6
).

We consider the following conditions on a subset
J
of
\Pi
.

(i)

For all
\alpha\in\Pi
, we have
B(\alpha,\beta)\neq 0
for only finitely many
\beta\in J_{\mathrm{fin}}
.

(ii)

J=J_{\mathrm{fin}}
(or equivalently,
J_{\inf}={\emptyset}
).

(iii)

V=\operatorname{span}(J_{\mathrm{fin}})\oplus(J_{\mathrm{fin}})^{\perp}
.

From the preceding remarks, for any
J\subseteq\Pi
, (iii) implies (i).

1.54.

Assume that
1.53
(iii) holds for all objects
J
of
G
. In the weakly realized signed groupoid-set

(G,\Upsilon_{X},{\mathcal{V}},\iota)
of Theorem
1.49
,
we canonically identify, for each object
J
of
G
,

(1.54.1)

{\mathcal{V}}(J)=V/\operatorname{span}(J_{\mathrm{fin}})=((J_{\mathrm{fin}})^{\perp}\oplus\operatorname{span}(J_{\mathrm{fin}}))/\operatorname{span}(J_{\mathrm{fin}})=(J_{\mathrm{fin}})^{\perp}.

Hence there is a symmetric bilinear form
B_{J}\colon{\mathcal{V}}(J)\times{\mathcal{V}}(J)\to\mathbb{R}
arising by restriction of
B\colon V\times V\to\mathbb{R}
.

The canonical map
\pi_{J}\colon V\mapsto V/\operatorname{span}(J_{\mathrm{fin}})
, given by

v\mapsto v+\operatorname{span}(J_{\mathrm{fin}})
, identifies with the orthogonal projection
V\mapsto(J_{\mathrm{fin}})^{\perp}
determined by
1.53
(iii). Moreover, for a morphism

w\colon K\to L
in
G
, determined by
w\in W
with
w(K)=L
, the map
{\mathcal{V}}(w\colon K\to L)\colon(K_{\mathrm{fin}})^{\perp}\to(L_{\mathrm{fin}})^{\perp}
is identified with
the restriction of
w\colon V\to V
.
Denoting this map simply as
w
, it respects the bilinear forms, in the sense that

B_{L}(wv,wv^{\prime})=B_{K}(v,v^{\prime}),\qquad v,v^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{V}}(K),

since
W
acts by orthogonal transformations on
(V,B)
.

Assumption 1.55
.

For the rest of this section, we assume that for all objects
J
of
G
, conditions
1.53
(i)–(iii) hold. We also assume, mainly for simplicity and uniformity in exposition, that
X
is equal to
R
,
M
or
P
, so that
X_{J}{\,\supseteq\,}P_{J}
.

Remark
.

Conditions
1.53
(i)–(iii) slightly refine Brink-Howlett’s reduction described in
1.39
. Though it is possible to prove more technical analogues of some of the following results assuming just
1.53
(iii), we do not go into details.
Similarly, using Theorem
1.33
(b) and Proposition
1.50
, results analogous to some of the following can also be formulated for
X=N
and
X=Q
.

1.56.

The consequences of the assumptions
1.55
described in
1.56
–
1.63
are proved in Section
6
. Let
J
in
\operatorname{ob}(G)
. Then
J=J_{\mathrm{fin}}
. We identify
\mathcal{V}(J)=J^{\perp}
and also identify
\pi_{J}
with the orthogonal projection
V\to J^{\perp}
.
Since
X_{J}{\,\supseteq\,}P_{J}
,
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-3.69446pt\kern 4.08334pt}}_{{\kern-6.47188pt{J}\kern 4.08334pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-2.52779pt\kern 2.91667pt}}_{{\kern-5.3052pt{J}\kern 2.91667pt}}}
is the set of realized simple roots of
\Upsilon_{X}
at
J
.

Somewhat more generally, for any subset
L
of
\Pi
such that all components of
L
are of finite type and such that

, we let

denote the

-orthogonal projection arising from this direct sum. We may sometimes write
L^{\perp}
as
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{L}}}}V^{{\kern-6.05559pt\kern 6.44447pt}}_{{\kern-9.8903pt{L}\kern 6.44447pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{L}}}}V^{{\kern-6.05559pt\kern 6.44447pt}}_{{\kern-9.8903pt{L}\kern 6.44447pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{L}}}}V^{{\kern-3.55832pt\kern 3.9472pt}}_{{\kern-6.44026pt{L}\kern 3.9472pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{L}}}}V^{{\kern-2.43054pt\kern 2.81943pt}}_{{\kern-5.31248pt{L}\kern 2.81943pt}}}
or,
if
L\in\operatorname{ob}(G)
, as
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{L}}}}\mathcal{V}^{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-9.34865pt{L}\kern 5.90282pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{L}}}}\mathcal{V}^{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-9.34865pt{L}\kern 5.90282pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{L}}}}\mathcal{V}^{{\kern-3.28612pt\kern 3.675pt}}_{{\kern-6.16806pt{L}\kern 3.675pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{L}}}}\mathcal{V}^{{\kern-2.23611pt\kern 2.625pt}}_{{\kern-5.11806pt{L}\kern 2.625pt}}}
.
For notational compactness, we may write
v_{L}:=\pi_{L}(v)
for

.

is linearly independent.

The following proposition lists some basic
properties of realized roots.

Proposition 1.58
.

Let
J\in\operatorname{ob}(G)
and
(U,\epsilon)\in\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Upsilon^{{\kern-5.75003pt\kern 6.13892pt}}_{{\kern-9.43843pt{J}\kern 6.13892pt{R}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Upsilon^{{\kern-5.75003pt\kern 6.13892pt}}_{{\kern-9.43843pt{J}\kern 6.13892pt{R}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Upsilon^{{\kern-3.42223pt\kern 3.81111pt}}_{{\kern-6.19965pt{J}\kern 3.81111pt{R}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Upsilon^{{\kern-2.33334pt\kern 2.72223pt}}_{{\kern-5.11076pt{J}\kern 2.72223pt{R}}}}
.

(a)

\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-3.69446pt\kern 4.08334pt}}_{{\kern-6.47188pt{J}\kern 4.08334pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-2.52779pt\kern 2.91667pt}}_{{\kern-5.3052pt{J}\kern 2.91667pt}}}\subseteq\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-8.85509pt{J}\kern 5.55557pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-8.85509pt{J}\kern 5.55557pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-7.9441pt{J}\kern 5.55557pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-7.9441pt{J}\kern 5.55557pt}}}^{+}_{X}\subseteq\operatorname{cone}(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-3.69446pt\kern 4.08334pt}}_{{\kern-6.47188pt{J}\kern 4.08334pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-2.52779pt\kern 2.91667pt}}_{{\kern-5.3052pt{J}\kern 2.91667pt}}})
where

\operatorname{cone}(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-3.69446pt\kern 4.08334pt}}_{{\kern-6.47188pt{J}\kern 4.08334pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-2.52779pt\kern 2.91667pt}}_{{\kern-5.3052pt{J}\kern 2.91667pt}}})\cap-\operatorname{cone}(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-3.69446pt\kern 4.08334pt}}_{{\kern-6.47188pt{J}\kern 4.08334pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-2.52779pt\kern 2.91667pt}}_{{\kern-5.3052pt{J}\kern 2.91667pt}}})=\{0\}
.

(b)

If
\alpha,\beta\in\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-3.69446pt\kern 4.08334pt}}_{{\kern-6.47188pt{J}\kern 4.08334pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-2.52779pt\kern 2.91667pt}}_{{\kern-5.3052pt{J}\kern 2.91667pt}}}
, then
B_{J}(\alpha,\beta)>0
if and only if
\alpha=\beta\in\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-3.69446pt\kern 4.08334pt}}_{{\kern-6.47188pt{J}\kern 4.08334pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-2.52779pt\kern 2.91667pt}}_{{\kern-5.3052pt{J}\kern 2.91667pt}}}^{\mathrm{re}}
.

(c)

B_{J}(r^{\prime}_{U,J},r^{\prime}_{U,J})\in\mathbb{R}
is positive, zero or negative according as the component of

\Pi_{U}
containing
r_{U,J}
is of
finite, affine or indefinite type.

(d)

Let
\alpha\in{\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-8.85509pt{J}\kern 5.55557pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-8.85509pt{J}\kern 5.55557pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-7.9441pt{J}\kern 5.55557pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-7.9441pt{J}\kern 5.55557pt}}}}_{X}
. Then

\alpha\in{\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-8.85509pt{J}\kern 5.55557pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-8.85509pt{J}\kern 5.55557pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-7.9441pt{J}\kern 5.55557pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-7.9441pt{J}\kern 5.55557pt}}}}_{X}^{\mathrm{re}}
if and only if

B_{J}(\alpha,\alpha)>0
.

(e)

{\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-8.85509pt{J}\kern 5.55557pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-8.85509pt{J}\kern 5.55557pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-7.9441pt{J}\kern 5.55557pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-7.9441pt{J}\kern 5.55557pt}}}}_{X}={\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-8.85509pt{J}\kern 5.55557pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-8.85509pt{J}\kern 5.55557pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-7.9441pt{J}\kern 5.55557pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-7.9441pt{J}\kern 5.55557pt}}}}_{X}^{\mathrm{re}}\dot{\cup}{\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-8.85509pt{J}\kern 5.55557pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-8.85509pt{J}\kern 5.55557pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-7.9441pt{J}\kern 5.55557pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{{\kern-5.16669pt\kern 5.55557pt}}_{{\kern-7.9441pt{J}\kern 5.55557pt}}}}_{X}^{\mathrm{im}}
and
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-3.69446pt\kern 4.08334pt}}_{{\kern-6.47188pt{J}\kern 4.08334pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-2.52779pt\kern 2.91667pt}}_{{\kern-5.3052pt{J}\kern 2.91667pt}}}={\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-3.69446pt\kern 4.08334pt}}_{{\kern-6.47188pt{J}\kern 4.08334pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-2.52779pt\kern 2.91667pt}}_{{\kern-5.3052pt{J}\kern 2.91667pt}}}}^{\mathrm{re}}\dot{\cup}{\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-3.69446pt\kern 4.08334pt}}_{{\kern-6.47188pt{J}\kern 4.08334pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-2.52779pt\kern 2.91667pt}}_{{\kern-5.3052pt{J}\kern 2.91667pt}}}}^{{\mathrm{im}}}

(f)

If
\alpha\in\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-3.69446pt\kern 4.08334pt}}_{{\kern-6.47188pt{J}\kern 4.08334pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-2.52779pt\kern 2.91667pt}}_{{\kern-5.3052pt{J}\kern 2.91667pt}}}^{\mathrm{re}}
, then

\alpha\not\in\operatorname{cone}(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-3.69446pt\kern 4.08334pt}}_{{\kern-6.47188pt{J}\kern 4.08334pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-2.52779pt\kern 2.91667pt}}_{{\kern-5.3052pt{J}\kern 2.91667pt}}}\setminus\{\alpha\})
.

1.59.
Longest elements

The next proposition describes some aspects of an analogy between morphisms
\nu(K,J)\colon J\to J^{\prime}
of
G
and longest elements of finite standard parabolic subgroups of Coxeter groups.

Proposition 1.60
.

Let
J\in\operatorname{ob}(G)
.

(a)

For a subset
K
of
\Pi\setminus J
, the element
\nu(K,J)
of
W
exists if and only if
K
is finite and the restriction of
B_{J}
to

\operatorname{span}(\pi_{J}(K))
is positive definite.

Assume in (b)–(f) that
w:=\nu(K,J)
exists.

(b)

\pi_{J}(K)=\{\,\alpha_{J}\mid\alpha\in K\,\}\subseteq\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-3.69446pt\kern 4.08334pt}}_{{\kern-6.47188pt{J}\kern 4.08334pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-2.52779pt\kern 2.91667pt}}_{{\kern-5.3052pt{J}\kern 2.91667pt}}}^{\mathrm{re}}
is linearly independent.

(c)

w^{-1}
is the join in weak (right) order
\leq
on
W
of

\{\,\nu(\alpha,J)^{-1}\mid\alpha\in K\,\}
.

(d)

Let
J^{\prime}:=wJ\subseteq K\cup J
and
K^{\prime}:=(K\cup J)\setminus J^{\prime}
. Then

(J^{\prime},w,J)
is a morphism in
G
with inverse

(J,w^{-1},J^{\prime})
.

(e)

The union
M
of all components of
K\cup J
which
meet
K
is spherical and the map

\alpha\mapsto-w_{M}\alpha
is a bijection

\sigma\colon K\to K^{\prime}
.

(f)

\mathcal{V}(J^{\prime},w,J)
restricts to a bijection

\alpha_{J}\mapsto-(\sigma\alpha)_{J^{\prime}}\colon\pi_{J}(K)\to-\pi_{J^{\prime}}(K^{\prime})
.

Remark 1.61
.

We say that a Brink-Howlett relation has
domain
J
if it expresses the equality of two specified products
of Brink-Howlett generators (and inverses), where both products
have domain
J
. For fixed
J
, the sets
K
in the proposition
such that
K
has cardinality one (resp., two) and
\nu(K,J)

exists are in natural bijective correspondence with the
Brink-Howlett generators with domain
J
and also with the
rank one Brink-Howlett relations with domain
J

(resp., with the rank two Brink-Howlett relations with domain

J
).

1.62.
Reflection-like action of Brink-Howlett generators

We give two formulae for the action of a Brink-Howlett generator, one showing it acts as a composite of an orthogonal reflection in a corresponding simple root with a linear isometry, and the other describing the action in a basis of simple roots.

Proposition 1.63
.

Let
J\in\operatorname{ob}(G)
and
\alpha\in\Pi\setminus J
be such that

w:=\nu(\alpha,J)
exists. Let
J^{\prime}:=\nu(J,\alpha)J\subseteq J\cup\{\alpha\}
and
\alpha^{\prime}\in\Pi
be such that
J\cup\{\alpha\}=J^{\prime}\cup\{\alpha^{\prime}\}
. Consider the Brink-Howlett generator
g=(J^{\prime},w,J)\colon J\to J^{\prime}
in
G
. Then:

(a)

\mathcal{V}(g)=\tau_{g}s_{\alpha_{J}}=s_{\alpha^{\prime}_{J^{\prime}}}\tau_{g}\colon\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\mathcal{V}^{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-9.20233pt{J}\kern 5.90282pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\mathcal{V}^{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-9.20233pt{J}\kern 5.90282pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\mathcal{V}^{{\kern-3.28612pt\kern 3.675pt}}_{{\kern-6.06354pt{J}\kern 3.675pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\mathcal{V}^{{\kern-2.23611pt\kern 2.625pt}}_{{\kern-5.01353pt{J}\kern 2.625pt}}}\to\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J^{\prime}}}}}\mathcal{V}^{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-10.66483pt{J^{\prime}}\kern 5.90282pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J^{\prime}}}}}\mathcal{V}^{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-10.66483pt{J^{\prime}}\kern 5.90282pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J^{\prime}}}}}\mathcal{V}^{{\kern-3.28612pt\kern 3.675pt}}_{{\kern-7.52603pt{J^{\prime}}\kern 3.675pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J^{\prime}}}}}\mathcal{V}^{{\kern-2.23611pt\kern 2.625pt}}_{{\kern-6.47603pt{J^{\prime}}\kern 2.625pt}}}

where
s_{\alpha_{J}}\colon\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\mathcal{V}^{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-9.20233pt{J}\kern 5.90282pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\mathcal{V}^{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-9.20233pt{J}\kern 5.90282pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\mathcal{V}^{{\kern-3.28612pt\kern 3.675pt}}_{{\kern-6.06354pt{J}\kern 3.675pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\mathcal{V}^{{\kern-2.23611pt\kern 2.625pt}}_{{\kern-5.01353pt{J}\kern 2.625pt}}}\to\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\mathcal{V}^{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-9.20233pt{J}\kern 5.90282pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\mathcal{V}^{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-9.20233pt{J}\kern 5.90282pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\mathcal{V}^{{\kern-3.28612pt\kern 3.675pt}}_{{\kern-6.06354pt{J}\kern 3.675pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\mathcal{V}^{{\kern-2.23611pt\kern 2.625pt}}_{{\kern-5.01353pt{J}\kern 2.625pt}}}
denotes the orthogonal reflection on
(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\mathcal{V}^{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-9.20233pt{J}\kern 5.90282pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\mathcal{V}^{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-9.20233pt{J}\kern 5.90282pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\mathcal{V}^{{\kern-3.28612pt\kern 3.675pt}}_{{\kern-6.06354pt{J}\kern 3.675pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\mathcal{V}^{{\kern-2.23611pt\kern 2.625pt}}_{{\kern-5.01353pt{J}\kern 2.625pt}}},B_{J})
in the vector
\alpha_{J}
,
s_{\alpha^{\prime}_{J^{\prime}}}
is the orthogonal reflection on
(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J^{\prime}}}}}\mathcal{V}^{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-10.66483pt{J^{\prime}}\kern 5.90282pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J^{\prime}}}}}\mathcal{V}^{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-10.66483pt{J^{\prime}}\kern 5.90282pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J^{\prime}}}}}\mathcal{V}^{{\kern-3.28612pt\kern 3.675pt}}_{{\kern-7.52603pt{J^{\prime}}\kern 3.675pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J^{\prime}}}}}\mathcal{V}^{{\kern-2.23611pt\kern 2.625pt}}_{{\kern-6.47603pt{J^{\prime}}\kern 2.625pt}}},B_{J^{\prime}})
in the vector
\alpha^{\prime}_{J^{\prime}}
and

\tau_{g}\colon\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\mathcal{V}^{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-9.20233pt{J}\kern 5.90282pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\mathcal{V}^{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-9.20233pt{J}\kern 5.90282pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\mathcal{V}^{{\kern-3.28612pt\kern 3.675pt}}_{{\kern-6.06354pt{J}\kern 3.675pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\mathcal{V}^{{\kern-2.23611pt\kern 2.625pt}}_{{\kern-5.01353pt{J}\kern 2.625pt}}}\to\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J^{\prime}}}}}\mathcal{V}^{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-10.66483pt{J^{\prime}}\kern 5.90282pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J^{\prime}}}}}\mathcal{V}^{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-10.66483pt{J^{\prime}}\kern 5.90282pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J^{\prime}}}}}\mathcal{V}^{{\kern-3.28612pt\kern 3.675pt}}_{{\kern-7.52603pt{J^{\prime}}\kern 3.675pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J^{\prime}}}}}\mathcal{V}^{{\kern-2.23611pt\kern 2.625pt}}_{{\kern-6.47603pt{J^{\prime}}\kern 2.625pt}}}
is the linear isometry which restricts to the identity map on
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J\cup\{\alpha\}}}}}V^{{\kern-6.05559pt\kern 6.44447pt}}_{{\kern-26.14522pt{J\cup\{\alpha\}}\kern 6.44447pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J\cup\{\alpha\}}}}}V^{{\kern-6.05559pt\kern 6.44447pt}}_{{\kern-26.14522pt{J\cup\{\alpha\}}\kern 6.44447pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J\cup\{\alpha\}}}}}V^{{\kern-3.55832pt\kern 3.9472pt}}_{{\kern-20.90805pt{J\cup\{\alpha\}}\kern 3.9472pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J\cup\{\alpha\}}}}}V^{{\kern-2.43054pt\kern 2.81943pt}}_{{\kern-19.78027pt{J\cup\{\alpha\}}\kern 2.81943pt}}}=\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J^{\prime}\cup\{\alpha^{\prime}\}}}}}V^{{\kern-6.05559pt\kern 6.44447pt}}_{{\kern-29.0702pt{J^{\prime}\cup\{\alpha^{\prime}\}}\kern 6.44447pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J^{\prime}\cup\{\alpha^{\prime}\}}}}}V^{{\kern-6.05559pt\kern 6.44447pt}}_{{\kern-29.0702pt{J^{\prime}\cup\{\alpha^{\prime}\}}\kern 6.44447pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J^{\prime}\cup\{\alpha^{\prime}\}}}}}V^{{\kern-3.55832pt\kern 3.9472pt}}_{{\kern-23.83304pt{J^{\prime}\cup\{\alpha^{\prime}\}}\kern 3.9472pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J^{\prime}\cup\{\alpha^{\prime}\}}}}}V^{{\kern-2.43054pt\kern 2.81943pt}}_{{\kern-22.70526pt{J^{\prime}\cup\{\alpha^{\prime}\}}\kern 2.81943pt}}}
and maps
\alpha_{J}\mapsto\alpha^{\prime}_{J^{\prime}}
.

(b)

Suppose that
\Pi
is a basis for
V
. Then for
\beta\in\Pi\setminus J
, we have

g(\beta_{J})=\kappa_{g}(\beta_{J})-\frac{B_{J}(\beta_{J},\alpha_{J})+B_{J^{\prime}}(\kappa_{g}(\beta_{J}),\alpha^{\prime}_{J^{\prime}})}{B_{J}(\alpha_{J},\alpha_{J})}\,\alpha^{\prime}_{J^{\prime}}.

where
\kappa_{g}\colon\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-3.69446pt\kern 4.08334pt}}_{{\kern-6.47188pt{J}\kern 4.08334pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-2.52779pt\kern 2.91667pt}}_{{\kern-5.3052pt{J}\kern 2.91667pt}}}\to\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J^{\prime}}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-11.33148pt{J^{\prime}}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J^{\prime}}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-11.33148pt{J^{\prime}}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J^{\prime}}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-3.69446pt\kern 4.08334pt}}_{{\kern-7.93437pt{J^{\prime}}\kern 4.08334pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J^{\prime}}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-2.52779pt\kern 2.91667pt}}_{{\kern-6.7677pt{J^{\prime}}\kern 2.91667pt}}}
is the bijection given by

\kappa_{g}(\alpha_{J})=\alpha^{\prime}_{J^{\prime}}
and

\kappa_{g}(\beta_{J})=\beta_{J^{\prime}}
if
\beta\in\Pi\setminus(J\cup\{\alpha\})
.

1.64.
Fundamental chamber and Tits cone

See
2.17
for definitions of the (closed) fundamental chamber and the Tits cone of the based root system
(\Phi,\Pi)
of
(W,S)
.
We define their analogues for realized root systems of Brink-Howlett groupoids. The results we state on these are proved in Section
7
.

For
J\in\operatorname{ob}(G)
, we define the
fundamental chamber

\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{C}}^{{\kern-5.31947pt\kern 5.70836pt}}_{{\kern-9.00787pt{J}\kern 5.70836pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{C}}^{{\kern-5.31947pt\kern 5.70836pt}}_{{\kern-9.00787pt{J}\kern 5.70836pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{C}}^{{\kern-3.15001pt\kern 3.5389pt}}_{{\kern-5.92743pt{J}\kern 3.5389pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{C}}^{{\kern-2.13889pt\kern 2.52777pt}}_{{\kern-4.9163pt{J}\kern 2.52777pt}}}\subseteq\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{V}}^{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-9.20233pt{J}\kern 5.90282pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{V}}^{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-9.20233pt{J}\kern 5.90282pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{V}}^{{\kern-3.28612pt\kern 3.675pt}}_{{\kern-6.06354pt{J}\kern 3.675pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{V}}^{{\kern-2.23611pt\kern 2.625pt}}_{{\kern-5.01353pt{J}\kern 2.625pt}}}
of
G
at
J
by

(1.64.1)

\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{C}}^{{\kern-5.31947pt\kern 5.70836pt}}_{{\kern-9.00787pt{J}\kern 5.70836pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{C}}^{{\kern-5.31947pt\kern 5.70836pt}}_{{\kern-9.00787pt{J}\kern 5.70836pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{C}}^{{\kern-3.15001pt\kern 3.5389pt}}_{{\kern-5.92743pt{J}\kern 3.5389pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{C}}^{{\kern-2.13889pt\kern 2.52777pt}}_{{\kern-4.9163pt{J}\kern 2.52777pt}}}:=\{\,v\in\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{V}}^{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-9.20233pt{J}\kern 5.90282pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{V}}^{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-9.20233pt{J}\kern 5.90282pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{V}}^{{\kern-3.28612pt\kern 3.675pt}}_{{\kern-6.06354pt{J}\kern 3.675pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{V}}^{{\kern-2.23611pt\kern 2.625pt}}_{{\kern-5.01353pt{J}\kern 2.625pt}}}\mid B_{J}(v,\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-3.69446pt\kern 4.08334pt}}_{{\kern-6.47188pt{J}\kern 4.08334pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-2.52779pt\kern 2.91667pt}}_{{\kern-5.3052pt{J}\kern 2.91667pt}}})\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\,\}.

The
Tits cone

.

1.66.

It is well known that pointwise stabilizers of subsets of the fundamental chamber of
C
of
(W,S)
on
V
are standard parabolic subgroups of
W
, which are themselves Coxeter groups. We state a corresponding result for Brink-Howlett groupoids.
It is essential for the formulation here that the functor
{\mathcal{V}}
from
G
to the category of real vector spaces is exactly as defined, not merely isomorphic to the one defined; the reader may reformulate the result in more category-theoretically natural terms if desired.

Recall that for an object
J
of
G
,
{\mathcal{V}}(J)=J^{\perp}\subseteq V
. For any morphism
g=(K,w,J)
of
G
,
{\mathcal{V}}(g)\colon J^{\perp}\to K^{\perp}
is the restriction of
w\colon V\to V
.
Then
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{X}}^{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-9.20233pt{J}\kern 5.90282pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{X}}^{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-9.20233pt{J}\kern 5.90282pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{X}}^{{\kern-3.28612pt\kern 3.675pt}}_{{\kern-6.06354pt{J}\kern 3.675pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{X}}^{{\kern-2.23611pt\kern 2.625pt}}_{{\kern-5.01353pt{J}\kern 2.625pt}}}
may be described as follows: the points of
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{X}}^{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-9.20233pt{J}\kern 5.90282pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{X}}^{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-9.20233pt{J}\kern 5.90282pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{X}}^{{\kern-3.28612pt\kern 3.675pt}}_{{\kern-6.06354pt{J}\kern 3.675pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{X}}^{{\kern-2.23611pt\kern 2.625pt}}_{{\kern-5.01353pt{J}\kern 2.625pt}}}\subseteq\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{V}}^{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-9.20233pt{J}\kern 5.90282pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{V}}^{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-9.20233pt{J}\kern 5.90282pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{V}}^{{\kern-3.28612pt\kern 3.675pt}}_{{\kern-6.06354pt{J}\kern 3.675pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{V}}^{{\kern-2.23611pt\kern 2.625pt}}_{{\kern-5.01353pt{J}\kern 2.625pt}}}=(J_{\mathrm{fin}})^{\perp}
are the points of
V
of the form
wp

where
p\in\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}{\mathcal{C}}^{{\kern-5.31947pt\kern 5.70836pt}}_{{\kern-10.33154pt{K}\kern 5.70836pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}{\mathcal{C}}^{{\kern-5.31947pt\kern 5.70836pt}}_{{\kern-10.33154pt{K}\kern 5.70836pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}{\mathcal{C}}^{{\kern-3.15001pt\kern 3.5389pt}}_{{\kern-6.87291pt{K}\kern 3.5389pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}{\mathcal{C}}^{{\kern-2.13889pt\kern 2.52777pt}}_{{\kern-5.86179pt{K}\kern 2.52777pt}}}\subseteq\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}{\mathcal{V}}^{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-10.526pt{K}\kern 5.90282pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}{\mathcal{V}}^{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-10.526pt{K}\kern 5.90282pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}{\mathcal{V}}^{{\kern-3.28612pt\kern 3.675pt}}_{{\kern-7.00902pt{K}\kern 3.675pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}{\mathcal{V}}^{{\kern-2.23611pt\kern 2.625pt}}_{{\kern-5.95901pt{K}\kern 2.625pt}}}=(K_{\mathrm{fin}})^{\perp}\subseteq V
for some
K\in\operatorname{ob}(G)
and for some morphism
(J,w,K)
of
G
. In this,
wp
denotes the image of
p\in V
under the action of
w\in W
on
V
.

Let
A\subseteq\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{V}}^{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-9.20233pt{J}\kern 5.90282pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{V}}^{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-9.20233pt{J}\kern 5.90282pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{V}}^{{\kern-3.28612pt\kern 3.675pt}}_{{\kern-6.06354pt{J}\kern 3.675pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{V}}^{{\kern-2.23611pt\kern 2.625pt}}_{{\kern-5.01353pt{J}\kern 2.625pt}}}\subseteq V
. Define a subgroupoid

of

, called the
pointwise stabilizer groupoid
of
A
in
G
,
as follows. The objects of

are the objects
K
of

such that
A\subseteq\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}{\mathcal{V}}^{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-10.526pt{K}\kern 5.90282pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}{\mathcal{V}}^{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-10.526pt{K}\kern 5.90282pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}{\mathcal{V}}^{{\kern-3.28612pt\kern 3.675pt}}_{{\kern-7.00902pt{K}\kern 3.675pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}{\mathcal{V}}^{{\kern-2.23611pt\kern 2.625pt}}_{{\kern-5.95901pt{K}\kern 2.625pt}}}=K^{\perp}
. A morphism

is by definition a morphism

in

such that

and

maps

for each

i.e. regarding

on

fixes

pointwise.

Theorem 1.67
.

Let
A\subseteq\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{C}}^{{\kern-5.31947pt\kern 5.70836pt}}_{{\kern-9.00787pt{J}\kern 5.70836pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{C}}^{{\kern-5.31947pt\kern 5.70836pt}}_{{\kern-9.00787pt{J}\kern 5.70836pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{C}}^{{\kern-3.15001pt\kern 3.5389pt}}_{{\kern-5.92743pt{J}\kern 3.5389pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{C}}^{{\kern-2.13889pt\kern 2.52777pt}}_{{\kern-4.9163pt{J}\kern 2.52777pt}}}

and

denote the full Brink-Howlett groupoid of

. Let

be the pointwise stabilizer groupoid of

in

(a)

A\subseteq C

(b)

The pointwise stabilizer of

in

is

.

(c)

H
is equal to the full subgroupoid of

on the objects of

.

(d)

is a union of components of

.

1.68.
Imaginary cone

Let

. Define cones
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{K}}^{{\kern-5.72922pt\kern 6.1181pt}}_{{\kern-9.41762pt{J}\kern 6.1181pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{K}}^{{\kern-5.72922pt\kern 6.1181pt}}_{{\kern-9.41762pt{J}\kern 6.1181pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{K}}^{{\kern-3.42223pt\kern 3.81111pt}}_{{\kern-6.19965pt{J}\kern 3.81111pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{K}}^{{\kern-2.33334pt\kern 2.72223pt}}_{{\kern-5.11076pt{J}\kern 2.72223pt}}}
and

\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{Y}}^{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-9.20233pt{J}\kern 5.90282pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{Y}}^{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-9.20233pt{J}\kern 5.90282pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{Y}}^{{\kern-3.28612pt\kern 3.675pt}}_{{\kern-6.06354pt{J}\kern 3.675pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{Y}}^{{\kern-2.23611pt\kern 2.625pt}}_{{\kern-5.01353pt{J}\kern 2.625pt}}}
in

by

(1.68.1)

\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{K}}^{{\kern-5.72922pt\kern 6.1181pt}}_{{\kern-9.41762pt{J}\kern 6.1181pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{K}}^{{\kern-5.72922pt\kern 6.1181pt}}_{{\kern-9.41762pt{J}\kern 6.1181pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{K}}^{{\kern-3.42223pt\kern 3.81111pt}}_{{\kern-6.19965pt{J}\kern 3.81111pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{K}}^{{\kern-2.33334pt\kern 2.72223pt}}_{{\kern-5.11076pt{J}\kern 2.72223pt}}}=-\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{C}}^{{\kern-5.31947pt\kern 5.70836pt}}_{{\kern-9.00787pt{J}\kern 5.70836pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{C}}^{{\kern-5.31947pt\kern 5.70836pt}}_{{\kern-9.00787pt{J}\kern 5.70836pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{C}}^{{\kern-3.15001pt\kern 3.5389pt}}_{{\kern-5.92743pt{J}\kern 3.5389pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{C}}^{{\kern-2.13889pt\kern 2.52777pt}}_{{\kern-4.9163pt{J}\kern 2.52777pt}}}\cap\operatorname{cone}(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-3.69446pt\kern 4.08334pt}}_{{\kern-6.47188pt{J}\kern 4.08334pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-2.52779pt\kern 2.91667pt}}_{{\kern-5.3052pt{J}\kern 2.91667pt}}}).

and

(1.68.2)

\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{Y}}^{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-9.20233pt{J}\kern 5.90282pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{Y}}^{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-9.20233pt{J}\kern 5.90282pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{Y}}^{{\kern-3.28612pt\kern 3.675pt}}_{{\kern-6.06354pt{J}\kern 3.675pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{Y}}^{{\kern-2.23611pt\kern 2.625pt}}_{{\kern-5.01353pt{J}\kern 2.625pt}}}=\bigcap_{K\in\operatorname{ob}(G)}\,\bigcap_{w\in\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-5.04243pt\kern 5.3202pt}}_{{\kern-7.81985pt{J}\kern 5.3202pt{K}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-5.04243pt\kern 5.3202pt}}_{{\kern-7.81985pt{J}\kern 5.3202pt{K}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-2.47409pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-4.60081pt{J}\kern 2.75186pt{K}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-1.68785pt\kern 1.96562pt}}_{{\kern-3.81458pt{J}\kern 1.96562pt{K}}}}}({\mathcal{V}}(w))(\operatorname{cone}(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-11.19266pt{K}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-11.19266pt{K}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-3.69446pt\kern 4.08334pt}}_{{\kern-7.41736pt{K}\kern 4.08334pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-2.52779pt\kern 2.91667pt}}_{{\kern-6.25069pt{K}\kern 2.91667pt}}})).

Note that for

, one has

if and only if

in

.

We define the
imaginary cone

\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\kern-4.45836pt\kern 4.84724pt}}_{{\kern-8.14676pt{J}\kern 4.84724pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\kern-4.45836pt\kern 4.84724pt}}_{{\kern-8.14676pt{J}\kern 4.84724pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\kern-2.60556pt\kern 2.99445pt}}_{{\kern-5.38298pt{J}\kern 2.99445pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\kern-1.75pt\kern 2.13889pt}}_{{\kern-4.52742pt{J}\kern 2.13889pt}}}
of

at

to be

(1.68.3)

\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\kern-4.45836pt\kern 4.84724pt}}_{{\kern-8.14676pt{J}\kern 4.84724pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\kern-4.45836pt\kern 4.84724pt}}_{{\kern-8.14676pt{J}\kern 4.84724pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\kern-2.60556pt\kern 2.99445pt}}_{{\kern-5.38298pt{J}\kern 2.99445pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\kern-1.75pt\kern 2.13889pt}}_{{\kern-4.52742pt{J}\kern 2.13889pt}}}=\bigcup_{K\in\operatorname{ob}(G)}\,\bigcup_{w\in\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-5.04243pt\kern 5.3202pt}}_{{\kern-7.81985pt{J}\kern 5.3202pt{K}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-5.04243pt\kern 5.3202pt}}_{{\kern-7.81985pt{J}\kern 5.3202pt{K}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-2.47409pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-4.60081pt{J}\kern 2.75186pt{K}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-1.68785pt\kern 1.96562pt}}_{{\kern-3.81458pt{J}\kern 1.96562pt{K}}}}}({\mathcal{V}}(w))(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}{\mathcal{K}}^{{\kern-5.72922pt\kern 6.1181pt}}_{{\kern-10.74129pt{K}\kern 6.1181pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}{\mathcal{K}}^{{\kern-5.72922pt\kern 6.1181pt}}_{{\kern-10.74129pt{K}\kern 6.1181pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}{\mathcal{K}}^{{\kern-3.42223pt\kern 3.81111pt}}_{{\kern-7.14513pt{K}\kern 3.81111pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}{\mathcal{K}}^{{\kern-2.33334pt\kern 2.72223pt}}_{{\kern-6.05624pt{K}\kern 2.72223pt}}})\subseteq\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{V}}^{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-9.20233pt{J}\kern 5.90282pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{V}}^{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-9.20233pt{J}\kern 5.90282pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{V}}^{{\kern-3.28612pt\kern 3.675pt}}_{{\kern-6.06354pt{J}\kern 3.675pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{V}}^{{\kern-2.23611pt\kern 2.625pt}}_{{\kern-5.01353pt{J}\kern 2.625pt}}}.

We call
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{K}}^{{\kern-5.72922pt\kern 6.1181pt}}_{{\kern-9.41762pt{J}\kern 6.1181pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{K}}^{{\kern-5.72922pt\kern 6.1181pt}}_{{\kern-9.41762pt{J}\kern 6.1181pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{K}}^{{\kern-3.42223pt\kern 3.81111pt}}_{{\kern-6.19965pt{J}\kern 3.81111pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{K}}^{{\kern-2.33334pt\kern 2.72223pt}}_{{\kern-5.11076pt{J}\kern 2.72223pt}}}
the
fundamental chamber
of
{\mathcal{Z}}
at
J
. The following theorem is proved in Section
8
.

Theorem 1.69
.

Let
J\in\operatorname{ob}(G)
.

(a)

For any morphism
(J,w,K)
in

,

.

(b)

\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\kern-4.45836pt\kern 4.84724pt}}_{{\kern-8.14676pt{J}\kern 4.84724pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\kern-4.45836pt\kern 4.84724pt}}_{{\kern-8.14676pt{J}\kern 4.84724pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\kern-2.60556pt\kern 2.99445pt}}_{{\kern-5.38298pt{J}\kern 2.99445pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\kern-1.75pt\kern 2.13889pt}}_{{\kern-4.52742pt{J}\kern 2.13889pt}}}=(-\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{X}}^{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-9.20233pt{J}\kern 5.90282pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{X}}^{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-9.20233pt{J}\kern 5.90282pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{X}}^{{\kern-3.28612pt\kern 3.675pt}}_{{\kern-6.06354pt{J}\kern 3.675pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{X}}^{{\kern-2.23611pt\kern 2.625pt}}_{{\kern-5.01353pt{J}\kern 2.625pt}}})\cap\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{Y}}^{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-9.20233pt{J}\kern 5.90282pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{Y}}^{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-9.20233pt{J}\kern 5.90282pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{Y}}^{{\kern-3.28612pt\kern 3.675pt}}_{{\kern-6.06354pt{J}\kern 3.675pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{Y}}^{{\kern-2.23611pt\kern 2.625pt}}_{{\kern-5.01353pt{J}\kern 2.625pt}}}

(c)

\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\kern-4.45836pt\kern 4.84724pt}}_{{\kern-8.14676pt{J}\kern 4.84724pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\kern-4.45836pt\kern 4.84724pt}}_{{\kern-8.14676pt{J}\kern 4.84724pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\kern-2.60556pt\kern 2.99445pt}}_{{\kern-5.38298pt{J}\kern 2.99445pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\kern-1.75pt\kern 2.13889pt}}_{{\kern-4.52742pt{J}\kern 2.13889pt}}}
is a cone in
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{V}}^{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-9.20233pt{J}\kern 5.90282pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{V}}^{{\kern-5.51393pt\kern 5.90282pt}}_{{\kern-9.20233pt{J}\kern 5.90282pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{V}}^{{\kern-3.28612pt\kern 3.675pt}}_{{\kern-6.06354pt{J}\kern 3.675pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{V}}^{{\kern-2.23611pt\kern 2.625pt}}_{{\kern-5.01353pt{J}\kern 2.625pt}}}
.

(d)

{\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Upsilon^{{\kern-5.75003pt\kern 6.13892pt}}_{{\kern-9.43843pt{J}\kern 6.13892pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Upsilon^{{\kern-5.75003pt\kern 6.13892pt}}_{{\kern-9.43843pt{J}\kern 6.13892pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Upsilon^{{\kern-3.42223pt\kern 3.81111pt}}_{{\kern-6.19965pt{J}\kern 3.81111pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Upsilon^{{\kern-2.33334pt\kern 2.72223pt}}_{{\kern-5.11076pt{J}\kern 2.72223pt}}}}^{\mathrm{im,+}}\subseteq\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\kern-4.45836pt\kern 4.84724pt}}_{{\kern-8.14676pt{J}\kern 4.84724pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\kern-4.45836pt\kern 4.84724pt}}_{{\kern-8.14676pt{J}\kern 4.84724pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\kern-2.60556pt\kern 2.99445pt}}_{{\kern-5.38298pt{J}\kern 2.99445pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\kern-1.75pt\kern 2.13889pt}}_{{\kern-4.52742pt{J}\kern 2.13889pt}}}
.

Remark 1.70
.

One can regard
{\mathcal{X}}
,
{\mathcal{Y}}
and
{\mathcal{Z}}
as cone-valued subfunctors of
{\mathcal{V}}
.

2.
Background and generalities on Coxeter groups

2.1.

Throughout this paper, unless otherwise specified,
(W,S)
denotes a Coxeter system associated to a based root system
(\Phi,\Pi)
in a real quadratic space
(V,B)
.

We may assume locally in the exposition or proofs that the Coxeter system or based root system satisfy additional conditions as convenience or necessity dictates, when there is no loss of generality in doing so. Two constructions which produce based root systems with useful additional properties are extension of the quadratic space, discussed in
2.2
and
2.3
, and lifts, discussed in
2.33
–
2.34
.

2.2.
Extension of quadratic space

If
(V,B)
and
(V^{\prime},B^{\prime})
are real quadratic spaces such that
V
is a subspace of
V^{\prime}
and
B
is the restriction of
B^{\prime}
to a bilinear form on
V
, we say
(V,B)
is a restriction of
(V^{\prime},B^{\prime})
and
(V^{\prime},B^{\prime})
is an extension of
(V,B)
.
3

Extensions
(V,B)
of
(V,B)
may be constructed as follows. Let
(U,D)
be any real quadratic space, and
C:U\times V\to\mathbb{R}
be any
\mathbb{R}
-bilinear form. Take
V^{\prime}:=V\oplus U
and define the symmetric bilinear form
B^{\prime}
by

B^{\prime}(v+u,v^{\prime}+u^{\prime})=B(v,v^{\prime})+C(u,v^{\prime})+C(u^{\prime},v)+D(u,u^{\prime}),

for
v,v^{\prime}\in V
and
u,u^{\prime}\in U
.

Extending the terminology of
[
25
]
, a quadratic space
(V,B)
is said to be ample for a subset
X
of
V
if every linear map
\operatorname{span}(X)\to\mathbb{R}
is of the form
v\mapsto B(v,v^{\prime}):\operatorname{span}(\Pi)\to\mathbb{R}
for some
v^{\prime}\in V
. Then
(V,B)
is also ample for any subset
X^{\prime}
of
\operatorname{span}(X)
.

Given a real quadratic space (V,B), one may choose an extension
(V^{\prime},B^{\prime})
of
(V,B)
as above such that
(V^{\prime},B^{\prime})
is ample for
V
, and is thus ample for every
subset of
V
. In fact, one may take
U:=V\oplus\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{R}}(V,\mathbb{R})
in the construction in the second paragraph of this subsection, any quadratic space
(U,D)
(for instance,
D=0
) and
C:U\times V\to\mathbb{R}
to be the evaluation pairing
C(f,v):=f(v)
.

2.3.

Let
(V^{\prime},B^{\prime}
) be an extension of the quadratic space
(V,B)
. Then any based root system
(\Phi,\Pi)
in
(V,B)
is also a based root system in
(V^{\prime},B^{\prime})
, and the associated Coxeter systems are canonically isomorphic (since they act faithfully on
\Phi\subseteq V
).

Suppose given a based root system
(\Phi,\Pi)
in the quadratic space
(V,B)
. Then
(V,B)
is ample for
\Pi
if and only if it is ample for
\Phi
; in that case, we may say that
(V,B)
is ample for
(\Phi,\Pi)
.

2.4.
Abstract based root systems

There may be uncountably many choices of a realized root system
\Phi
of a Coxeter system
(W,S)
, up to isomorphism, even requiring the simple roots to be a basis of the ambient vector space
V
. However, the underlying structure of
\Phi
as “abstract root system” (i.e. as a
W\times\{\pm 1\}
-set with distinguished subsets of positive roots and simple roots) depends only on
(W,S)
as Coxeter system.

In
2.5
–
2.6
, we give two different
(well-known) descriptions of this abstract root system directly in terms of
(W,S)
. Many of the results discussed in this section before
1.39
are of a purely algebraic and combinatorial nature, in that they can be (but are not always, here) formulated in terms of the abstract root system of
(W,S)
; however, their currently known proofs often involve realized root systems.

2.5.

We recall the definition (from
[
5
]
and
[
3
]
, for instance) of an “abstract” analogue
(\Xi,\Theta)
of
(\Phi,\Pi)
, which has the advantage it depends only on
(W,S)
(but the disadvantage that techniques from linear algebra, convex geometry etc which usefully apply to
(\Phi,\Pi)
are not applicable to it).

Let
\{\pm 1\}
be the cyclic group of order two with identity element
+1
. We sometimes write it as
\{\pm\}

Define the
W\times\{\pm 1\}
-set

\Xi:=T\times\{\pm 1\}
with
\{\pm 1\}
action by multiplication on the right factor
\{\pm 1\}
of
\Xi
and with
W
-action defined by

(2.5.1)

w(t,\epsilon)=(wtw^{-1},\eta_{w,t}\epsilon)

for
w\in W
and
t\in T
, where
\eta(w,t)=-1
if
t\in N(w^{-1})
and
\eta(w,t)=+1
otherwise.

This
W
-action is determined by that of the simple reflections, which is as follows:

(2.5.2)

s(t,\epsilon)=\begin{cases}(sts,-\epsilon),&\text{\rm if $t=s$}\\
(sts,\epsilon)&\text{\rm if $t\neq s$}\end{cases},\qquad\text{\rm for $s\in S$ and $t\in T$.}

Define sets of
positive roots

\Xi^{+}:=T\times\{+1\}
and
simple roots

\Theta:=S\times\{+1\}
of
\Xi
. Define the reflection in a root
\alpha=(t,\epsilon)\in\Xi
by
s_{\alpha}=s_{(t,\epsilon)}:=t
.
We call the pair
(\Xi,\Theta)
of a
W\times\{\pm\}
-set
\Xi
with distinguished subset
\Theta
the standard
abstract based root system
of
(W,S)
.

The relation of
(\Xi,\Theta)
to the based root system
(\Phi,\Pi)
is as follows: the map
\Phi\to\Xi
given by
\epsilon\alpha\mapsto(s_{\alpha},\epsilon)
for
\alpha\in\Phi^{+}
and
\epsilon\in\{\pm 1\}
is a
W\times\{\pm 1\}
-equivariant bijection which maps
\Phi^{+}
to
\Xi^{+}
and
\Pi
to
\Theta
.

2.6.

We recall another realization of the abstract root system of
(W,S)
which is
used in the study of buildings (see

[
37
]
,
[
1
]
,
[
33
]
).

The
W
-action
(w,x)\mapsto wx
by left translation on
W
leads naturally to an action
(w,A)\mapsto wA:=\{\,wa\mid a\in A\,\}
of
W
by left translation on the power set
\mathcal{P}(W)
. For each
t\in T
and
\epsilon\in\{\pm 1\}
, let

H^{t,\epsilon}:=\{\,w\in W\mid\epsilon(\ell(tw)-\ell(w))>0\,\}.

One has
W\setminus H^{t,\epsilon}=H^{t,-\epsilon}
.
Let

\Xi^{\prime}:=\{\,H^{t,\epsilon}\mid t\in T,\epsilon\in\{\pm 1\}\,\}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(W),

\Xi^{\prime+}:=\{\,A\in\Xi^{\prime}\mid 1_{W}\in A\,\}

and
\Theta^{\prime}:=\{\,H^{s,+}\mid s\in S\,\}\subseteq\Xi^{\prime+}
.
Then
\Xi^{\prime}
is a
W\times\{\pm 1\}
-set with
W
-action as a subset of
\mathcal{P}(W)
and with
\{\pm\}
-action determined by
-A:=W\setminus A
for
A\in\Xi^{\prime}
. The map

(t,\epsilon)\mapsto H^{t,\epsilon}
is a
W\times\{\pm\}
-equivariant bijection
\Xi\xrightarrow{\cong}\Xi^{\prime}
which maps
\Xi^{+}\xrightarrow{\cong}\Xi^{\prime+}
and
\Theta\xrightarrow{\cong}\Theta^{\prime+}
.

Remark 2.7
.

Note that as
W
-set,
\Xi^{\prime}
is the union of the
W
-orbits on
\mathcal{P}(W)
of elements of
\Theta^{\prime}
, the action by
-1
is
A\mapsto W\setminus A
, and the positive elements of
\Xi^{\prime}
are those which contain
1_{W}
.
This description can be used to generalize the construction of
(\Xi^{\prime},\Theta^{\prime})
to preprincipal signed groupoid-sets, for which the atomic generators are good analogues of simple reflections.

We do not know of any generalization of the construction in
2.5
to preprincipal signed groupoid sets, because of the absence in general of suitable analogues of conjugacy and reflections, but Theorem

1.33
provides such a generalization for Brink-Howlett groupoids, using the extra structure provided by the relationship of those groupoids to Coxeter systems.

2.8.
Reflection subgroups

We record some basic facts concerning reflection subgroups,
shortest coset representatives, and corank.

First, there is the following description of canonical simple systems
\Pi_{U}
of reflection subgroups:

Theorem 2.9

(
[
20
]
,
[
13
]
)
.

For a subset
\Delta
of
\Phi^{+}
, one has
\Delta=\Pi_{U}
for some reflection subgroup
U
of
W
(where necessarily
U=W_{\Delta}=\langle\,s_{\beta}\mid\beta\in\Delta\,\rangle
) if and only if for all distinct
\beta,\gamma\in\Delta
, one has
B(\beta,\gamma)\in-\mathrm{COS}
where

\mathrm{COS}:=\{\,\cos\frac{\pi}{n}\mid n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 2}\,\}\cup\{\,\lambda\in\mathbb{R}\mid\lambda\geq 1\,\}.

Remark
.

A closely related result is as follows. Let
(V,B)
be a real quadratic space and
\Pi\subseteq V
. Then there exists a based root system
(\Phi,\Pi)
in
V
if and only if
\Pi
is positively independent,
B(\alpha,\alpha)=1
for all
\alpha\in\Pi
and
B(\alpha,\beta)\in-\mathrm{COS}
for all distinct
\alpha,\beta\in\Pi
.

Proposition 2.10
.

Let
W^{\prime}
be a reflection subgroup and
x\in W
.

(a)

If
W^{\prime}=\langle\,T^{\prime}\,\rangle
where
T^{\prime}\subseteq T
, then

W^{\prime}\cap T=\{\,wtw^{-1}\mid w\in W^{\prime},t\in T^{\prime}\,\}
.

(b)

There is a unique element
d
of the left coset
xW^{\prime}
with
\ell(d)
minimal.

(c)

Let
y\in xW^{\prime}
and
d
be as in (b). Then
y=d\iff y\Pi_{W^{\prime}}\subseteq\Phi^{+}\iff\Phi_{y^{-1}}\cap\Pi_{W^{\prime}}={\emptyset}\iff\Phi_{y^{-1}}\cap\Phi_{W^{\prime}}^{+}={\emptyset}\iff N(y^{-1})\cap(W^{\prime}\cap T)={\emptyset}\iff N(y^{-1})\cap\chi(W^{\prime})={\emptyset}
.

(d)

For
d
as in (b), we have
\chi(xW^{\prime}x^{-1})=d\chi(W^{\prime})d^{-1}
and
\Pi_{xW^{\prime}x^{-1}}=d\Pi_{W^{\prime}}
.

(e)

For
J\subseteq\Pi
, each coset
yW_{J}
with
y\in W
contains a unique representative
y^{J}
of minimal length
\ell(y^{J})
.
We have
y=y^{J}z
for a unique element
z\in W_{J}
.
Let
W^{J}:=\{\,y^{J}\mid y\in W\,\}
. Then
\ell(vw)=\ell(v)+\ell(w)
for all
v\in W^{J}
and
w\in W_{J}
.

Proof.

For (a), see
[
20
]
. For (b)–(c), see
[
4
, Lemma 1.2]
,
[
25
, 1.7]
or
[
20
, (3.4)]
. For (d), see
[
21
, Lemma 1]

or
[
25
, 1.7-1.8]
.

The assertions of (e) are well-known; see for instance
[
5
]
,
[
31
]
or
[
3
]
. ∎

Proposition 2.11
.

Let
U
be a reflection subgroup of
W
.

(a)

One has
\corank_{W}(U)=0
if and only if
U=W
.

(b)

If
w\in W
, then
\corank_{W}(wUw^{-1})=\corank_{W}(U)
.

(c)

If
J\subseteq\Pi
, then the standard parabolic subgroup
W_{J}
is a reflection subgroup of
W
with
\Pi_{W_{J}}=J
and
\corank_{W}(W_{J})=|\Pi\setminus J|
.

(d)

If
U_{1}\subseteq U_{2}\subseteq U_{3}
are reflection subgroups of
W
with
U_{i}
parabolic in
U_{i+1}
for
i=1,2
, then
U_{1}
is parabolic in
U_{3}
and

\corank_{U_{3}}(U_{1})=\corank_{U_{3}}(U_{2})+\corank_{U_{2}}(U_{1}).

Proof.

For (a), note that if
\textrm{corank}_{W}(U)=0
, then
W=\langle U\cap T\rangle=U
since
U
is a reflection subgroup.

Part (b) holds by definition of corank since for
T^{\prime}\subseteq T
,

W=\langle\,(U\cap T)\cup T^{\prime}\,\rangle\iff W=\langle\,(wUw^{-1}\cap T)\cup wT^{\prime}w^{-1}\,\rangle,

because
wTw^{-1}=T
implies

w((U\cap T)\cup T^{\prime})w^{-1}=(wUw^{-1}\cap T)\cup wT^{\prime}w^{-1}
.

We prove (c). Let
K=\{\,s_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in J\,\}
.
To show
\Pi_{W_{J}}=J
, it suffices to show that
\chi(W_{K})=K
. For
s\in K
, we have
N(s)\cap W_{K}=\{s\}\cap W_{K}=\{s\}
. This shows that
K\subseteq\chi(W_{K})
. Equality must hold since
K
generates
W_{K}
and any set of Coxeter generators (such as
\chi(W_{K})
) of
W_{K}
is an inclusion minimal set of generators of
W_{K}
(see
[
5
]
).

We have
W=\langle\,W_{K}\cup(S\setminus K)\,\rangle
. This implies that
\corank_{W}(W_{K})\leq|S\setminus K|=|\Pi\setminus J|
.
To prove this inequality is in fact an equality, it suffices by the Schröder-Bernstein theorem to show that if
T^{\prime}\subseteq T
is such that
\langle\,(W_{K}\cap T)\cup T^{\prime}\,\rangle=W
, then
|T^{\prime}|\geq|\Pi\setminus J|
. We may assume without loss of generality for the proof of this that
(\Phi,\Pi)
is a standard based root system, so
\Pi
is linearly independent. Let
\Gamma^{\prime}:=\{\,\alpha\in\Phi^{+}\mid s_{\alpha}\in T^{\prime}\,\}
.
Since
\langle\,(W_{K}\cap T)\cup T^{\prime}\,\rangle=W
, Proposition
2.10
(a) implies
\operatorname{span}(J\cup\Gamma^{\prime})=\operatorname{span}(\Phi_{J}\cup\Gamma^{\prime})=\operatorname{span}(\Pi)
.
Hence the image of
\Gamma^{\prime}
under the natural surjective linear map
\operatorname{span}(\Pi)\to\operatorname{span}(\Pi)/\operatorname{span}(J)
spans the quotient vector space, implying
|T^{\prime}|=|\Gamma^{\prime}|\geq\mathrm{dim}(\operatorname{span}(\Pi)/\operatorname{span}(J))=|\Pi\setminus J|
. This proves (c).

We prove (d). Let
S_{i}:=\chi(U_{i})
for
i=1,2,3
.
For
i=1,2
,
U_{i}=x_{i}\langle\,R_{i}\,\rangle x_{i}^{-1}
for some

x_{i}\in U_{i+1}
and
R_{i}\subseteq S_{i+1}
, by definition of parabolic subgroups. By Proposition
2.10
, we may assume without loss of generality that
N(x_{i}^{-1})\cap\langle\,R_{i}\,\rangle={\emptyset}
, and this implies that
S_{i}=\chi(U_{i})=x_{i}R_{i}x_{i}^{-1}
. Hence
x_{i}^{-1}S_{i}x_{i}=R_{i}\subseteq S_{i+1}
for
i=1,2
and

x_{2}^{-1}x_{1}^{-1}S_{1}x_{1}x_{2}\subseteq S_{3}
. This shows that
U_{1}
is parabolic in
U_{3}
, since it is generated by a conjugate
S_{1}
of a subset of
S_{3}
. Moreover, by (b)–(c),

\begin{split}\corank_{U_{3}}(U_{1})&=|S_{3}\setminus x_{2}^{-1}x_{1}^{-1}S_{1}x_{1}x_{2}|=|(S_{3}\setminus x_{2}^{-1}S_{2}x_{2})\,\dot{\cup}\,x_{2}^{-1}(S_{2}\setminus x_{1}^{-1}S_{1}x_{1})x_{2}|\\
&=|S_{2}\setminus x_{1}^{-1}S_{1}x_{1}|+|S_{3}\setminus x_{2}^{-1}S_{2}x_{2}|=\corank_{U_{2}}(U_{1})+\corank_{U_{3}}(U_{2}),\end{split}

completing the proof.
∎

2.12.

The following proposition is included only to suggest the complexity of behavior of ranks and coranks of general reflection subgroups of infinite Coxeter groups, which is similar (and related) to behavior of ranks of subgroups of non-abelian free groups. No subsequent use is made of the result.

Recall that a Coxeter system is of
locally finite type
if each of its finite rank standard parabolic subgroups is finite. Write
\aleph_{0}:=|\mathbb{N}|
.

Proposition 2.13
.

Let
(n_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
be a sequence such that
n_{2i}=3i+3
for
i\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0}
,

n_{-2i}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 3}
for
i\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
and

n_{2i+1}=\aleph_{0}
for
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then if
(W,S)
is irreducible and is not of finite, affine or locally finite type, it contains a chain
(W_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
of reflection subgroups
such that
W_{i}{\,\subsetneq\,}W_{j}
if
i<j
in
\mathbb{Z}
and
\rank(W_{i})=n_{i}
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

Recall that a Coxeter system
(U,R)
is
universal
if for any distinct
r,s\in R
, the order of
rs
is infinite.
Firstly, from
[
25
]
, the stated assumptions on
(W,S)
imply that
W
contains a universal reflection subgroup
U
of rank three. Secondly, in any rank three universal Coxeter group
U
such that
\chi(U)=\{r,s,t\}
, there is an infinite rank universal reflection subgroup
U^{\prime}
such that
\chi(U^{\prime})=\{\,(rs)^{k}t(rs)^{-k}\mid k\in\mathbb{Z}\,\}
, which is denumerably infinite (see
[
20
]
or
[
13
, Example 3.19]
; note also that the subgroup
\langle\,rs,rt\,\rangle
of
W
is free of rank two). One may construct a chain of reflection subgroups of
(W,S)
as described, all contained in
U
as in the first fact, by adroit use of the second fact. Details are left to the reader. ∎

2.14.
Parabolic subgroups

The
parabolic closure

\pc(G)
of a subset (e.g. subgroup)
G
of
W
is defined to be the intersection of all parabolic subgroups of
W
which contain
G
. In general, it is not necessarily a parabolic subgroup of
W
, but is a locally parabolic (reflection) subgroup as defined by Nuida
[
34
]
. We collect below some known facts about parabolic subgroups and parabolic closure.

Proposition 2.15
.

Let
U
and
U^{\prime}
be parabolic subgroups of
W
, and
J\subseteq\Pi
.

(a)

U\cap U^{\prime}
is a parabolic subgroup of both
U
and
U^{\prime}
.

(b)

U\cap U^{\prime}
is a parabolic subgroup of
W
.

(c)

If
W^{\prime}
is a finite reflection subgroup of
W
, and
P:=\pc(W^{\prime})
, then
P
is finite,
\rank(P)=\rank(W^{\prime})=\dim(\operatorname{span}(\Phi_{W^{\prime}}))=|\Pi_{W^{\prime}}|
and
\Phi_{P}=\operatorname{span}(\Phi_{W^{\prime}})\cap\Phi
.

(d)

If
W^{\prime}=\langle\,J\cup X\,\rangle
for some finite
X\subseteq W
, then
\pc(W^{\prime})=P
where
P
is the unique parabolic subgroup
P{\,\supseteq\,}W^{\prime}
of minimum (finite) corank
\corank_{P}(W_{J})
.

Proof.

Parts (a)-(c) are well-known; see for example

[
21
, 2.4]
,
[
25
, 2.7, 2.9, 2.12]
and
[
33
, 1.2.6, 3.2]
.

We prove (d). Since
X
is finite, there is a finite subset
Q
of
\Pi
such that
X\subseteq W_{Q}
. For instance, take
Q
to be the set of all simple roots corresponding to simple reflections
s\in S
such that there is some
x\in X
such that
s
appears as a factor in some (equivalently, every) reduced expression of
x
. Let
H:=W_{J\cup Q}
. Clearly,
W^{\prime}\subseteq H
. By definition of corank,
\corank_{H}(W_{J})\leq|Q|<\infty
.
Thus,
H
is a (standard) parabolic subgroup of
W
such that
W^{\prime}\subseteq H
and
\corank_{H}(W_{J})
is finite.

Amongst all parabolic subgroups
P
of
W
such that
P{\,\supseteq\,}W^{\prime}
, choose one for which
\corank_{P}(W_{J})
is minimal.
Denote it as
P
, so

\corank_{P}(W_{J})\leq\corank_{H}(W_{J})<\infty
.

Suppose that
P^{\prime}
is a parabolic subgroup of
W
such that
W^{\prime}\subseteq P^{\prime}
. Note that
P^{\prime}\cap P
is parabolic in
P
by (b), and
P^{\prime}\cap P{\,\supseteq\,}W^{\prime}{\,\supseteq\,}W_{J}
. Since
W_{J}
is standard parabolic in
W
, it is (standard) parabolic in any reflection subgroup (such as
P^{\prime}\cap P
) which contains it. By Proposition

2.11
(d),

\corank_{P}(P^{\prime}\cap P)+\corank_{P^{\prime}\cap P}(W_{J})=\corank_{P}(W_{J})<\infty.

By choice of
P
,
\corank_{P^{\prime}\cap P}(W_{J})\geq\corank_{P}(W_{J})
. This forces
\corank_{P^{\prime}\cap P}(W_{J})=\corank_{P}(W_{J})
and

\corank_{P}(P^{\prime}\cap P)=0
and so
P=P^{\prime}\cap P
by Proposition

2.11
(a). Thus,
P\subseteq P^{\prime}
. Hence
P
is the inclusion-minimum parabolic subgroup containing
W^{\prime}
, which implies

\pc(W^{\prime})=P
. This proves (d).
∎

Lemma 2.16
.

Let
W^{\prime}
be a parabolic subgroup of
W
, say
wW^{\prime}w^{-1}=W_{K}
where
K\subseteq\Pi
and
w\in W
. Assume that
W^{\prime}{\,\supseteq\,}W_{J}
where
J\subseteq\Pi
. Let
d
be the element of minimal length in
wW^{\prime}
. Then
dW^{\prime}d^{-1}=W_{K}
and
dW_{J}d^{-1}=W_{L}
where
L:=dJ\subseteq K
.

Proof.

This holds since
d\Pi_{W^{\prime}}=K
, by Proposition
2.10
(d), and
J\subseteq\Pi_{W^{\prime}}
.
∎

2.17.
Fundamental chamber, Tits cone and facial subgroups

Recall that the
fundamental chamber
4
4

4

For other notions of reflection representations of Coxeter groups, as in
[
5
]
or
[
26
]
, the fundamental chamber and Tits cone of a root system are naturally defined as subsets of the dual space of the ambient vector space of the root system, or subsets of the ambient vector space of an associated “dual” root system.
The root systems we consider in this paper are
“self-dual,” and for simplicity, we regard the fundamental chamber and Tits cone as subsets of the ambient vector space of the root system, in the natural way.

C
and
Tits cone

X
of
(W,S)

on
(V,B)
are defined by

\,C:=\{\,v\in V\mid B(v,\Pi)\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\,\},\qquad X:=\bigcup_{w\in W}wC.

A subset
J
of
\Pi
is said to be
facial
if
J=\Pi\cap v^{\perp}
for some
v\in C
, where
C
is the fundamental chamber of
(W,S)
on
(V,B)
.
A
standard facial subgroup
is defined to be a standard parabolic subgroup
W_{J}
of
W
such that
J\subseteq\Pi
is facial.
A
facial subgroup
of
W
is a (necessarily parabolic) subgroup of
W
which is
W
-conjugate to a standard facial subgroup.

Proposition 2.18
.

(a)

Every
W
-orbit on
X
contains a unique point of
C
.

(b)

If
v\in C
, the stabilizer in
W
of
v
is the standard
facial subgroup
W_{J}
where

J:=\{\,\alpha\in\Pi\mid B(\alpha,v)=0\,\}=\Pi\cap v^{\perp}
.

(c)

The standard facial subgroups of
W
are the stabilizers of points of the fundamental chamber
C
.

(d)

The facial subgroups of
W
are the stabilizers in
W
of points of the Tits cone
X
.

(e)

The Tits cone
X
consists of all points
v
of
V
which satisfy
B(v,\alpha)<0
for only finitely many
\alpha\in\Phi^{+}
. In particular,
X
is a convex cone.

(f)

Let
w=s_{1}s_{2}\cdots s_{n}\in W
, where
s_{i}=s_{\alpha_{i}}
withs
\alpha_{i}\in\Pi
. Let
\beta_{i}:=s_{1}s_{2}\cdots s_{i-1}(\alpha_{i})

for
i=1,\ldots,n
. Then
v-w(v)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}B(v,\alpha_{i}^{\vee})\beta_{i}
for all
v\in V
. Further, if
\ell(w)=n
, then
\{\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{n}\}=\Phi_{w}\subseteq\Phi^{+}
.

(g)

If
v\in V
and
w\in W_{J}
where
J\subseteq\Pi
, then

v-wv\in\operatorname{span}(J)
.

(h)

If
v\in C
and
w\in W_{J}
where
J\subseteq\Pi
, then

v-wv\in\operatorname{cone}(J)
.

Proof.

See
[
25
, Lemma 1.13 and Lemma 1.16]
. ∎

2.19.

Recall that a poset
I
is said to be directed if
I\neq{\emptyset}
and for any
x,y\in I
, there is
z\in I
such that
x\leq z
and
y\leq z
. A
I
-directed family of subsets of a set
X
is a family
(A_{i})_{i\in I}
of subsets of
X
such that

i\leq j
in
I
implies
A_{i}\subseteq A_{j}
.

We shall use below the following fact. If
I
is a directed set and

(A_{i})_{i\in I}
and
(B_{i})_{i\in I}
are
I
-directed families of subsets of
X
, then

(2.19.1)

(\bigcup_{i\in I}A_{i})\cap(\bigcup_{i\in I}B_{i})=\bigcup_{i\in I}(A_{i}\cap B_{i}).

We shall also require some elementary convex geometry.
Given a cone
C=\operatorname{cone}(X)
in
V
, a subset
F
of
C
is called a face of
C
if
F=\operatorname{cone}(F)
is itself a cone, and for all
x,y\in C
, if
x+y\in F
then
x,y\in F
. For any
f\in V^{*}:=\mathrm{Hom}(V,\mathbb{R})
such that
f(C)\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
,
C\cap\ker f
is a face of
C
, called an
exposed face
. We use below the well-known fact that
all faces of a
polyhedral cone
in
V
(i.e. a cone of the form
\operatorname{cone}(X)
where
X\subseteq V
is finite) are exposed faces (see for instance
[
39
]
).

Proposition 2.20
.

Let
J\subseteq\Pi
.

(a)

\Pi\cap\operatorname{cone}(J)=J
.

(b)

If
J
is a facial subset of
\Pi
, then
\operatorname{cone}(J)
is an exposed face of
\operatorname{cone}(\Pi)
.
The converse holds if
(V,B)
is ample for
\Phi
.

(c)

Suppose that
\Pi
is linearly independent. Then
\operatorname{cone}(J)
is a face of
\operatorname{cone}(\Pi)
. If
(V,B)
is ample for
\Phi
, then
J
is a facial subset of
\Pi
.

(d)

If
(V,B)
is ample for
\Phi
and
\Pi
is finite, then

J
is a facial subset of
\Pi
if and only if
\operatorname{cone}(J)
is a face of
\operatorname{cone}(\Pi)
.

Proof.

We prove (a). Suppose that
\alpha\in\Pi\cap\operatorname{cone}(J)
. Write
\alpha=\sum_{\beta\in J}c_{\beta}\beta
where all
c_{\beta}\geq 0
. Then

1=B(\alpha,\alpha)=\sum_{\beta\in J}c_{\beta}B(\alpha,\beta).

Since
B(\alpha,\beta)\leq 0
if
\beta\neq\alpha
, we must have
\alpha\in J
. Hence
\Pi\cap\operatorname{cone}(J)\subseteq J
. The reverse inclusion is trivial, so we have equality, proving (a).

Parts (b)–(c) follow readily from the definitions. Details are omitted.

We prove (d). The “only if” direction follows from (b). The “if” direction follows from (b) since the exposed faces and faces of
\operatorname{cone}(\Pi)
coincide, when
\Pi
is finite.
∎

Proposition 2.21
.

(a)

If a connected Brink-Howlett groupoid has an object which is a facial subset of
\Pi
, all its objects are facial subsets of
\Pi
.

(b)

Suppose that
J\subseteq\Pi
is such that either (1)
J
is a facial subset of
\Pi
(2)
J\cap\Gamma
is a facial subset of
\Gamma
for each finite subset
\Gamma
of
\Pi
or (3) each component of
J
is of finite type. Then
\operatorname{cone}(J)
is a face of
\operatorname{cone}(\Pi)
.

(c)

Suppose that
J\subseteq\Pi
is such that
\operatorname{cone}(J)
is a face of
\operatorname{cone}(\Pi)
. Then for any
G\subseteq\Pi
,
G\cap\operatorname{cone}(J)=G\cap J
,
\Phi^{+}_{J}=\Phi^{+}\cap\operatorname{span}(J)=\Phi\cap\operatorname{cone}(J)
and

\operatorname{cone}(G)\cap\operatorname{cone}(J)=\operatorname{cone}(G)\cap\operatorname{span}(J)=\operatorname{cone}(G\cap J).

Proof.

Part (a) follows directly from the fact (
[
25
, Lemma 2.10(d)]
) that if
J
is a facial subset of
\Pi
and
w\in W
satisfies
wJ=K
, then
K
is a facial subset of
\Pi
.

For the proofs of (b)–(c), assume without loss of generality, by extending the quadratic space
(V,B)
if necessary, that
(V,B)
is ample for
\Pi
. Let
I
denote the inclusion-ordered poset of finite subsets of
\Pi
.

We prove (b). If (1) holds, then
\operatorname{cone}(J)
is a face of
\operatorname{cone}(\Pi)
by Proposition
2.20
. Suppose (2) holds. Then for
\Gamma\in I
,
\operatorname{cone}(J\cap\Gamma)
is a face of
\operatorname{cone}(\Gamma)
. It is readily checked from the definition of faces of cones that, since
I
is directed, this implies that

\operatorname{cone}(J)=\bigcup_{\Gamma\in I}\operatorname{cone}(\Gamma\cap J)
is a face of
\operatorname{cone}(\Pi)=\bigcup_{\Gamma\in I}\operatorname{cone}(\Gamma)
.
Finally, suppose (3) holds. For each
\Gamma\in I
,
\operatorname{cone}({\emptyset})=\{0\}
is a face of
\operatorname{cone}(\Pi)
, by positive independence of
\Pi
. Hence
{\emptyset}
is a facial subset of
\Gamma
, by Proposition
2.20
(d). By
[
25
, Lemma 2.10(b)]
,
J\cap\Gamma
is a facial subset of
\Gamma
, since
J\cap\Gamma
is finite with all its components of finite type. By (2), it follows that
\operatorname{cone}(J)
is a face of
\operatorname{cone}(\Pi)
. This proves (b).

We now prove (c). Let
G\subseteq\Pi
. By Proposition
2.20
(a), we have

G\cap\operatorname{cone}(J)=G\cap\Pi\cap\operatorname{cone}(J)=G\cap J.

Suppose first that
J
is a facial subset of
\Pi
. Then the assertion in (c) that

\operatorname{cone}(G)\cap\operatorname{cone}(J)=\operatorname{cone}(G)\cap\operatorname{span}(J)=\operatorname{cone}(G\cap J)
is easily checked using the definition of facial subset, and the remaining
assertion of (c) (concerning
\Phi_{J}^{+}
) is contained in
[
25
, Lemma 2.4]
.

Now let
J
be any subset of
\Pi
such that
\operatorname{cone}(J)
is a face of
\operatorname{cone}(\Pi)
. We claim that for each
\Gamma\in I
,

\operatorname{cone}(\Gamma\cap J)
is a face of
\operatorname{cone}(\Gamma)
. Certainly,
\operatorname{cone}(\Gamma\cap J)
is a cone. Suppose that

x,y\in\operatorname{cone}(\Gamma)
satisfy
x+y\in\operatorname{cone}(\Gamma\cap J)
.
Write
x=\sum_{\alpha\in\Pi}c_{\alpha}\alpha
and

y=\sum_{\alpha\in\Pi}d_{\alpha}\alpha
where
c_{\alpha}=0=d_{\alpha}
unless
\alpha\in\Gamma
.
For
\alpha\in\Pi
, we have
(c_{\alpha}+d_{\alpha})\alpha+\sum_{\beta\in\Pi,\beta\neq\alpha}(c_{\beta}+d_{\beta})\beta=x+y\in\operatorname{cone}(\Gamma\cap J)\subseteq\operatorname{cone}(J)
. Since
\operatorname{cone}(J)
is a face of
\operatorname{cone}(\Pi)
, it follows that

(c_{\alpha}+d_{\alpha})\alpha\in\operatorname{cone}(J)
. This implies that if
c_{\alpha}\neq 0
or
d_{\alpha}\neq 0
, then
\alpha\in\Gamma
and
\alpha\in\operatorname{cone}(J)\cap\Pi=J
by Proposition
2.20
(a), so
\alpha\in J\cap\Gamma
. We conclude that
x,y\in\operatorname{cone}(\Gamma\cap J)
, proving the above claim.

The claim implies that for each
\Gamma\in I
,

\Gamma\cap J
is a facial subset of
\operatorname{cone}(\Gamma)
.
By the first paragraph of the proof of (c), all assertions of (c) hold with
\Pi
replaced by
\Gamma
,
J
replaced by
J\cap\Gamma
and
G
replaced by
G\cap\Gamma
.
Note that
\Pi
(resp.,
J
,
\operatorname{cone}(J)
,
\operatorname{span}(J)
,
G
,
G\cap J
,
\operatorname{cone}(G\cap J)
,

\Phi=\Phi_{\Pi}
,
\Phi^{+}=\Phi^{+}_{\Pi}
,
\Phi_{J}^{+}
) is the directed union over
\Gamma\in I
of the sets
\Pi\cap\Gamma
(resp.,
J\cap\Gamma
,
\operatorname{cone}(J\cap\Gamma)
,
\operatorname{span}(J\cap\Gamma)
,
G\cap\Gamma
,
G\cap J\cap\Gamma
,
\operatorname{cone}(G\cap J\cap\Gamma)
,

\Phi_{\Gamma}
,
\Phi^{+}_{\Gamma}
,
\Phi_{J\cap\Gamma}^{+}
). The assertions of (c) now follow in general from the special case considered above, when
J
is facial in
\Pi
, by taking directed unions and using (
2.19.1
).
∎

2.22.

We digress to observe an obvious consequence of the preceding proposition which was not explicitly highlighted in
[
38
]
(where results essentially equivalent to some of those above were obtained in finite rank) or
[
25
]
.

Define the
presentation graph
of a Coxeter
system
(W,S)
to be the edge-labeled (undirected, simple)
graph with vertex set
S
and an edge
\{r,s\}
joining

r,s\in S
if
r\neq s
and
rs
has finite order
m_{r,s}<\infty
,
that edge being labeled by the positive integer
m_{r,s}
if
m_{r,s}>2
. The presentation graph should not be confused
with the Coxeter graph, which may have edges labeled by
\infty
and does not have edges joining commuting simple
reflections.

The definition of based root system implies that the map
\alpha\mapsto\operatorname{cone}(\alpha)
is a bijection between
\Pi
and the set of one-dimensional faces (i.e. extreme rays) of
\operatorname{cone}(\Pi)
. The following result, which is an immediate consequence of Proposition
2.21
(b) shows that the edges of the presentation graph correspond to certain (not necessarily all) two-dimensional faces of
\operatorname{cone}(\Pi)
.

Corollary 2.23
.

Let
\alpha,\beta\in\Pi
be distinct. If there is an edge of the presentation graph joining
s_{\alpha}
and
s_{\beta}
, then
\operatorname{cone}(\{\alpha,\beta\})
is a face of
\operatorname{cone}(\Pi)
.

Remark
.

Parts of Proposition
2.21
can be
strengthened and generalized, leading in particular to more general constructions of realized or weakly realized root systems of Brink-Howlett groupoids based on the more general root systems of Coxeter groups described in
[
26
]
, and extensions of Corollary
2.23
and parts of Proposition
2.21
to Brink-Howlett groupoids. We do not discuss this further in this paper.

2.24.

The corollary above can be reformulated as follows if
(W,S)
has finite rank.

Assume that
\Pi
is finite, so
\operatorname{cone}(\Pi)
is a polyhedral cone. One may choose an affine hyperplane
H
in
V

such that for each
\alpha\in\Pi
,
\operatorname{cone}(\alpha)\cap H=\{p_{\alpha}\}
is a singleton set with
p_{\alpha}\neq 0
. Then
P:=H\cap\operatorname{cone}(\Pi)
is a convex polytope (a polytopal cross-section of
\operatorname{cone}(\Pi)
) and the map
\alpha\mapsto p_{\alpha}
induces a bijection from
\Pi
to the set of vertices of
P
.
In this situation, the corollary implies that if
\alpha,\beta\in\Pi
are distinct and there is an edge of the presentation graph joining
s_{\alpha}
and
s_{\beta}
, then there is an edge of
P
with
p_{\alpha}
and
p_{\beta}
as its vertices. Hence (the unlabelled graph underlying) the presentation graph identifies with a subgraph (containing all vertices) of the edge-graph of the convex polytope
P
.
More generally, the abstract simplicial complex of spherical subsets of
\Pi
has a geometric realization which is a subcomplex of the geometric simplicial complex of simplicial faces of
P
. See Example
6.12
.

2.25.
Finiteness criteria

We collect below some variants, which are useful in this paper, of known finiteness criteria for irreducible Coxeter systems. (There are many other important finiteness criteria we do not list.) Some of the cited proofs involve reduction of the results in general to their special cases for finite rank Coxeter systems in
[
12
, §4]
(see also
[
33
, Proposition 1.2.6]
).

Proposition 2.26
.

Assume that
(W,S)
is irreducible (so
S\neq\emptyset
). Then the following conditions are equivalent.

(i)

W
is finite.

(ii)

\Phi
is finite.

(iii)

For some
J\subsetneq\Pi
,
W^{J}
is finite.

(iv)

For some
J\subsetneq\Pi
,
\Phi\setminus\Phi_{J}
is finite.

(v)

For some
\alpha\in\Pi
, the
W
-orbit
W\alpha
on
\Phi
is finite.

(vi)

For some
J\subsetneq\Pi
, the index
[W:W_{J}]
is finite.

(vii)

For some
J\subsetneq\Pi
,
(W\setminus W_{J})\cap T
is finite.

(viii)

For some
s\in S
, the conjugacy class in
W
of
s
is finite.

In conditions (iii)–(viii), “some” can be replaced by “all”.

Proof.

It is well known that (i) and (ii) are equivalent.
Indeed, we have (ii)
\implies
(i) since the natural
W
-action on
\Phi
is faithful. The converse implication (i)
\implies
(ii). holds since the map
\beta\mapsto s_{\beta}\colon\Phi\to W

has the
\{\pm\}
-orbits on
\Phi
as its non-empty fibers. This map
\beta\mapsto s_{\beta}
restricts to surjections
\Phi\setminus\Phi_{J}\twoheadrightarrow(W\setminus W_{J})\cap T
for
J\subseteq\Pi
, and
W\alpha\twoheadrightarrow\{\,ws_{\alpha}w^{-1}\mid w\in W\,\}
for
\alpha\in\Pi
, from which it follows that (iv)
\iff
(vii) and (v)
\iff
(viii). We also have (iii)
\iff
(vi) since for
J\subseteq\Pi
,
W^{J}
is a set of left coset representatives for
W_{J}
in
W
.

By the equivalence of (i) and (ii), condition (i) implies the variants of (iii)-(viii) with “all” instead of “some.” Also, each such variant with “all” implies the corresponding condition with “some,” since
S
and
\Pi
are non-empty.
Finally, by
[
25
, Proposition 1.21 and Lemma 1.23]
, each condition (iii), (iv) or (v) implies (i). ∎

Proof of Proposition
1.35
.

We assume without loss of generality that
\Pi_{U}\setminus J
is finite, since that finiteness is readily implied by each condition
1.35
(i)–(iv). There are only finitely many components of
\Pi_{U}
which intersect
\Pi_{U}\setminus J
non-trivially. Denote these components as
\Pi_{1},\ldots,\Pi_{n}

The other components of
\Pi_{U}
are those components of
J
which have no vertex joined (in the Coxeter graph of
U
) to a vertex in
\Pi_{U}\setminus J
. For
i=1,\ldots,n
,
let
U_{i}:=W_{\Pi_{i}}
. Then
U_{i}
is a component of
U

(i.e. a non-trivial, inclusion-maximal, irreducible standard parabolic subgroup of
U
). Define
J_{i}:=J\cap\Pi_{i}\subseteq\Pi
. Then
W_{U_{i}}
is a reflection overgroup of
W_{J_{i}}
, and the above implies

\displaystyle(U\setminus W_{J})\cap T

\displaystyle=\dot{\bigcup}_{i=1}^{n}((U_{i}\setminus W_{J_{i}})\cap T),

\displaystyle\Phi_{U}\setminus\Phi_{J}

\displaystyle=\dot{\bigcup}_{i=1}^{n}(\Phi_{U_{i}}\setminus\Phi_{J_{i}}),

\displaystyle\corank_{U}(W_{J})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\corank_{U_{i}}(W_{J_{i}}),

\displaystyle[U:W_{J}]

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{n}[U_{i}\colon W_{J_{i}}].

Using these formulae, the proof of Proposition
1.35
readily reduces to that of its special case in which
U
