is irreducible, in which case it follows by applying Proposition
2.26
to
(U,\chi(U))
instead of
(W,S)
.
∎

2.27.
Root poset

Define a relation
\lessdot
on
\Phi
such that
\rho\lessdot\tau
if
\rho,\tau\in\Phi
and there exists
\alpha\in\Pi
such that
\rho=s_{\alpha}\tau
and
0\neq\tau-\rho\in\operatorname{cone}(\alpha)
. Let
\leq
denote the preorder on
\Phi
which is defined as the reflexive, transitive closure of the relation
\lessdot
. We call
(\Phi,\leq)
the
root poset

of
(\Phi,\Pi)
, as justified by the following proposition. This differs slightly from
[
3
, Chapter 4]
, where the restriction of
\leq
to a partial order on
\Phi^{+}

is called the root poset.

Proposition 2.28
.

(a)

The preorder
\leq
on
\Phi
is a partial order.

(b)

The map
\rho\mapsto-\rho
is an antitone (i.e. order reversing) bijection of the root poset with itself.

(c)

Define a function
\ell^{\prime}\colon\Phi\to\mathbb{Z}
by
\ell^{\prime}(\epsilon\alpha)=\epsilon\ell(s_{\alpha})
for
\alpha\in\Phi^{+}
and
\epsilon\in\{\pm 1\}
. Then
\ell^{\prime}(\tau)
is odd
and
\ell^{\prime}(-\tau)=-\ell^{\prime}(\tau)
for all
\tau\in\Phi
.

(d)

If
\rho,\tau\in\Phi
with
\rho\lessdot\tau
, then
\ell^{\prime}(\tau)=\ell^{\prime}(\rho)+2
.

(e)

\lessdot
is the covering relation for
(\Phi,\leq)
.

(f)

If
\tau\in\Phi^{+}
, then
\{\rho\in\Phi^{+}\mid\rho\leq\tau\}
is finite.

(g)

For any
\rho\leq\tau
in
\Phi
, the closed interval

[\rho,\tau]:=\{\,\sigma\in\Phi\mid\rho\leq\sigma\leq\tau\,\}
in the root poset is finite.

Proof.

For (a), we have to show
\leq
is anti-symmetric. The definitions of
\lessdot
and
\leq
imply that if
\rho\leq\tau
in
\Phi
, then
\tau-\rho\in\operatorname{cone}(\Pi)
. Since

\operatorname{cone}(\Pi)\cap-\operatorname{cone}(\Pi)=\{0\}
, (a) follows. Part (b) follows from the easily checked fact that if
\rho\lessdot\tau
in
\Phi
, then
-\tau\lessdot-\rho
. Part (c) s trivial.

We prove (d). Let
\rho,\tau\in\Phi
with
\rho\lessdot\tau
.
If
\rho
and
\tau
are both positive roots, or both negative roots, the result follows from the definitions using the notion of the depth of a positive root and its relation to length of the corresponding reflection (see
[
23
, Equation (1) and Proposition 2.4]
). Otherwise, we have
\tau\in\Phi^{+}
and

\rho=s_{\alpha}(\tau)\in\Phi_{-}
for some
\alpha\in\Pi
, which implies
\tau=\alpha=-\rho\in\Pi
,
\ell^{\prime}(\tau)=1
and so
\ell^{\prime}(\rho)=-1
. Part (e) follows easily from (d) and the definitions.

We prove (f). Fix
\tau\in\Phi^{+}
. Suppose that
\rho\lessdot\tau
in
\Phi
with
\rho\in\Phi^{+}
. Let
\alpha\in\Pi
with
\rho=s_{\alpha}\tau
and
0\neq\tau-\rho\in\operatorname{cone}(\alpha)
. Then
s_{\tau}=s_{\alpha}s_{\rho}s_{\alpha}
with
\ell(s_{\tau})=\ell(s_{\rho})+2
by (d). Hence
s_{\rho}\leq^{\prime}s_{\tau}
where
\leq^{\prime}
is Bruhat order of
(W,S)
. It now follows that for any
\tau\in\Phi^{+}
, any
\rho\in\Phi^{+}
with
\rho\leq\tau
satisfies
1\leq^{\prime}s_{\rho}\leq^{\prime}s_{\tau}
. Since Bruhat intervals are finite and
\beta\mapsto s_{\beta}\colon\Phi^{+}\to T
is injective, (f) follows.

Finally, (g) follows
from (b)–(c) on noting that

[\rho,\tau]\subseteq\{\,\sigma\in\Phi^{+}\mid\sigma\leq\tau\,\}\cup\{\,\sigma\in\Phi^{-}\mid\rho\leq\sigma\,\}.\qed

Proposition 2.29
.

Let
K\subseteq\Pi
and let
C_{K}:=\{\,v\in V\mid B(v,K)\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\,\}
denote the fundamental chamber for
W_{K}
on
V
. Let
\gamma\in\Phi^{+}\cap C_{K}
. Then:

(a)

The stabilizer of
\gamma
in
W_{K}
is
W_{J}
where
J:=\{\,\beta\in K\mid B(\gamma,\beta)=0\,\}
.

(b)

If
\rho\in W_{K}\gamma
, then
\rho\leq\gamma
in the root poset
(\Phi,\leq)
.

(c)

|W_{K}\gamma|=[W_{K}\colon W_{J}]<\infty
.

(d)

\gamma\in\Phi^{+}_{K}\iff W_{K}\gamma\cap\Phi^{-}\neq{\emptyset}\iff\gamma\in\Phi^{+}_{K}\setminus\Phi^{+}_{J}
.

(e)

Suppose that
\gamma\in\Phi_{K}^{+}\cap C_{K}
. Let
L
denote the component of

K
such that
\gamma\in\Phi_{L}
. Then
L
is of finite type,

\gamma\in\Phi^{+}_{L}\cap C_{L}
and
K=J\cup L
.

Proof.

Part (a) follows from Proposition
2.18
.

To prove (b), write
\rho=w\gamma
where
w\in W_{K}^{J}:=W_{K}\cap W^{J}
. Choose a reduced expression

w=s_{\alpha_{m}}\cdots s_{\alpha_{1}}
with each
\alpha_{i}\in K
. We show that

\rho=s_{\alpha_{m}}\cdots s_{\alpha_{1}}\gamma\lessdot\ldots\lessdot s_{\alpha_{1}}\gamma\lessdot\gamma.

To prove this, it suffices to show that for
i=1,\ldots,m
,

0<B(\alpha_{i},s_{\alpha_{i-1}}\cdots s_{\alpha_{1}}(\gamma))=B(s_{\alpha_{1}}\cdots s_{\alpha_{i-1}}(\alpha_{i}),\gamma).

Let
\beta_{i}:=s_{\alpha_{1}}\ldots s_{\alpha_{i-1}}(\alpha_{i})
. We have to show
B(\beta_{i},\gamma)>0
. It is well known (see for instance
[
31
]
,
[
3
]
,
[
25
, Lemma 1.16]
) that

\Phi_{w^{-1}}=\{\,\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{m}\,\}
. Since
w\in W^{J}_{K}
, we have
\Phi_{w^{-1}}\subseteq\Phi_{K}^{+}\setminus\Phi_{J}
. From
\beta_{i}\in\Phi_{K}^{+}
, we get
B(\beta_{i},\gamma)\geq 0
. Since
\beta_{i}\not\in\Phi_{J}
, we have

s_{\beta_{i}}\in W_{K}\setminus W_{J}
, so
s_{\beta_{i}}(\gamma)\neq\gamma
and
B(\beta_{i},\gamma)\neq 0
. Hence
B(\beta_{i},\gamma)>0
, completing the proof of (b).

Note that (a) implies
|W_{K}\gamma|=[W_{K}\colon W_{J}]
in (c). To complete the proof of (c), we shall show that the orbit
W_{K}\gamma
is finite.

If the orbit
W_{K}\gamma
is contained in
\Phi^{+}
, it is finite by Proposition
2.28
(f).
Otherwise, let
\rho\in W_{K}\gamma\cap\Phi^{-}
. We may write
\rho=w\gamma
with
w\in W_{K}^{J}
as in the proof of (b). In that argument, there must exist

i>0
such that
\delta:=s_{\alpha_{i-1}}\cdots s_{\alpha_{1}}\gamma\in\Phi^{+}
and
s_{\alpha_{i}}\cdots s_{\alpha_{1}}\gamma\in\Phi^{-}
. This implies that
\delta=\alpha_{i}\in K
.
Since
\delta\in W_{K}\gamma\cap K
, we get

W_{K}\gamma=W_{K}\delta=(W_{K}s_{\delta})\delta=W_{K}(s_{\delta}\delta)=-W_{K}\delta=-W_{K}\gamma.

Since
W_{K}\gamma
has maximum element
\gamma
in

\leq
, it also has minimum element
-\gamma
by
Proposition
2.28
(c). Thus,

W_{K}\gamma\subseteq\{\,\tau\in\Phi\mid-\gamma\leq\tau\leq\gamma\,\}
, which is finite by Proposition
2.28
(g).
This proves (c).

We prove (d). If
\gamma\in\Phi_{K}^{+}
, then

-\gamma=s_{\gamma}\gamma\in W_{K}\gamma\cap\Phi_{-}
.
Next, suppose
W_{K}\gamma\cap\Phi_{-}\neq{\emptyset}
. From
the proof of (c), there exists
w\in W_{K}^{J}
with
w\gamma\in\Phi^{-}
. Then
\gamma\in\Phi_{w^{-1}}\subseteq\Phi_{K}^{+}\setminus\Phi_{J}^{+}
. Trivially, the rightmost condition in (d) implies the leftmost one. This completes the proof of (d).

We prove (e). By (c),
[W_{K}\colon W_{J}]
is finite, so Proposition
1.35
implies that the component
\Phi_{L}
of
\Phi_{K}
containing
\gamma
is of finite type. Every component of
K
other than
L
is orthogonal to
L
, hence contained in
J
. This shows that
K=J\cup L
. Since
L\subseteq K
, we have
\gamma\in C_{K}\subseteq C_{L}
. ∎

Proof of Proposition
1.41
.

We prove (a). Let
M:=\Pi\cap L^{\perp}
.
We will show that
\Phi\cap L^{\perp}=\Phi_{M}
. Clearly

\Phi_{M}\subseteq\Phi\cap L^{\perp}
, since
M\subseteq\Phi\cap L^{\perp}
. For the proof of the reverse inclusion, let
\alpha\in\Phi^{+}\cap L^{\perp}
. We show

\alpha\in\Phi_{M}
by induction on
\ell(s_{\alpha})
.
If
\ell(s_{\alpha})=1
, then
\alpha\in\Pi\cap L^{\perp}=M\subseteq\Phi_{M}
.

Suppose
\ell(s_{\alpha})>1
. Since
\alpha\in\operatorname{cone}(\Pi)
and
B(\alpha,\alpha)=1
, there exists some
\beta\in\Pi
with
B(\alpha,\beta)>0
. Let
K:=L\cup\{\beta\}
Then
\alpha\in\Phi^{+}\cap C_{K}
. Note
L=\{\,\gamma\in K\mid B(\alpha,\gamma)=0\,\}
and
\beta\in K\setminus L
. By Proposition
2.29
, the index
[W_{K}:W_{L}]
is finite, and therefore by Proposition
1.35
, the component of
K
containing

\beta
is of finite type. Since each component of
L
is of infinite type,

\beta
cannot be joined to any vertex of
L
in the Coxeter graph of
K
. Hence
\beta\in\Pi\cap L^{\perp}=M
.

Now from
\beta\in\Pi\cap L^{\perp}
and
\alpha\in\Phi\cap L^{\perp}
, follows that
s_{\beta}(\alpha)\in\Phi\cap L^{\perp}
. Since
s_{\beta}(\alpha)\lessdot\alpha
where
\ell(s_{\alpha})\geq 3
, we also have
\ell(s_{s_{\beta}\alpha})=\ell(s_{\alpha})-2\geq 1
and
s_{\beta}(\alpha)\in\Phi^{+}
, by Proposition
2.28
. Induction gives
s_{\beta}\alpha\in\Phi^{+}_{M}
and so
\alpha\in\Phi_{M}
, proving (a)

The proof of (b) follows the same general lines as that of (a).
Let
U:=\{\,v\in V\mid\text{\rm$B(v,\gamma)\neq 0$ for only finitely many $\gamma\in L$}\,\}
.
Let
M:=\Pi\cap U
. We show
\Phi\cap U=\Phi_{M}
. Clearly

\Phi_{M}\subseteq\Phi\cap U
, since
M\subseteq\Phi\cap U
. For the proof of the reverse inclusion, let
\alpha\in\Phi^{+}\cap U
. We show

\alpha\in\Phi_{M}
by induction on
\ell(s_{\alpha})
.
If
\ell(s_{\alpha})=1
, then
\alpha\in\Pi\cap U=M\subseteq\Phi_{M}
.

Suppose
\ell(s_{\alpha})>1
. Since
B(\alpha,\alpha)>0
, we may choose
\beta\in\Pi
with
c:=B(\alpha,\beta^{\vee})>0
.
Then
s_{\beta}(\alpha)=\alpha-c\beta\in\Phi^{+}
. We claim that
\beta\in M
. Suppose to the contrary that
\beta\not\in M
.
For
\gamma\in L
, we have

(2.29.1)

B(s_{\beta}(\alpha),\gamma)=B(\alpha-c\beta,\gamma)=B(\alpha,\gamma)-cB(\beta,\gamma).

Note that
B(\alpha,\gamma)\neq 0
for only
finitely many
\gamma\in L
since
\alpha\in U
. Since

\beta\in\Pi
but
\beta\not\in M
, we have
\beta\not\in U
.
That is,
B(\beta,\gamma)\neq 0
for infinitely many

\gamma\in L\subseteq\Pi
. But
B(\beta,\gamma)\leq 0
for all

\gamma\in\Pi\setminus\{\beta\}
. Hence

B(\beta,\gamma)<0
for infinitely many
\gamma\in L
. By
(
2.29.1
), it follows that

K:=\{\,\gamma\in L\mid B(s_{\beta}(\alpha),\gamma)>0\,\}
is
infinite. Then
s_{\beta}(\alpha)\in\Phi^{+}\cap C_{K}
. We
have
J:=\{\,\gamma\in K\mid B(s_{\beta}(\alpha),\gamma)=0\,\}={\emptyset}
. Proposition
2.29

implies that
|W_{K}|=[W_{K}\colon W_{J}]
is finite,
contrary to the fact that
K\subseteq W_{K}
is infinite. This
contradiction shows that
\beta\in M
as claimed.

Since
\alpha\in U\cap\Phi^{+}
and
\beta\in M=U\cap\Pi
with
\ell(s_{\alpha})\geq 3
, it follows that
s_{\beta}(\alpha)\in\Phi^{+}\cap U
. By induction,
s_{\beta}(\alpha)\in\Phi_{M}
. Then since
\beta\in M
, we get
\alpha\in\Phi_{M}
as required to complete the proof of (b). ∎

Proposition 2.30
.

Let
J\subseteq\Pi
be such that the components

(J_{i})_{i\in I}
of
J
are all of finite type.

(a)

The restriction of the form
B
to
\operatorname{span}(J)
is positive definite. More precisely,
\operatorname{span}(J)=\bigoplus_{i\in I}\operatorname{span}(J_{i})
is an orthogonal direct sum of finite-dimensional subspaces

\operatorname{span}(J_{i})
, on each of which the restriction of
B
is positive definite.

(b)

J
is linearly independent.

(c)

\operatorname{cone}(\Pi)\cap\operatorname{span}(J)=\operatorname{cone}(J)
,
\Phi_{J}=\Phi\cap\operatorname{span}(J)
and
\Phi^{+}_{J}=\Phi\cap\operatorname{cone}(J)
.

(d)

\operatorname{cone}(\Pi\setminus J)\cap\operatorname{span}(J)=\{0\}
.

Proof.

Note that
\operatorname{span}(J_{i})
and
\operatorname{span}(J_{i^{\prime}})
are orthogonal for
i\neq i^{\prime}
in
I
, since
J_{i}
and
J_{i^{\prime}}
are orthogonal. Parts (a)–(b) then follow from the well-known facts that
for any
i\in I
, finiteness of
W_{J_{i}}
implies that
J_{i}
is linearly independent
and the restriction of
B
to
\operatorname{span}(J_{i})
is positive definite
(see for instance
[
5
]
,
[
33
]
or
[
25
]
).

Parts (c)–(d) follow from Proposition
2.21
.
∎

Proof of relative Tits theorem (Theorem
1.43
).

The assertions of the theorem are independent of the choice of root system, so we may assume
\Pi
is linearly independent. By
2.2
, we may also assume without loss of generality that
(V,B)
is ample for
(\Phi,\Pi)
. That is, every linear function
\operatorname{span}(\Pi)\to\mathbb{R}
is of the form
\alpha\mapsto B(\alpha,v)
for some
v\in V
.
We prove that (i) implies (ii). The argument is essentially the same as that of Tits proof in the case
J={\emptyset}
. For the proof, we may replace
W
by any parabolic subgroup
U
of
W
which contains
W^{\prime}
. Fix a set of left coset representatives
\{w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}\}
, where
n:=[W^{\prime}:W_{J}]
and
w_{1}=1_{W}
, for
W_{J}
in
W^{\prime}
. Thus,

W^{\prime}=w_{1}W_{J}\dot{\cup}\ldots\dot{\cup}w_{n}W_{J}.

We replace
W
if necessary by its standard parabolic subgroup generated by
J
and the (finitely many) simple reflections
s
in
S
for which there exists some
i=1,\ldots,n
such that
s
appears in a reduced expression for
w_{i}
. Thus, without loss of generality, we assume
\corank_{W}(W_{J})<\infty
. We prove the result by induction on this corank.

Choose
\rho_{J}\in C
so
B(\rho_{J},\alpha)=0
for all

\alpha\in J
and
B(\rho_{J},\alpha)>0
for all
\alpha\in\Pi\setminus J
. Then the stabilizer in
W
of
\rho_{J}
is
W_{J}
.
Define

\rho_{J}^{\prime}:=w_{1}\rho_{J}+\dotsb+w_{n}\rho_{J}\in V.

Now
\rho^{\prime}_{J}
is fixed by
W^{\prime}
. For if
w\in W^{\prime}
, there exists a
permutation
\sigma
in the symmetric group
S_{n}
and elements
u_{i}\in W_{J}
such that

ww_{i}=w_{\sigma i}u_{i},\qquad i=1,\ldots,n

Then

w\rho_{J}^{\prime}=w\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}\rho_{J}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}ww_{i}\rho_{J}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{\sigma i}u_{i}\rho_{J}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{\sigma i}\rho_{J}=\rho^{\prime}_{J}

as required. We also have
\rho_{J}^{\prime}\in X
, the Tits cone of
W
on
V
, so the stabilizer
U
of
\rho_{J}^{\prime}
is a parabolic subgroup of
W
with
U{\,\supseteq\,}W^{\prime}{\,\supseteq\,}W_{J}
.
If the corank of
W_{J}
in
U
is strictly less than the corank of
W_{J}
in
W
, the desired conclusion follows by induction.
Suppose it is not strictly less. Then it is equal, so the corank of
U
in
W
is
0
by Lemma
2.11
, which forces
U=W
.
This means
\rho_{J}^{\prime}\in\Pi^{\perp}=C\cap-C\subseteq-X
.
Since
X{\,\supseteq\,}C
is a
W
-stable cone with
\rho_{J}\in X
.

\rho_{J}=w_{1}\rho_{J}=\rho^{\prime}_{J}-(w_{2}\rho_{J}+\dotsb+w_{n}\rho_{J})\in-X

Proposition
2.18
implies
B(\rho_{J},\alpha)>0
for only finitely many
\alpha\in\Phi^{+}
. But since
\Pi
is linearly independent, we have
\Phi^{+}\setminus\Phi_{J}^{+}\subseteq\operatorname{cone}(\Pi)\setminus\operatorname{span}(\Pi_{J})
. By definition of
\rho_{J}
,
B(\rho_{J},\Phi^{+}\setminus\Phi_{J}^{+})\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. Therefore
\Phi^{+}\setminus\Phi_{J}^{+}
is finite.
By Proposition
1.35
, this proves that

[W:W_{J}]<\infty
and therefore that (i) implies (ii).

Now we prove (ii) implies (iii). Assume (ii) holds.
We may write
U=dW_{K}d^{-1}
for some
K\subseteq\Pi
and
d\in W
.
Without loss of generality, we may assume that

d\in W^{K}
. Then
\Pi_{U}=d\Pi_{W_{K}}=dK
.
We have
U{\,\supseteq\,}W^{\prime}{\,\supseteq\,}W_{J}
. Hence

J\subseteq\Pi_{U}=dK
,

L:=d^{-1}J\subseteq K
and
d^{-1}W_{J}d\subseteq d^{-1}W^{\prime}d\subseteq d^{-1}Ud=W_{K}
. Taking
w=d^{-1}
, the conditions of (iii) are satisfied.

One has (iii) implies (i), since given (iii),
one has

[W^{\prime}:W_{J}]=[wW^{\prime}w^{-1}:wW_{J}w^{-1}]=[wW^{\prime}w^{-1}:W_{L}]

and

[W_{K}:wW^{\prime}w^{-1}][wW^{\prime}w^{-1}:W_{L}]=[W_{K}:W_{L}]<\infty.

The statement in the theorem concerning existence of
U_{0}
follows directly from Proposition
2.15
(a). The final statement of the theorem is part of (c) of the following proposition.
∎

Proposition 2.31
.

Let
J\subseteq\Pi
and
W^{\prime}{\,\supseteq\,}W_{J}
be a reflection subgroup of
W
such that
[W^{\prime}:W_{J}]
is finite. Let
U_{0}:=\pc(W^{\prime})
(see
2.15
). Then:

(a)

[U_{0}:W_{J}]<\infty
,

(b)

U_{0}
is a parabolic subgroup of
W
.

(c)

\corank_{U_{0}}(W_{J})=\corank_{W^{\prime}}(W_{J})<\infty
.

Proof.

By Theorem
1.43
, there is a finite index overgroup of
W_{J}
which contains
W^{\prime}
and is a parabolic
subgroup of
W
. By replacing
W
by that overgroup, we may assume without loss of generality for the rest of the proof that
[W:W_{J}]<\infty
. This immediately implies (a). Part (b) holds by Proposition
2.15
(c).

Let
E
denote the subspace of
V
consisting of all

v\in(J_{\mathrm{inf}})^{\perp}
such that
B(v,\alpha)\neq 0
for only
finitely many
\alpha\in J_{\mathrm{fin}}
. By Proposition
1.41
,

E\cap\Phi=\Phi_{M}
is a standard parabolic subsystem
of
\Phi
where
M:=E\cap\Pi
.
We have
J_{\mathrm{fin}}\subseteq M
,
J_{\mathrm{inf}}\cap M={\emptyset}
and

\Pi\setminus J\subseteq M
by Proposition
1.35
. Hence
M=J_{\mathrm{fin}}\,\dot{\cup}\,(\Pi\setminus J)=\Pi\setminus J_{\inf}
.
Proposition
1.35

shows further now that
M=\Pi_{\mathrm{fin}}
and
\Pi_{\mathrm{inf}}=J_{\mathrm{inf}}
.

Let
K
denote the union of those components of
\Pi_{W^{\prime}}
which contain a root in
\Pi_{W^{\prime}}\setminus J
.
Note that
K
is of finite type and
K\subseteq\Phi_{M}
, by Proposition
1.35
. Hence
\Phi_{K}\subseteq\Phi_{M}
. Choose a finite union of components
N
of
M
so that
\Phi_{N}{\,\supseteq\,}\Phi_{K}\cup(\Pi\setminus J)
. We have
N\perp(M\setminus N)
and
N\subseteq M\perp(J\setminus M)
so
N\perp(J\setminus N)
.

Let
L:=K\cup(N\cap J)\subseteq\Pi_{W^{\prime}}
, so
W_{L}
is a finite reflection subgroup of
W_{N}
with
\Pi_{W_{L}}=L
. Consider the parabolic closure
\pc(L)\subseteq W_{N}
of
L
.
It is a finite parabolic subgroup of
W_{N}
, and we write
P:=\Pi_{\pc(L)}{\,\supseteq\,}N\cap J
for its canonical simple system.
Let
Q=P\cup(J\setminus N)
. We have
Q{\,\supseteq\,}(N\cap J)\cup(J\setminus N)=J
and
W_{Q}{\,\supseteq\,}W_{P}{\,\supseteq\,}W_{L}{\,\supseteq\,}W_{K}{\,\supseteq\,}K{\,\supseteq\,}\Pi_{W^{\prime}}\setminus J
,
so
W_{Q}{\,\supseteq\,}\Pi_{W^{\prime}}
. We claim that
U_{0}:=W_{Q}
is a parabolic subgroup
and that
\corank_{U_{0}}(W_{J})=\corank_{W^{\prime}}(W_{J})
.

Since
P=\Pi_{\pc(L)}
is parabolic in
W_{N}
, there exists
d\in W_{N}
with
dP\subseteq N
. We therefore have

d(N\cap J)\subseteq N
. Since
d\in W_{N}
where
N\perp(J\setminus N)
, we have
d(J\setminus N)=J\setminus N\subseteq\Pi
.
It follows that
dQ\subseteq\Pi
, which implies that
W_{Q}
is parabolic and
\Pi_{W_{Q}}=Q
.

By definition of
K
, we have
\corank_{W^{\prime}}(W_{J})=|\Pi_{W^{\prime}}\setminus J|=|K\setminus(K\cap J)|
. Note that since
N{\,\supseteq\,}P{\,\supseteq\,}J\cap N
, we have
J\cap N{\,\supseteq\,}J\cap P{\,\supseteq\,}J\cap N
and so
J\cap N=J\cap P
. Using
Q=P\cup(J\setminus N)
,

|P|=\rank(\pc(L))=|\Pi_{W_{L}}|=|L|
,
L:=K\cup(N\cap J)
, we find

\begin{split}&\corank_{U_{0}}(W_{J})=|\Pi_{U_{0}}\setminus J|=|Q\setminus J|=|((P\cup(J\setminus N))\setminus J)|=|P\setminus(J\cap P)|\\
=&|P|-|J\cap P|=|L|-|J\cap N|=(|K|+|N\cap J|-|K\cap(N\cap J)|)-|J\cap N|\\
=&|K|-|(K\cap J)\cap N)|=|K|-|K\cap J|=\corank_{W^{\prime}}(W_{J})\end{split}

since
W_{K}\subseteq W_{N}
where
N\subseteq\Pi
implies
K\cap J\subseteq N
.

To complete the proof, it suffices to show that
U_{0}=\pc(W^{\prime})
.
But since
W_{L}\subseteq W^{\prime}
, we have
\pc(W^{\prime}){\,\supseteq\,}\pc(W_{L})=W_{P}
. Also,
\pc(W^{\prime}){\,\supseteq\,}J\setminus N
, so
\pc(W^{\prime}){\,\supseteq\,}W_{Q}{\,\supseteq\,}W^{\prime}
. We have
\pc(W^{\prime})=W_{Q}
since
W_{Q}
is parabolic.
∎

2.32.
Maximal corank
k
reflection overgroups

We next prove Theorem
1.30
, which generalizes facts about rank
k
reflection subgroups in
[
17
]
. For convenience, subsections
2.33
–
2.38
recall without proof the definitions and technical facts from
[
17
]
concerning “lifts” of root systems, which are used in the proofs here.

2.33.

The category
C
of lifts of root systems has as objects the quadruples
D=(V_{0},B_{0},\Phi_{0},\Pi_{0})
where
(\Phi_{0},\Pi_{0})
is a based
root system for the real quadratic space
(V_{0},B_{0})
. A morphism

L\colon D_{1}\to D_{2}
in
C
, where

D_{i}=(V_{i},B_{i},\Phi_{i},\Pi_{i})
, is by definition a morphism

L\colon(V_{1},B_{1})\to(V_{2},B_{2})
of quadratic spaces (that is, a
\mathbb{R}
-linear map
L\colon V_{1}\to V_{2}
which satisfies
B_{2}(L(u),L(v))=B_{1}(u,v)
for all
u
and
v
in
V_{1}
) such that the function
L\colon V_{1}\to V_{2}
restricts to a bijection
\Pi_{1}\to\Pi_{2}
. Composition of morphisms is given by composition of the underlying linear maps. We call
D_{1}
above (or more precisely, the pair
(D_{1},L)
) a lift of
D_{2}
.

Consider a morphism
L\colon D_{1}\to D_{2}
in
C
as above.
Let
(W_{i},S_{i})
and
\Phi_{i}^{+}

denote respectively the Coxeter system and positive roots attached to

D_{i}
, for
i=1,2
. The map defined by
s_{\alpha}\mapsto s_{L(\alpha)}\colon S_{1}\to S_{2}
,
for
\alpha\in\Pi_{1}
, is a bijection which extends uniquely to a group
isomorphism
\theta\colon W_{1}\to W_{2}
. That is,
\theta\colon(W_{1},S_{1})\to(W_{2},S_{2})
is an isomorphism of Coxeter systems.
Unless otherwise specified, we identify
(W_{2},S_{2})=(W_{1},S_{1})
by means of
\theta
, so
\theta=\mathrm{Id}_{W_{1}}
.
One then has
L(w(u))=w(L(u))
for all
w\in W_{1}
and

u\in V_{1}
. Further,
L
restricts to bijections
\Phi_{1}\to\Phi_{2}
and

\Phi_{1}^{+}\to\Phi_{2}^{+}
satisfying
s_{L(\alpha)}=s_{\alpha}
for
all
\alpha\in\Phi_{1}
.

2.34.

For any fixed object
D=(V,B,\Phi,\Pi)
of
C
, there exists an object

\widetilde{D}=(\widetilde{V},\widetilde{B},\widetilde{\Phi},\widetilde{\Pi})
of
C
and a morphism

L\colon\widetilde{D}\to D
such that
\widetilde{\Pi}
is a
\mathbb{R}
-basis of
\widetilde{V}
. The pair
(\widetilde{D},L)
is uniquely determined by
D
up to isomorphism as an object of the category of objects of
C
over
D
; we call
\widetilde{D}
(with
L
understood), the universal lift of
D
, and call
\widetilde{\Phi}
a universal lift of
\Phi
. A universal lift of a lift of
D
is a universal lift of
D
.

2.35.

In the following, consider a based root system

(\Phi,\Pi)
, with associated Coxeter system
(W,S)
, in a quadratic space
(V,B)
.
Note that
D=(V,B,\Phi,\Pi)
is an object of the category
C
.
For any reflection subgroup
W^{\prime}
of
W
, there is an object

D_{W^{\prime}}:=(V,B,\Phi_{W^{\prime}},\Pi_{W^{\prime}})
of
C
.

Consider a lift
(D^{\prime},L)
of
D_{W^{\prime}}
where

D^{\prime}=(V^{\prime},B^{\prime},\Psi,\Delta)
.
A reflection subgroup
U
of
W^{\prime}

determines from
D
an object
D_{U}=(V,B,\Phi_{U},\Pi_{U})
of
C
, in the same way as
D_{W^{\prime}}
is determined from
D
by
W^{\prime}
.
Also,
U
determines from
D^{\prime}
an object
D^{\prime}_{U}=(V^{\prime},B^{\prime},\Psi_{U},\Delta_{U})
of
C
in the same way as
D_{W^{\prime}}
is determined from
D
by
W^{\prime}
, We have
\Psi_{U}\subseteq\Psi
and
\Delta_{U}\subseteq\Psi^{+}
,
and
(D^{\prime}_{U},L)
is a lift of
D_{U}

To lighten notation and terminology, we shall say simply that the root system
\Psi
is a lift
of
\Phi_{W^{\prime}}
, and that
\Psi_{U}
is the
induced lift
of
\Phi_{U}
. If the simple roots of
\Psi
are denoted
\Delta
,
we denote the simple roots of
\Psi_{U}
as
\Delta_{U}
, as above.

Lemma 2.36
.

Let
\Psi
be a lift, with simple roots
\Delta
, of
\Phi
, and let
U\subseteq U^{\prime}
be reflection subgroups of
W
.

(a)

If
\Pi_{U}
is linearly independent, then
\Delta_{U}
is linearly independent.

(b)

If
\Phi_{U}=\Phi\cap\operatorname{span}(\Phi_{U})
, then

\Psi_{U}=\Psi\cap\operatorname{span}(\Psi_{U})
.

(c)

If
\Psi_{U^{\prime}}\subseteq\operatorname{span}(\Psi_{U})
, then
\Phi_{U^{\prime}}\subseteq\operatorname{span}(\Phi_{U})
.

2.37.

Let
\mathcal{W}
denote the (inclusion-ordered) complete lattice of reflection subgroups of
W
. The join of a subset
X=\{\,W_{i}\mid i\in I\,\}
of
\mathcal{W}
is denoted as
\bigvee X
or
\bigvee_{i\in I}W_{i}
.
For any subset
P
of
\mathcal{W}
such that
P
has a maximum element (in the induced order by inclusion), we denote that maximum element by
\max(P)
.

For any reflection subgroup
U
of
W
, say that
W
is
\Phi
-spanned by
U
if

\Phi\subseteq\operatorname{span}(\Phi_{U})
. Since
\Phi=\Phi_{W}
,
W
is trivially
\Phi
-spanned by
W
.
Introduce a relation
\leq
on
\mathcal{W}
be letting
U\leq W^{\prime}
, for
U,W^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}
, if
U\subseteq W^{\prime}
and for every lift
\Psi
of
\Phi_{W^{\prime}}
,
W^{\prime}
is
\Psi
-spanned by
U
(that is,
\Psi\subseteq\operatorname{span}(\Psi_{U})
). Equivalently by Lemma
2.36
,
U\leq W^{\prime}
if
U\subseteq W^{\prime}
and
W^{\prime}
is
\Psi
-spanned by
U
where
\Psi
is a universal lift of
\Phi_{W^{\prime}}
.

Define a map
c\colon\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{W}
as follows.
For each reflection subgroup
U
of
W
, let
P_{U}:=\{W^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}\mid U\leq W^{\prime}\}
and
c(U):=\bigvee_{W^{\prime}\in P_{U}}W^{\prime}
.

Lemma 2.38
.

(a)

The relation
\leq
is a partial order on
\mathcal{W}
.

(b)

c
is a closure operator on
\mathcal{W}
.

(c)

If
U\in\mathcal{W}
, then
c(U)=\max(P_{U})
.

Proposition 2.39
.

Let
J\subseteq\Pi
and
U\in\mathcal{W}
with
U{\,\supseteq\,}W_{J}
. Assume
\corank_{U}(W_{J})=k\in\mathbb{N}
and that
\Pi_{U}
is linearly independent. Define

P_{U}^{\prime}:=\{W^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}\mid W^{\prime}\supseteq U,\corank_{W^{\prime}}(W_{J})=k\}.

Then

(a)

For any
W^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}
with
W^{\prime}\supseteq U
, one has
\corank_{W^{\prime}}(W_{J})\geq\corank_{U}(W_{J})
.

(b)

P_{U}=P^{\prime}_{U}
, and
c(U)=\max(P_{U}^{\prime})
.

(c)

If
W^{\prime}\in P_{U}
, then
\Pi_{W^{\prime}}
is linearly independent.

Proof.

We prove (a). Let
W^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}
with
W^{\prime}\supseteq U
.
Then
\operatorname{span}(\Pi_{U})\subseteq\operatorname{span}(\Phi_{W^{\prime}})=\operatorname{span}(\Pi_{W^{\prime}})
. Since
\Pi_{U}
is linearly independent by assumption,
\Pi_{U}{\,\supseteq\,}J
and

\Pi_{W^{\prime}}{\,\supseteq\,}J
imply

\begin{split}\corank_{U}(W_{J})&=|\Pi_{U}\setminus J|=\dim(\operatorname{span}(\Pi_{U})/\operatorname{span}(J))\\
&\leq\dim(\operatorname{span}(\Pi_{W^{\prime}})/\operatorname{span}(J))\leq|\Pi_{W^{\prime}}\setminus J|=\corank_{W^{\prime}}(W_{J}).\end{split}

In the rest of the proof, for a reflection subgroup denoted
W^{\prime}
, we denote a universal lift of
\Phi_{W^{\prime}}
as
\Psi
, and the (linearly independent) set of simple roots of
\Psi
as
\Delta
.

We prove (b). We first show that
P_{U}\subseteq P^{\prime}_{U}
. Let
W^{\prime}\in P_{U}
. Then
U\leq W^{\prime}
.
We have
U\subseteq W^{\prime}
and
\Psi_{U}\subseteq\Psi\subseteq\operatorname{span}(\Psi_{U})
, so

\operatorname{span}(\Psi)=\operatorname{span}(\Psi_{U})
.
We may identify the induced lift
\Psi_{W_{J}}
of
\Phi_{J}

with
\Phi_{J}
since
J\subseteq\Pi_{U}
is linearly independent.
In particular, we make the identification
J=\Pi_{W_{J}}=\Delta_{W_{J}}
, so

J\subseteq\Delta_{U}
also.
Since
\Delta
and
\Delta_{U}
are linearly independent and both contain
J
, we have

\begin{split}\corank_{W^{\prime}}(W_{J})&=|\Delta\setminus J|=\dim(\operatorname{span}(\Delta)/\operatorname{span}(J))=\dim(\operatorname{span}(\Psi)/\operatorname{span}(J))\\
&=\dim(\operatorname{span}(\Psi_{U})/\operatorname{span}(J))=\dim(\operatorname{span}(\Delta_{U})/\operatorname{span}(J))\\
&=|\Delta_{U}\setminus J|=\corank_{U}(W_{J})=k.\end{split}

Hence
W^{\prime}\in P^{\prime}_{U}
, which shows that

P_{U}\subseteq P^{\prime}_{U}
.

To prove the reverse inclusion
P^{\prime}_{U}\subseteq P_{U}
, let

W^{\prime}\in P^{\prime}_{U}
. That is,
W^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}
,
U\subseteq W^{\prime}

and
\corank_{W^{\prime}}(W_{J})=k
. To prove that
W^{\prime}\in P_{U}
(that is,
U\leq W^{\prime}
) it will suffice to show that
W^{\prime}
is
\Psi
-spanned by
{U}
,
since
\Psi
is a universal lift of
\Phi_{W^{\prime}}
.
Since
\Pi_{U}
is linearly independent, it follows that
\Delta_{U}
is linearly independent. Let
\pi\colon\operatorname{span}(\Delta)\to\operatorname{span}(\Delta)/\operatorname{span}(J)
be the canonical map. Note that

\operatorname{span}(\Delta)/\operatorname{span}(J)=\operatorname{span}(\pi(\Delta\setminus J))

has basis
\pi(\Delta\setminus J)
and dimension
k
, since
\Delta
is linearly independent,
\Delta{\,\supseteq\,}J
and
|\Delta\setminus J|=\corank_{W^{\prime}}(W_{J})=k
.
Since
\Delta_{U}{\,\supseteq\,}J
is linearly independent, and

|\Delta_{U}\setminus J|=\corank_{U}(W_{J})=k
, it follows that

\pi(\Delta_{U}\setminus J)
is another basis for
\operatorname{span}(\Delta)/\operatorname{span}(J)
.
In particular,
\pi(\Psi)\subseteq\operatorname{span}(\pi(\Delta_{U}\setminus J))
, which implies

\Psi\subseteq\operatorname{span}(\Delta_{U}\setminus J)+\ker\pi=\operatorname{span}(\Delta_{U}\setminus J)+\operatorname{span}(J)=\operatorname{span}(\Delta_{U})\subseteq\operatorname{span}(\Psi_{U}).

Thus,
W^{\prime}
is
\Psi
-spanned by
U
as claimed.
This shows that
P_{U}=P_{U}^{\prime}
. Then
c(U)=\max(P^{\prime}_{U})
follows from Proposition
2.38
(c), completing the proof of (b).

Finally, we prove (c). Let
W^{\prime}\in P_{U}
.
Since
\Phi_{W^{\prime}}
is (trivially) a lift of
\Phi_{W^{\prime}}
, it follows by definition of
P_{U}
that

\operatorname{span}(\Phi_{U})=\operatorname{span}(\Phi_{W^{\prime}})
. That is,
\operatorname{span}(\Pi_{U})=\operatorname{span}(\Pi_{W^{\prime}})
. Let
\pi\colon\operatorname{span}(\Pi)\to\operatorname{span}(\Pi)/\operatorname{span}(J)
be the projection.
We have

\operatorname{span}(\pi(\Pi_{U}\setminus J))=\operatorname{span}(\Pi_{U})/\operatorname{span}(J)=\operatorname{span}(\Pi_{W^{\prime}})/\operatorname{span}(J)=\operatorname{span}(\pi(\Pi_{W^{\prime}}\setminus J))

But since
\Pi_{U}{\,\supseteq\,}J
is linearly independent,
\pi(\Pi_{U}\setminus J)
is linearly independent, of cardinality
|\Pi_{U}\setminus J|=\corank_{U}(W_{J})=k
. Also,
\Pi_{W^{\prime}}\setminus J
has cardinality
|\Pi_{W^{\prime}}\setminus J|=\corank_{W^{\prime}}(W_{J})=k

since
W^{\prime}\in P^{\prime}_{U}
by (b). It follows that
\pi(\Pi_{W^{\prime}}\setminus J)
is linearly independent and hence

(\Pi_{W^{\prime}}\setminus J)\cup J=\Pi_{W^{\prime}}
is linearly independent since
J
, as a subset of the linearly independent set
\Pi_{U}
, is a basis of
\operatorname{span}(J)=\ker\pi
.
∎

Theorem 2.40
.

Let
J\subseteq\Delta
and
U{\,\supseteq\,}W_{J}
be a reflection subgroup of
W
. Assume that
\corank_{U}(W_{J})=k\in\mathbb{N}
and that
\Pi_{U}
is linearly independent.

(a)

The set
P^{\prime}_{U}
of all reflection subgroups
W^{\prime}
of
W
which contain
U
and satisfy
\corank_{W^{\prime}}(W_{J})=k
has a maximum element

c(U)
under inclusion.

(b)

Suppose further that
\Phi_{U}=\Phi\cap\operatorname{span}(\Phi_{U})
.
Then
c(U)=U
. Let
I
be the set of all reflection subgroups which are inclusion maximal elements of the set of all reflection subgroups
W^{\prime}
of
W
which contain
U
and satisfy
\corank_{W^{\prime}}(W_{J})=k+1
. Then
\Phi\setminus\Phi_{U}=\dot{\bigcup}_{W^{\prime}\in I}(\Phi_{W^{\prime}}\setminus\Phi_{U})
.

Proof.

Theorem
2.40
(a) follows directly from Propositions
2.39
(b) and
2.38
(c).

We prove Theorem
2.40
(b). Fix
U\in\mathcal{W}

such that
\corank_{U}(W_{J})=k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\Pi_{U}
is linearly independent and
\Phi_{U}=\Phi\cap\operatorname{span}(\Phi_{U})
.
We show first that
c(U)=U
. To see this, let
W^{\prime}\in P_{U}
. Then
U\leq W^{\prime}
, so in particular,
U\subseteq W^{\prime}
and
U

\Phi
-spans
W
. By assumption, this implies
\Phi_{W^{\prime}}\subseteq\operatorname{span}(\Phi_{U})\cap\Phi=\Phi_{U}
and so

W^{\prime}\subseteq U\subseteq W^{\prime}
, showing
W^{\prime}=U
.
Thus,
P_{U}=\{U\}
and
c(U)=\max(P_{U})=U
as required.

We claim that for any
\alpha\in\Phi\setminus\Phi_{U}
,
the reflection subgroup
W^{\prime}:=\langle U\cup\{s_{\alpha}\}\rangle
satisfies
\corank_{W^{\prime}}(W_{J})=k+1
.
By Proposition
2.39
(a),
\corank_{W^{\prime}}(W_{J})\geq k
.
The inequality must be strict, since
W^{\prime}\supsetneq U
and
Proposition
2.39
(b) guarantees that
U
is an inclusion maximal refelction subgroup with
\corank_{U}(W_{J})=k
. Also,

W^{\prime}=\langle\chi(U)\cup\{s_{\alpha}\}\rangle=\langle\{\,s_{\beta}\mid\beta\in J\,\}\cup(\chi(U)\setminus\{\,s_{\beta}\mid\beta\in J\,\})\cup\{s_{\alpha}\}\rangle,

so

\corank_{W^{\prime}}(W_{J})\leq|(\chi(U)\setminus\{\,s_{\beta}\mid\beta\in J\,\})|+1=\corank_{U}(W_{J})+1=k+1,

proving the claim.

We claim that for any reflection subgroup
W^{\prime}\supseteq U
such that
\corank_{W^{\prime}}(W_{J})=k+1
, the set
\Pi_{W^{\prime}}
is linearly independent.
For we have

\operatorname{span}(\Pi_{W^{\prime}})=\operatorname{span}(\Phi_{W^{\prime}})\supseteq\operatorname{span}(\Phi_{U})=\operatorname{span}(\Pi_{U}).

Moreover, the containment
\operatorname{span}(\Phi_{W^{\prime}})\supseteq\operatorname{span}(\Phi_{U})
is strict. For otherwise, we would have

\Phi_{W^{\prime}}\subseteq\Phi\cap\operatorname{span}(\Phi_{U})=\Phi_{U}

and so
W^{\prime}\subseteq U\subseteq W^{\prime}
, which would imply that
W^{\prime}=U
contrary to
\corank_{W^{\prime}}(W_{J})=k+1>k=\corank_{U}(W_{J})
.
Since
J\subseteq\Pi_{U}
and
J\subseteq\Pi_{W^{\prime}}
, it follows that

\operatorname{span}(\Pi_{W^{\prime}})/\operatorname{span}(J)\supsetneq\operatorname{span}(\Pi_{U})/\operatorname{span}(J).

Noting that
\Pi_{U}{\,\supseteq\,}J
is linearly independent, we have

\dim(\operatorname{span}(\Pi_{U})/\operatorname{span}(J))=|\Pi_{U}\setminus J|=\corank_{U}(W_{J})=k.

Hence
\dim\operatorname{span}(\Pi_{W^{\prime}})/\operatorname{span}(J)>k
. On the other hand,

k+1=\corank_{W^{\prime}}(W_{J})=|\Pi_{W^{\prime}}\setminus J|\geq\dim(\operatorname{span}(\Pi_{W^{\prime}})/\operatorname{span}(J))>k.

It follows that

\dim(\operatorname{span}(\Pi_{W^{\prime}})/\operatorname{span}(J))=|\Pi_{W^{\prime}}\setminus J|=k+1\in\mathbb{N}.

Now since
J\subseteq\Pi_{W^{\prime}}
is linearly independent, it follows that
\Pi_{W^{\prime}}
is linearly independent as claimed.

From the two claims above, the definition of
I
and Proposition
2.39
(b), it follows directly that for each
\alpha\in\Phi\setminus\Phi_{U}
, there is a unique element
W^{\prime}\in I

such that
\langle U\cup\{s_{\alpha}\}\rangle\subseteq W^{\prime}
, or equivalently by definition of
I
, such that
\alpha\in\Phi_{W^{\prime}}
. This is what we were required to prove.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.30
.

Theorem
1.30
(b)–(c) follow from Theorem
2.40
(a)–(b) respectively. To prove Theorem
1.30
(a), there is no loss of generality in assuming that
\Pi
is linearly independent, by taking a universal lift of
\Phi
, for instance. Then
\Phi_{U}
is linearly independent and
\Phi_{U}=\Phi\cap\operatorname{span}(\Phi_{U})
by
Proposition
2.21
, so Theorem
1.30
(a) follows from Theorem
2.40
(b).∎

2.41.

The following proposition constructs maximal rank
k
reflection overgroups under purely group-theoretic assumptions which are more general than the conditions in Theorem
1.30
(b). However, the resulting maximal rank
k
reflection overgroups are not known to be unique, as in Theorem
1.30
(b).

Proposition 2.42
.

Assume that
J\subseteq\Pi
and that
U
is a reflection overgroup of
W_{J}
such that
\corank_{U}(W_{J})=k\in\mathbb{N}
. Assume also that there is no reflection overgroup
U^{\prime}
of
U
with
\corank_{U^{\prime}}(W_{J})<k
(this holds for instance if
\Pi_{U}
is linearly independent or if
k=1
). Then there are only finitely many reflection overgroups
U^{\prime}
of
U
such that
\corank_{U^{\prime}}(W_{J})=k
.
In particular,
U
is contained in some inclusion maximal corank
k
reflection overgroup of
W_{J}

Proof.

Let
U
be any subgroup of
W
. Let
Q_{U}
denote the set of all reflection subgroups
V
of
W
such
that
V
contains
U
and
U
is not contained in any proper standard parabolic subgroup

\langle\,K\,\rangle
, where
K{\,\subsetneq\,}\chi(V)
, of
V
.
An argument similar to that in the proof of
[
21
, Proposition 1]
shows that if
U=\langle\,W_{J}\cup X\,\rangle
for some finite subset
X
of
W
, then
Q_{U}
is finite. Now suppose that
U
is a reflection overgroup of
W_{J}
satisfying the assumptions of the proposition. Then
U=\langle\,W_{J}\cup X\,\rangle
for some finite subset
X
of
T
, so
Q_{U}
is finite. We claim that any reflection overgroup
U^{\prime}
of
U
such that
\corank_{U^{\prime}}(W_{J})=k
is an element of
Q_{U}
. For otherwise,
U^{\prime}
would have a proper standard parabolic subgroup
U^{\prime\prime}
, where
\Pi_{U^{\prime\prime}}{\,\subsetneq\,}\Pi_{U^{\prime}}
, such that
U\subseteq U^{\prime\prime}
and we would have

k=\corank_{U^{\prime}}(W_{J})=|\Pi_{U^{\prime}}\setminus J|=|\Pi_{U^{\prime}}\setminus\Pi_{U^{\prime\prime}}|+|\Pi_{U^{\prime\prime}}\setminus J|>|\Pi_{U^{\prime\prime}}\setminus J|=\corank_{U^{\prime\prime}}(W_{J}),

contrary to the assumptions.
∎

2.43.
Root coefficients

We end this section with some facts we shall have need of concerning root coefficients.

Proposition 2.44
.

(a)

Suppose that
\alpha
,
\beta
and
\gamma
are in
\Phi
and
\gamma-\beta\in\operatorname{span}(\alpha)
. Then either

\gamma=\beta
or
\gamma=s_{\alpha}(\beta)
. Hence

\Phi\cap(\beta+\operatorname{span}(\{\alpha\})=W_{\{\alpha\}}\beta
.

(b)

If
W
is finite,
J\subseteq\Pi
and
\beta\in\Phi\setminus\Phi_{J}
, then
W\beta\cap(\beta+\operatorname{span}(J))=W_{J}\beta
.

(c)

Assume
W
is finite and irreducible. Let
\alpha\in\Pi
and
J:=\Pi\setminus\{\alpha\}
. Then for any
\beta\in\Phi\setminus\Phi_{J}
, we have
\Phi\cap(\beta+\operatorname{span}(J))=W_{J}\beta
.

Remark
.

Proposition
2.44
(b) also holds if
\Phi

is a reduced, crystallographic root system of a finite Weyl
group, which is not in general a based root system in the sense
of this paper since roots may have unequal lengths; see

[
5
]
,
[
35
]
and
[
18
]
. However, (a) (and
therefore (c)) does
not
hold in general for non-simply-laced
(reduced) crystallographic root systems of finite Weyl groups,
because such root systems contain root strings of length at
least
3
(see
[
5
]
or
[
30
]
).

Proof.

The second assertion of (a) follows immediately
from the first, the
proof of which uses only the facts that
\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in V
,
B(\beta,\beta)=B(\gamma,\gamma)
,

B(\alpha,\alpha)\neq 0
and
\gamma-\beta\in\operatorname{span}(\alpha)
.
Write
\gamma=\beta+c\alpha
, where
c\in\mathbb{R}
.
Then

\begin{split}0&=-B(\beta,\beta)+B(\gamma,\gamma)=-B(\beta,\beta)+B(\beta+c\alpha,\beta+c\alpha)\\
&=-B(\beta,\beta)+B(\beta,\beta)+2cB(\beta,\alpha)+c^{2}B(\alpha,\alpha)=c(cB(\alpha,\alpha)+2B(\beta,\alpha)).\end{split}

Hence either
c=0
, in which case
\gamma=\beta
, or
c=-\frac{2B(\beta,\alpha)}{B(\alpha,\alpha)}
,
in which case,
\gamma=s_{\alpha}(\beta)
.

Part (b) is a special case of
[
18
, Proposition 1.4(a)]
.

We prove (c). Let assumptions be as there. If
|\Pi|\leq 1
, the result is trivial and if
|\Pi|=2
, the desired conclusion follows from (a).
Assume
|\Pi|>2
.

The inclusion
W_{J}\beta\subseteq\Phi\cap(\beta+\operatorname{span}(J))
holds since for any
v\in V
and
\gamma\in J
,
s_{\gamma}v-v\in\operatorname{span}(J)
. To prove the reverse inclusion, let

\gamma\in\Phi\cap(\beta+\operatorname{span}(J))
. If
\gamma\in W\beta
, the result follows from (b). Suppose
\gamma\not\in W\beta
.
It will suffice to show that
W\gamma\cap(\beta+\operatorname{span}(J))={\emptyset}
.
Note that
\Phi
contains at least two
W
-orbits of roots, so
in virtue of our assumptions,
\Phi
must be of type
B_{n}

(equivalently,
C_{n}
) for
n>2
, or of type
F_{4}
, and there
are exactly two
W
-orbits of roots, namely
W\beta
and

W\gamma
.

Define
\dot{\rho}:=\rho
if
\rho\in W\beta
and

\dot{\rho}:=\sqrt{2}\rho
if
\rho\in W\gamma
, so in either case

\dot{\rho}=\|\dot{\rho}\|\rho
. Then
\Psi:=\{\,\dot{\rho}\mid\rho\in\Phi\,\}
is a crystallographic root system of
W
in
\operatorname{span}(\Pi)
, with simple roots

\Delta:=\{\,\dot{\rho}\mid\rho\in\Pi\,\}
.
Since
\Psi
is crystallographic, for any
\rho\in\Psi
, there are integers
(n_{\tau,\rho})_{\tau\in\Pi}
such that
\dot{\rho}=\sum_{\tau\in\Pi}n_{\tau,\rho}\dot{\tau}
.
Hence
\rho=\sum_{\tau\in\Pi}c_{\tau,\rho}\tau
where

c_{\tau,\rho}=\frac{\|\dot{\tau}\|}{\|\dot{\rho}\|}n_{\tau,\rho}\in\frac{\|\dot{\tau}\|}{\|\dot{\rho}\|}\mathbb{Z}
.

Suppose
that
\delta:=w\gamma\in W\gamma\cap(\beta+\operatorname{span}(J))

where
w\in W
.
Note
c_{\alpha,\beta}\neq 0
since
\beta\in\operatorname{span}(J\cup\{\alpha\})\setminus\operatorname{span}(J)
. Also,
c_{\alpha,\delta}=c_{\alpha,\beta}
since
\delta\in\beta+\operatorname{span}(J)
.
Hence
c_{\alpha,\delta}\in\frac{\|\dot{\alpha}\|}{\|\dot{\beta}\|}\mathbb{\mathbb{Z}}\setminus\{0\}={\|\dot{\alpha}\|}\mathbb{\mathbb{Z}}\setminus\{0\}
. But also,

c_{\alpha,\delta}\in\frac{\|\dot{\alpha}\|}{\|\dot{\delta}\|}\mathbb{\mathbb{Z}}=\frac{\|\dot{\alpha}\|}{\|\dot{\gamma}\|}\mathbb{\mathbb{Z}}=\frac{\|\dot{\alpha}\|}{\sqrt{2}}\mathbb{\mathbb{Z}}
. This would imply that
\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\|\dot{\alpha}\|}c_{\alpha,\delta}\in\mathbb{Z}\cap\sqrt{2}(\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\})={\emptyset}
, a contradiction which completes the proof of (c).
∎

2.45.

We call a linear map
\height
as in (a) in the following proposition a
height function
on
V
for
(\Phi,\Pi)
.

Proposition 2.46
.

Assume that
(W,S)
is of finite rank.

(a)

There exists a linear function
\height\colon V\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\height(\alpha)>0
for all
\alpha\in\Pi
.

(b)

For any function
\height
as in (a), there exists a positive constant
\epsilon
such that

\height(\alpha)>\epsilon\ell(s_{\alpha})
for all
\alpha\in\Phi^{+}
.

Proof.

We sketch a proof. Part (a) follows from basic facts
about duals of polyhedral cones; see
[
25
, Lemma 4.2(d)]
for
an equivalent result.
Part (b) holds on taking
\epsilon:=\epsilon_{1}\epsilon_{2}

where
\epsilon_{1}>0
is chosen so that
0<\epsilon_{1}<\height(\alpha)
for all
\alpha\in\Pi
, and
\epsilon_{2}
is
chosen so
0<\epsilon_{2}<1
and

\epsilon_{2}<|B(\alpha,\beta)|
and for all pairs
(\alpha,\beta)
of non-orthogonal simple roots such that there is a (rank
two) finite standard parabolic root subsystem of
\Phi
containing
both
\alpha
and
\beta
. (This implies that
\epsilon_{2}<|B(\alpha,\beta)|
for any non-orthogonal roots
\alpha
and

\beta
; see
[
14
, Lemma 1.22(1)]
.) The above more
precise version of (b) can be proved by induction on

\ell(s_{\alpha})
, in a similar manner to the proof of its special
case
[
14
, Lemma 1.22(2)]
. ∎

3.
Brink-Howlett groupoids as signed groupoid-sets

Subsections
3.1
–
3.9
provide more details concerning general signed groupoid sets, and especially the notions of real and imaginary roots, realification and compression. The rest of Section
3
provides a proof of Theorem
1.22
.

3.1.

Let
(G,X)
be a signed
G
-set. For any
a\in\operatorname{ob}(G)
,
each root
x\in X(a)
has a sign
\sgn(x)\in\{\pm\}=\{\pm 1\}
, with
\sgn(x)=+
if
x\in X(a)^{+}
or
\sgn(x)=-
if
x\in X(a)^{-}
. We say that
x
is an
imaginary root
if
\sgn(x)=\sgn(gx)
for all
g\in G_{a}:=\bigcup_{b\in\mathrm{Ob}(G)}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{b}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.44pt{b}\kern 6.22598pt{a}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{b}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.44pt{b}\kern 6.22598pt{a}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{b}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-5.4658pt{b}\kern 3.85262pt{a}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{b}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-4.36505pt{b}\kern 2.75186pt{a}}}}
, and that
x
is a
real root
otherwise. We say
(G,X)
is a
real
signed groupoid set if every root of
X
is real.

It is easily seen there is a signed
G
-subset
X^{{\mathrm{re}}}
of
X

such that, for all
a\in\operatorname{ob}(G)
,
X^{{\mathrm{re}}}(a)
is equal to the set
of all real roots of
X(a)
. One necessarily has

X^{{\mathrm{re}},\epsilon}(a)=X^{{\mathrm{re}}}(a)\cap X^{\epsilon}(a)
for

\epsilon\in\{\pm\}
. We call the signed
G
-set
(G,X^{\mathrm{re}})
the
realification
of
(G,X)
. Note

(G,(X^{{\mathrm{re}}})^{{\mathrm{re}}})=(G,X^{{\mathrm{re}}})
. Also,
(G,X)
is real if and only if it is isomorphic to
(G,X^{{\mathrm{re}}})
. We have

(3.1.1)

X^{{\mathrm{re}},+}(a)=\bigcup_{g\in\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.04243pt\kern 5.3202pt}}_{{\kern-7.39249pt{a}\kern 5.3202pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.04243pt\kern 5.3202pt}}_{{\kern-7.39249pt{a}\kern 5.3202pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-2.47409pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-4.29556pt{a}\kern 2.75186pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-1.68785pt\kern 1.96562pt}}_{{\kern-3.50932pt{a}\kern 1.96562pt}}}}X_{g},\qquad g\in\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-5.81378pt{a}\kern 3.85262pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-4.71303pt{a}\kern 2.75186pt}}}\implies X^{{\mathrm{re}}}_{g}=X_{g}.

Similarly, there is a signed
G
-set
(G,X^{{\mathrm{im}}})
such that for each
a\in\operatorname{ob}(G)
,
X^{{\mathrm{im}}}(a)=X(a)\setminus X^{{\mathrm{re}}}(a)
is the set of all imaginary roots in
X(a)
.

3.2.

Consider a morphism
\nu\colon(G,X)\mapsto(G,Y)
of signed
G
-sets. For all
a\in\operatorname{ob}(G)
, the component

\nu_{a}\colon X(a)\to Y(a)
is positivity preserving. Hence

(3.2.1)

\sgn(x)=\sgn(\nu_{a}(x)),\qquad\text{\rm if $x\in X(a)$}

and

(3.2.2)

\sgn(gx)=\sgn(\nu_{b}(gx))=\sgn(g\nu_{a}(x))\qquad\text{\rm if $x\in X(a)$, $g\in\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{b}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.44pt{b}\kern 6.22598pt{a}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{b}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.44pt{b}\kern 6.22598pt{a}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{b}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-5.4658pt{b}\kern 3.85262pt{a}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{b}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-4.36505pt{b}\kern 2.75186pt{a}}}}$.}

These equations imply that

(3.2.3)

x\in X^{{\mathrm{re}}}(a)\iff\nu_{a}(x)\in Y^{{\mathrm{re}}}(a).

In particular,
\nu_{a}

restricts to a positivity preserving morphism
\nu^{{\mathrm{re}}}_{a}\colon X^{{\mathrm{re}}}(a)\to Y^{{\mathrm{re}}}(a)
of signed sets. The maps
(\nu^{{\mathrm{re}}}_{a})_{a\in\operatorname{ob}(G)}
so defined
are the components of a morphism
\nu^{\mathrm{re}}\colon(G,X^{{\mathrm{re}}})\to(G,Y^{{\mathrm{re}}})
of signed
G
-sets. The maps
(G,X)\mapsto(G,X^{{\mathrm{re}}})
on objects and
\nu\mapsto\nu^{{\mathrm{re}}}
on morphisms defines an endofunctor, which we call
realification
, of the category of signed
G
-sets.

Using imaginary roots instead of real roots, there is a similarly defined endofunctor, denoted
(G,X)\mapsto(G,X^{{\mathrm{im}}})
on objects, and
\nu\mapsto\nu^{{\mathrm{im}}}
on morphisms, of the category of signed
G
-sets. The only essential use we make of it is notational:
X^{{\mathrm{im}}}(a)
,
X^{{\mathrm{im}},+}(a)
and
X^{{\mathrm{im}},-}(a)
denote, respectively, the sets of imaginary roots, positive imaginary roots and negative imaginary roots of
X
based at
a
. We note however that
(G,X)\cong(G,X^{{\mathrm{re}}})\coprod(G,X^{{\mathrm{im}}})
where
\coprod
denotes coproduct in the category of signed
G
-sets.

3.3.

For each object
a
of
G
, the
dominance preorder
5
5

5

This notion abstracts the dominance order on the root system of a Coxeter group, as defined initially on positive roots in
[
8
]
and extended to the whole root system in
[
28
]
.

\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\preceq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.22745pt{a}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\preceq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.22745pt{a}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\preceq^{{\kern-5.69846pt\kern 6.08734pt}}_{{\kern-8.04851pt{a}\kern 6.08734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\preceq^{{\kern-4.60957pt\kern 4.99846pt}}_{{\kern-6.95963pt{a}\kern 4.99846pt}}}
of
(G,X)
at
a
is the preorder on
X(a)
which is defined as follows: for
x,y\in X(a)
, let
x\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\preceq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.22745pt{a}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\preceq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.22745pt{a}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\preceq^{{\kern-5.69846pt\kern 6.08734pt}}_{{\kern-8.04851pt{a}\kern 6.08734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\preceq^{{\kern-4.60957pt\kern 4.99846pt}}_{{\kern-6.95963pt{a}\kern 4.99846pt}}}y
if for all
b\in\operatorname{ob}(G)
and
g\in\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{b}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.44pt{b}\kern 6.22598pt{a}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{b}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.44pt{b}\kern 6.22598pt{a}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{b}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-5.4658pt{b}\kern 3.85262pt{a}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{b}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-4.36505pt{b}\kern 2.75186pt{a}}}}
, we have
gy\in X^{-}(b)\implies gx\in X^{-}(b)
. There is then an associated equivalence relation
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\sim^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.22745pt{a}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\sim^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.22745pt{a}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\sim^{{\kern-5.69846pt\kern 6.08734pt}}_{{\kern-8.04851pt{a}\kern 6.08734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\sim^{{\kern-4.60957pt\kern 4.99846pt}}_{{\kern-6.95963pt{a}\kern 4.99846pt}}}
on
X(a)
, which we call
parallelism
(at
a
), in which

x\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\sim^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.22745pt{a}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\sim^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.22745pt{a}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\sim^{{\kern-5.69846pt\kern 6.08734pt}}_{{\kern-8.04851pt{a}\kern 6.08734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\sim^{{\kern-4.60957pt\kern 4.99846pt}}_{{\kern-6.95963pt{a}\kern 4.99846pt}}}y\iff x\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\preceq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.22745pt{a}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\preceq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.22745pt{a}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\preceq^{{\kern-5.69846pt\kern 6.08734pt}}_{{\kern-8.04851pt{a}\kern 6.08734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\preceq^{{\kern-4.60957pt\kern 4.99846pt}}_{{\kern-6.95963pt{a}\kern 4.99846pt}}}y\text{ \rm and }y\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\preceq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.22745pt{a}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\preceq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.22745pt{a}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\preceq^{{\kern-5.69846pt\kern 6.08734pt}}_{{\kern-8.04851pt{a}\kern 6.08734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\preceq^{{\kern-4.60957pt\kern 4.99846pt}}_{{\kern-6.95963pt{a}\kern 4.99846pt}}}x,\qquad x,y\in X(a).

All roots in the parallelism class

\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}[x]^{{\kern-8.67365pt\kern 9.06253pt}}_{{\kern-11.76373pt{a}\kern 9.06253pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}[x]^{{\kern-8.67365pt\kern 9.06253pt}}_{{\kern-11.76373pt{a}\kern 9.06253pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}[x]^{{\kern-5.13382pt\kern 5.5227pt}}_{{\kern-7.48387pt{a}\kern 5.5227pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}[x]^{{\kern-3.55588pt\kern 3.94476pt}}_{{\kern-5.90593pt{a}\kern 3.94476pt}}}:=\{\,y\in X(a)\mid y\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\sim^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.22745pt{a}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\sim^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.22745pt{a}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\sim^{{\kern-5.69846pt\kern 6.08734pt}}_{{\kern-8.04851pt{a}\kern 6.08734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\sim^{{\kern-4.60957pt\kern 4.99846pt}}_{{\kern-6.95963pt{a}\kern 4.99846pt}}}x\,\}
(i.e.
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\sim^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.22745pt{a}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\sim^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.22745pt{a}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\sim^{{\kern-5.69846pt\kern 6.08734pt}}_{{\kern-8.04851pt{a}\kern 6.08734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\sim^{{\kern-4.60957pt\kern 4.99846pt}}_{{\kern-6.95963pt{a}\kern 4.99846pt}}}\,
-equivalence class) of a root
x
based at
a
have the sign as
x
, and we define the sign (positive or negative) of
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}[x]^{{\kern-8.67365pt\kern 9.06253pt}}_{{\kern-11.76373pt{a}\kern 9.06253pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}[x]^{{\kern-8.67365pt\kern 9.06253pt}}_{{\kern-11.76373pt{a}\kern 9.06253pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}[x]^{{\kern-5.13382pt\kern 5.5227pt}}_{{\kern-7.48387pt{a}\kern 5.5227pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}[x]^{{\kern-3.55588pt\kern 3.94476pt}}_{{\kern-5.90593pt{a}\kern 3.94476pt}}}
to be equal to that of
x
.

Note that if a root is real, then so is every root in its parallelism
class. The parallelism class of an imaginary root is the set of all
imaginary roots with the same base and sign as that root.

We say that
(G,X)
is compressed if all of the dominance
preorders
(\,\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}X^{{\kern-6.71323pt\kern 7.10211pt}}_{{\kern-9.80331pt{a}\kern 7.10211pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}X^{{\kern-6.71323pt\kern 7.10211pt}}_{{\kern-9.80331pt{a}\kern 7.10211pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}X^{{\kern-4.05513pt\kern 4.44402pt}}_{{\kern-6.40518pt{a}\kern 4.44402pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}X^{{\kern-2.78542pt\kern 3.1743pt}}_{{\kern-5.13547pt{a}\kern 3.1743pt}}},\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\preceq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.22745pt{a}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\preceq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.22745pt{a}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\preceq^{{\kern-5.69846pt\kern 6.08734pt}}_{{\kern-8.04851pt{a}\kern 6.08734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\preceq^{{\kern-4.60957pt\kern 4.99846pt}}_{{\kern-6.95963pt{a}\kern 4.99846pt}}})
with
a\in\operatorname{ob}(G)

are partial orders, in which case we call them
dominance
orders
. Note that
(G,X)
is compressed if and only if any two
parallel roots of
X
(which necessarily have the same base)
are equal.

3.4.

Let
X^{{\mathrm{c}}}(a)
denote the set of all parallelism
classes in
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}X^{{\kern-6.71323pt\kern 7.10211pt}}_{{\kern-9.80331pt{a}\kern 7.10211pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}X^{{\kern-6.71323pt\kern 7.10211pt}}_{{\kern-9.80331pt{a}\kern 7.10211pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}X^{{\kern-4.05513pt\kern 4.44402pt}}_{{\kern-6.40518pt{a}\kern 4.44402pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}X^{{\kern-2.78542pt\kern 3.1743pt}}_{{\kern-5.13547pt{a}\kern 3.1743pt}}}
. That is,

X^{{\mathrm{c}}}(a):=X(a)/\negthinspace\negthinspace\negthinspace\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\sim^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.22745pt{a}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\sim^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.22745pt{a}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\sim^{{\kern-5.69846pt\kern 6.08734pt}}_{{\kern-8.04851pt{a}\kern 6.08734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\sim^{{\kern-4.60957pt\kern 4.99846pt}}_{{\kern-6.95963pt{a}\kern 4.99846pt}}}\,\,=\{\,\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}[x]^{{\kern-8.67365pt\kern 9.06253pt}}_{{\kern-11.76373pt{a}\kern 9.06253pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}[x]^{{\kern-8.67365pt\kern 9.06253pt}}_{{\kern-11.76373pt{a}\kern 9.06253pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}[x]^{{\kern-5.13382pt\kern 5.5227pt}}_{{\kern-7.48387pt{a}\kern 5.5227pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}[x]^{{\kern-3.55588pt\kern 3.94476pt}}_{{\kern-5.90593pt{a}\kern 3.94476pt}}}\mid x\in\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}X^{{\kern-6.71323pt\kern 7.10211pt}}_{{\kern-9.80331pt{a}\kern 7.10211pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}X^{{\kern-6.71323pt\kern 7.10211pt}}_{{\kern-9.80331pt{a}\kern 7.10211pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}X^{{\kern-4.05513pt\kern 4.44402pt}}_{{\kern-6.40518pt{a}\kern 4.44402pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}X^{{\kern-2.78542pt\kern 3.1743pt}}_{{\kern-5.13547pt{a}\kern 3.1743pt}}}\,\}.

This is a signed set with
-\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}[^{{\kern-1.87502pt\kern 2.2639pt}}_{{\kern-4.9651pt{a}\kern 2.2639pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}[^{{\kern-1.87502pt\kern 2.2639pt}}_{{\kern-4.9651pt{a}\kern 2.2639pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}[^{{\kern-0.97223pt\kern 1.36111pt}}_{{\kern-3.32228pt{a}\kern 1.36111pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}[^{{\kern-0.58333pt\kern 0.97221pt}}_{{\kern-2.93338pt{a}\kern 0.97221pt}}}x]:=\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}[^{{\kern-1.87502pt\kern 2.2639pt}}_{{\kern-4.9651pt{a}\kern 2.2639pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}[^{{\kern-1.87502pt\kern 2.2639pt}}_{{\kern-4.9651pt{a}\kern 2.2639pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}[^{{\kern-0.97223pt\kern 1.36111pt}}_{{\kern-3.32228pt{a}\kern 1.36111pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}[^{{\kern-0.58333pt\kern 0.97221pt}}_{{\kern-2.93338pt{a}\kern 0.97221pt}}}-x]

The

3.5.

Denote the parallelism classes of

It follows from above that there is a (well-defined) positivity preserving morphism of signed sets

\nu^{{\mathrm{c}}}_{a}\colon X^{{\mathrm{c}}}(a)\to Y^{{\mathrm{c}}}(a)
defined by

\nu^{{\mathrm{c}}}_{a}(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}[x]^{{\kern-8.67365pt\kern 9.06253pt}}_{{\kern-11.76373pt{a}\kern 9.06253pt{X}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}[x]^{{\kern-8.67365pt\kern 9.06253pt}}_{{\kern-11.76373pt{a}\kern 9.06253pt{X}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}[x]^{{\kern-5.13382pt\kern 5.5227pt}}_{{\kern-7.48387pt{a}\kern 5.5227pt{X}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}[x]^{{\kern-3.55588pt\kern 3.94476pt}}_{{\kern-5.90593pt{a}\kern 3.94476pt{X}}}})=\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}[\nu_{a}(x)]^{{\kern-22.02304pt\kern 22.41193pt}}_{{\kern-25.11313pt{a}\kern 22.41193pt{Y}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}[\nu_{a}(x)]^{{\kern-22.02304pt\kern 22.41193pt}}_{{\kern-25.11313pt{a}\kern 22.41193pt{Y}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}[\nu_{a}(x)]^{{\kern-13.71548pt\kern 14.10437pt}}_{{\kern-16.06554pt{a}\kern 14.10437pt{Y}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}[\nu_{a}(x)]^{{\kern-10.35709pt\kern 10.74597pt}}_{{\kern-12.70714pt{a}\kern 10.74597pt{Y}}}},\qquad x\in X(a).

Moreover, the underlying function (of sets) of

\nu^{{\mathrm{c}}}_{a}
is injective, and it is therfore bijective if (the underlying function of)
\nu_{a}
is surjective.

The definitions now show that
\nu^{{\mathrm{c}}}:=(\nu^{{\mathrm{c}}}_{a})_{a\in\operatorname{ob}(G)}
is a morphism
X^{{\mathrm{c}}}\to Y^{{\mathrm{c}}}
of signed
G
-sets. Moreover,
\nu^{{\mathrm{c}}}
is an isomorphism if the (underlying functions of) the components of
\nu
are surjective. The maps
(G,X)\mapsto(G,X^{{\mathrm{c}}})
on objects and
\nu\mapsto\nu^{{\mathrm{c}}}
on morphisms, evidently define an endofunctor, which we call
compression
, of the category of signed
G
-sets.

3.6.

The
real compression
of the signed
G
-set
(G,X)
is the signed
G
-set
(G,X^{{\mathrm{rec}}})
where

X^{{\mathrm{rec}}}:=(X^{\mathrm{re}})^{{\mathrm{c}}}=(X^{{\mathrm{c}}})^{\mathrm{re}}.

For an object
a
of
G
,
X^{{\mathrm{rec}}}(a)
is the set of parallelism classes of real roots in
X(a)
. Similarly, for a morphism

\nu\colon(G,X)\to(G,Y)
of signed
G
-sets, , one has
(\nu^{{\mathrm{re}}})^{{\mathrm{c}}}=(\nu^{{\mathrm{c}}})^{{\mathrm{re}}}\colon(G,X^{{\mathrm{rec}}})\to(G,Y^{{\mathrm{rec}}})
. It follows that
\mathrm{Id}
,
{\mathrm{re}}
,
{\mathrm{c}}
,

{\mathrm{rec}}={\mathrm{re}}\circ{\mathrm{c}}={\mathrm{c}}\circ{\mathrm{re}}
are four endofunctors of the category of signed
G
-sets.

There is a morphism

\iota_{X}\colon(G,X^{{\mathrm{re}}})\to(G,X)
of signed groupoid sets
with components given by the inclusions
{X}^{{\mathrm{re}}}(a)\to X(a)
for
a\in\operatorname{ob}(G)
. These morphisms define a natural transformations
\iota\colon{\mathrm{re}}\to\mathrm{Id}
. Similarly, there is a morphism

\eta_{X}\colon(G,X)\to(G,X^{{\mathrm{c}}})
of signed groupoid sets
with component at
a\in\operatorname{ob}(G)
given by the natural projection

X(a)\to X(a)/\negthinspace\negthinspace\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\sim^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.22745pt{a}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\sim^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.22745pt{a}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\sim^{{\kern-5.69846pt\kern 6.08734pt}}_{{\kern-8.04851pt{a}\kern 6.08734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\sim^{{\kern-4.60957pt\kern 4.99846pt}}_{{\kern-6.95963pt{a}\kern 4.99846pt}}}\,=X^{{\mathrm{c}}}(a)
. These endofunctors and natural transformations appear in a commutative diagram as follows:

Proposition 3.7
.

Let
(G,X)
be a signed groupoid set.

(a)

For
a\in\operatorname{ob}(G)
, the weak preorders
(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-5.81378pt{a}\kern 3.85262pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-4.71303pt{a}\kern 2.75186pt}}},\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.22745pt{a}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.22745pt{a}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\leq^{{\kern-5.69846pt\kern 6.08734pt}}_{{\kern-8.04851pt{a}\kern 6.08734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}\leq^{{\kern-4.60957pt\kern 4.99846pt}}_{{\kern-6.95963pt{a}\kern 4.99846pt}}})
of

(G,X)
,
(G,X^{{\mathrm{re}}})
,
(G,X^{{\mathrm{c}}})
and
(G,X^{{\mathrm{rec}}})
at
a
are equal.

(b)

If any of the four signed
G
-sets in (a) is faithful (resp., preprincipal), then they all are.

Proof.

Using (
3.1.1
), the weak preorders at
a
of
(G,X)
and
(G,X^{{\mathrm{re}}})
are equal, and, by the definitions,
(G,X)
is faithful (resp., preprincipal) if and only if
(G,X^{{\mathrm{re}}})
is faithful (resp., preprincipal). Similarly, using (
3.4.1
), the weak preorders of
(G,X)
and
(G,X^{{\mathrm{c}}})
are equal, and by the definitions,
(G,X)
is faithful (resp., preprincipal) if and only if
(G,X^{{\mathrm{c}}})
is faithful (resp., preprincipal). All the assertions now follow on recalling that
(G,(X^{{\mathrm{re}}})^{{\mathrm{c}}})=(G,X^{{\mathrm{rec}}})
.
∎

We shall also use the following general facts (see
[
19
]
or
[
15
]
–
[
16
]
).

Proposition 3.8
.

Let
(G,X)
is preprincipal signed groupoid set, and let
A
denote the set of atomic generators of
(G,X)
.

(a)

For any
a\in\operatorname{ob}(G)
, the set of parallelism classes of real roots of
(G,X)
at
a
is

X^{{\mathrm{rec}}}(a)=\{\,g(X_{s})\mid b\in\operatorname{ob}(G),g\in\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt{b}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt{b}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-5.81378pt{a}\kern 3.85262pt{b}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-4.71303pt{a}\kern 2.75186pt{b}}}},s\in A\cap\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{b}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.44pt{b}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{b}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.44pt{b}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{b}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-5.4658pt{b}\kern 3.85262pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{b}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-4.36505pt{b}\kern 2.75186pt}}}\,\}
.

(b)

(G,X^{{\mathrm{rec}}})
is a principal signed groupoid set with
A
as its set of simple generators. It is real and compressed.

Remark
.

Under the hypotheses of (a), if

a,b_{i}\in\operatorname{ob}(G)
,
g_{i}\in\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt{b_{i}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt{b_{i}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-5.81378pt{a}\kern 3.85262pt{b_{i}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-4.71303pt{a}\kern 2.75186pt{b_{i}}}}}
,
s_{i}\in A\cap\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{b_{i}}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-10.1458pt{b_{i}}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{b_{i}}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-10.1458pt{b_{i}}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{b_{i}}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-7.1716pt{b_{i}}\kern 3.85262pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{b_{i}}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-6.07085pt{b_{i}}\kern 2.75186pt}}}
for
i=1,2
, then the subsets
g_{i}(X_{s_{i}})
of
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}X^{{\kern-6.71323pt\kern 7.10211pt}}_{{\kern-9.80331pt{a}\kern 7.10211pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}X^{{\kern-6.71323pt\kern 7.10211pt}}_{{\kern-9.80331pt{a}\kern 7.10211pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}X^{{\kern-4.05513pt\kern 4.44402pt}}_{{\kern-6.40518pt{a}\kern 4.44402pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}X^{{\kern-2.78542pt\kern 3.1743pt}}_{{\kern-5.13547pt{a}\kern 3.1743pt}}}
for
i=1,2
are either disjoint or equal.

Example 3.9
.

The signed
W
-set
(W_{\bullet},\Phi_{\bullet})
of Example
1.16
is real, compressed, principal and rootoidal.

If
W
is, further, a finite Weyl group, it has also a (possibly non-reduced) crystallographic root system
\Psi
in the sense of
[
5
]
; this has a set of simple roots
\Delta
, but
(\Psi,\Delta)
is not a based root system in the sense of this paper. The natural
W
-action on
\Psi
gives rise, in a similar manner to the construction of
(W_{\bullet},\Phi_{\bullet})
, to a signed groupoid set
(W_{\bullet},\Psi_{\bullet})
. Each parallelism classes of roots for
(W_{\bullet},\Psi_{\bullet})
is either a singleton set
\{\alpha\}
or of the form
\{\alpha,2\alpha\}
for some root
\alpha\in\Psi
. The set of parallelism classes is in bijection with the set of indivisible roots
\alpha
of
\Phi
. Thus,
(W_{\bullet},\Phi_{\bullet})
is compressed if and only if
\Psi
is reduced in the usual sense (that is,
\alpha\in\Psi
implies
2\alpha\not\in\Psi
); in that case,

(W_{\bullet},\Phi_{\bullet})
and
(W_{\bullet},\Psi_{\bullet})
are isomorphic as signed
W_{\bullet}
-sets. It follows also that
(W_{\bullet},\Psi_{\bullet})
is principal if and only if
\Psi
is reduced. Even if
\Psi
is non-reduced,

(W_{\bullet},\Psi_{\bullet})
is real, preprincipal and rootoidal, and its (real) compression is isomorphic to
(W_{\bullet},\Phi_{\bullet})
.

3.10.
Abstract root systems of Brink-Howlett groupoid sets

In the remainder of this section,
(G,\Lambda)
denotes the signed groupoid set attached to a Brink-Howlett groupoid
G
associated to the Coxeter system
(W,S)
and its based root system
(\Phi,\Pi)
as in
1.21
.
Recall that the weak order of
(W,S)
is denoted as
(W,\leq)
.

Proposition 3.11
.

Let
J\in\operatorname{ob}(G)
and
g,g^{\prime}\in\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-9.5255pt{J}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-9.5255pt{J}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-6.24115pt{J}\kern 3.85262pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-5.1404pt{J}\kern 2.75186pt}}}
. Write
g=(J,w,K)
and
g^{\prime}=(J,w^{\prime},K)
where
K,K^{\prime}\subseteq\Pi
,
w,w^{\prime}\in W
and
wK=w^{\prime}K^{\prime}=J
.

(a)

|\Lambda_{g}|
is finite.

(b)

g\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.82576pt{J}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.82576pt{J}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-5.69846pt\kern 6.08734pt}}_{{\kern-8.47588pt{J}\kern 6.08734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-4.60957pt\kern 4.99846pt}}_{{\kern-7.387pt{J}\kern 4.99846pt}}}g^{\prime}
if and only if
w\leq w^{\prime}
.

(c)

If
\Lambda_{g}=\Lambda_{g^{\prime}}
, then
g=g^{\prime}
. That is,
(G,\Lambda)
is faithful.

(d)

The map
F_{J}\colon(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-9.5255pt{J}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-9.5255pt{J}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-6.24115pt{J}\kern 3.85262pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-5.1404pt{J}\kern 2.75186pt}}},\negthinspace\negthinspace\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.82576pt{J}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.82576pt{J}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-5.69846pt\kern 6.08734pt}}_{{\kern-8.47588pt{J}\kern 6.08734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-4.60957pt\kern 4.99846pt}}_{{\kern-7.387pt{J}\kern 4.99846pt}}})\to(W,\leq)
, induced by restriction to
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-9.5255pt{J}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-9.5255pt{J}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-6.24115pt{J}\kern 3.85262pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-5.1404pt{J}\kern 2.75186pt}}}
of the map of morphisms for the functor
F\colon G\to W_{\bullet}
in
1.21
, is an order-embedding.

(e)

For each object
K
of
G
, each closed interval in the poset
(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-10.84917pt{K}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-10.84917pt{K}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-7.18663pt{K}\kern 3.85262pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-6.08588pt{K}\kern 2.75186pt}}},\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-13.14943pt{K}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-13.14943pt{K}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}\leq^{{\kern-5.69846pt\kern 6.08734pt}}_{{\kern-9.42136pt{K}\kern 6.08734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}\leq^{{\kern-4.60957pt\kern 4.99846pt}}_{{\kern-8.33247pt{K}\kern 4.99846pt}}})
is finite. That is,
(G,\Lambda)
is interval finite.

Proof.

We have
\Lambda(J)^{+}=\Phi^{+}
and
\Lambda(J)^{-}=\Phi^{-}
, so

(3.11.1)

\Lambda_{g}=\Lambda(J)^{+}\cap w(\Lambda(K)^{-})=\Phi^{+}\cap w(\Phi^{-})=\Phi_{w}.

Part (a) follows since
|\Phi_{w}|=\ell(w)<\infty
.
The definitions imply from above that

(3.11.2)

g\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.82576pt{J}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.82576pt{J}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-5.69846pt\kern 6.08734pt}}_{{\kern-8.47588pt{J}\kern 6.08734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-4.60957pt\kern 4.99846pt}}_{{\kern-7.387pt{J}\kern 4.99846pt}}}g^{\prime}\iff\Lambda_{g}\subseteq\Lambda_{g^{\prime}}\iff\Phi_{w}\subseteq\Phi_{w^{\prime}}\iff w\leq w^{\prime},

proving (b).
If
\Lambda_{g}=\Lambda_{g^{\prime}}
, then from above,
g\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.82576pt{J}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.82576pt{J}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-5.69846pt\kern 6.08734pt}}_{{\kern-8.47588pt{J}\kern 6.08734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-4.60957pt\kern 4.99846pt}}_{{\kern-7.387pt{J}\kern 4.99846pt}}}g^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.82576pt{J}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.82576pt{J}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-5.69846pt\kern 6.08734pt}}_{{\kern-8.47588pt{J}\kern 6.08734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-4.60957pt\kern 4.99846pt}}_{{\kern-7.387pt{J}\kern 4.99846pt}}}g
and hence
w\leq w^{\prime}
and
w^{\prime}\leq w
, so
w=w^{\prime}
. Then
K=w^{-1}J=w^{\prime-1}J=K^{\prime}
so
g=g^{\prime}
, proving (c).

In (d), we have
F_{J}((J,w,K))=w
for
(J,w,K)\in\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-9.5255pt{J}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-9.5255pt{J}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-6.24115pt{J}\kern 3.85262pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-5.1404pt{J}\kern 2.75186pt}}}
. Therefore,
F_{J}
is an order embedding by (b). Part (e) follows from (a), (c) and the definition of the weak order
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.82576pt{J}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.82576pt{J}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-5.69846pt\kern 6.08734pt}}_{{\kern-8.47588pt{J}\kern 6.08734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-4.60957pt\kern 4.99846pt}}_{{\kern-7.387pt{J}\kern 4.99846pt}}}
, or from (d) and the fact intervals in weak order on
W
are finite.∎

Lemma 3.12
.

Let
w\colon J\to K
be a morphism in
G
.

(a)

If
L\subseteq\Pi\cap\Phi_{w^{-1}}
,
then
\nu(L,J)^{-1}\leq w^{-1}
.

(b)

If
\beta\in\Pi
and
\alpha\in\Phi_{w^{-1}}\cap\Phi_{J\cup\{\beta\}}
, then
\nu(\beta,J)^{-1}\leq w^{-1}
.

(c)

If
\alpha\in\Phi_{w^{-1}}\cap\Pi
, then
\nu(\alpha,J)^{-1}\leq w^{-1}

Proof.

Assume without loss of generality that
\Pi
is linearly independent. For (a), note first that
w(J)=K\subseteq\Phi^{+}
and
w(L)\subseteq\Phi^{-}
, so
J\cap L={\emptyset}
. Hence

\Phi^{+}_{L\cup J}\setminus\Phi^{+}_{J}\subseteq\left((\operatorname{cone}(L)+\operatorname{span}(J))\setminus\operatorname{span}(J)\right)\cap\Phi.

Using
w(J)=K
and
w(L)\subseteq\Phi^{-}
again shows that

w(\Phi^{+}_{L\cup J}\setminus\Phi^{+}_{J})\subseteq\left((\operatorname{cone}(w(L))+\operatorname{span}(K))\setminus\operatorname{span}(K)\right)\cap\Phi\subseteq\Phi^{-}.

Thus,
\Phi^{+}_{L\cup J}\setminus\Phi^{+}_{J}\subseteq\Phi_{w^{-1}}
, which is finite. Therefore,
\nu(L,J)
is defined, and since
\Phi_{\nu(L,J)^{-1}}=\Phi^{+}_{L\cup J}\setminus\Phi^{+}_{J}
, (a) follows.

To prove (b), note
\alpha\in\operatorname{cone}(J\cup\{\beta\})
.
Since
w(J)=K\subseteq\Phi^{+}
and
w(\alpha)\in\Phi^{-}
we must have
w(\beta)\in\Phi^{-}
and so
L:=\{\beta\}\subseteq\Pi\cap\Phi_{w^{-1}}
. Now (b) follows from (a). Part (c) is the special case of (b) in which
\beta=\alpha
.
∎

Proposition 3.13

(
[
12
, Proposition 5.5]
,
[
9
, Proposition 2.3]
)
.

Let
g=(K,w,J)
be a morphism in
G
. Then there are morphism
g_{i}=(J_{i},w_{i},J_{i-1})
of
G
, for
i=1,\ldots,n
, where
J_{0}=J
,
J_{n}=K
,

\alpha_{i}\in\Pi
,
J_{i}\subseteq\Pi
and
w_{i}=\nu(\alpha_{i},J_{i-1})
for
i=1,\ldots,n
, such that
g=g_{n}\cdots g_{1}
in
G
, and
w=w_{n}\cdots w_{1}
and
\ell(w)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell(w_{i})
in
W
. Moreover, if
\alpha\in\Pi\cap\Phi_{w^{-1}}
, one may choose these morphisms so that
\alpha_{1}=\alpha
.

Proof.

We prove this by induction on
\ell(w)
. If
\ell(w)=0
, then
w=1_{W}
,
K=J
and one takes
n=0
and
J_{0}=J
.
Otherwise, we have
w\neq 1
and may choose
\alpha\in\Pi\cap\Phi_{w^{-1}}
. By Lemma
3.12
, we have
\nu(\alpha,J)^{-1}\leq w^{-1}
. That is,
we have
w=w^{\prime}\nu(\alpha,J)
where
\ell(w)=\ell(w^{\prime})+\ell(\nu(\alpha,J)>\ell(w^{\prime})
. Let
J^{\prime}:=\nu(\alpha,J)J\subseteq\Pi
.
One has morphisms
g^{\prime}:=(K,w^{\prime},J^{\prime})
and
g_{1}=(J^{\prime},\nu(\alpha,J),J)
in
G
with
g=g^{\prime}g_{1}
. The desired conclusion follows by applying the inductive hypothesis to the morphism
g^{\prime}
of
G
.
∎

Proposition 3.14
.

(a)

The set of atomic morphisms of
(G,\Lambda)
coincides with the set of Brink-Howlett generators of
G
.

(b)

(G,\Lambda)
is preprincipal.

Proof.

We prove (a). Consider a non-identity morphism
g
of
G
in
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-10.84917pt{K}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-10.84917pt{K}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-7.18663pt{K}\kern 3.85262pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-6.08588pt{K}\kern 2.75186pt}}}
.
Write it as a composite
g=g_{n}\cdots g_{1}
of Brink-Howlett generators
g_{i}=(J_{i},\nu(\alpha_{i},J_{i-1}),J_{i-1})
as in Proposition

3.13
, where necessarily
n>0
. From
w=w_{n}\cdots w_{1}
with
l(w)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell(w_{i})
, it follows that
w_{n}\leq w
in
W
and hence
g_{n}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-13.14943pt{K}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-13.14943pt{K}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}\leq^{{\kern-5.69846pt\kern 6.08734pt}}_{{\kern-9.42136pt{K}\kern 6.08734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}\leq^{{\kern-4.60957pt\kern 4.99846pt}}_{{\kern-8.33247pt{K}\kern 4.99846pt}}}g
in
G
by Proposition
3.11
. If
g
is an atom of
(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-10.84917pt{K}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-10.84917pt{K}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-7.18663pt{K}\kern 3.85262pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-6.08588pt{K}\kern 2.75186pt}}},\negthinspace\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-13.14943pt{K}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-13.14943pt{K}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}\leq^{{\kern-5.69846pt\kern 6.08734pt}}_{{\kern-9.42136pt{K}\kern 6.08734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}\leq^{{\kern-4.60957pt\kern 4.99846pt}}_{{\kern-8.33247pt{K}\kern 4.99846pt}}})
, this forces
g=g_{n}
, since
g_{n}\neq 1_{K}
. Hence every atomic morphism of
G
is a Brink-Howlett generator.

Conversely, let
g=(K,\nu(\alpha,J),J)
be a Brink-Howlett generator of
G
. Since
\hskip-6.69205pt\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-13.14943pt{K}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-13.14943pt{K}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}\leq^{{\kern-5.69846pt\kern 6.08734pt}}_{{\kern-9.42136pt{K}\kern 6.08734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}\leq^{{\kern-4.60957pt\kern 4.99846pt}}_{{\kern-8.33247pt{K}\kern 4.99846pt}}}
has finite intervals and a minimum element, there is an atom
a
of
\negthinspace\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-13.14943pt{K}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-13.14943pt{K}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}\leq^{{\kern-5.69846pt\kern 6.08734pt}}_{{\kern-9.42136pt{K}\kern 6.08734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}\leq^{{\kern-4.60957pt\kern 4.99846pt}}_{{\kern-8.33247pt{K}\kern 4.99846pt}}}
such that
a\negthinspace\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-13.14943pt{K}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-13.14943pt{K}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}\leq^{{\kern-5.69846pt\kern 6.08734pt}}_{{\kern-9.42136pt{K}\kern 6.08734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}\leq^{{\kern-4.60957pt\kern 4.99846pt}}_{{\kern-8.33247pt{K}\kern 4.99846pt}}}g
. From the previous paragraph,
a
is a Brink-Howlett generator
a=(K,\nu(\alpha^{\prime},J^{\prime}),J^{\prime})
, and we therefore have
\nu(\alpha^{\prime},J^{\prime})\leq\nu(\alpha,J)
. Write
g^{-1}=(J,\nu(\beta,K),K)
and
a^{-1}=(J^{\prime},\nu(\beta^{\prime},K),K)
.
Then

\Phi_{\nu(\beta^{\prime},K)^{-1}}=\Phi_{\nu(\alpha^{\prime},J^{\prime})}\subseteq\Phi_{\nu(\alpha,J)}=\Phi_{\nu(\beta,K)^{-1}}.

Hence

\begin{split}\{\beta^{\prime}\}&=\Pi\cap(\Phi_{K\cup\{\beta^{\prime}\}}^{+}\setminus\Phi_{K}^{+})=\Pi\cap\Phi_{\nu(\beta^{\prime},K)^{-1}}\\
&\subseteq\Pi\cap\Phi_{\nu(\beta,K)^{-1}}=\Pi\cap(\Phi_{K\cup\{\beta\}}^{+}\setminus\Phi_{K}^{+})=\{\beta\}.\end{split}

From this, we conclude
\beta^{\prime}=\beta
,
J^{\prime}=J
,
g^{-1}=a^{-1}
and
g=a
. Hence
g
is an atom of
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-13.14943pt{K}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-13.14943pt{K}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}\leq^{{\kern-5.69846pt\kern 6.08734pt}}_{{\kern-9.42136pt{K}\kern 6.08734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{K}}}}\leq^{{\kern-4.60957pt\kern 4.99846pt}}_{{\kern-8.33247pt{K}\kern 4.99846pt}}}
as required to complete the proof of (a).

Now we prove (b). Let
s,g\in\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-9.5255pt{J}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-9.5255pt{J}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-6.24115pt{J}\kern 3.85262pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-5.1404pt{J}\kern 2.75186pt}}}
be such that
s
is atomic. Then
s
is a Brink-Howlett generator
s=(J,\nu(\alpha,K),K)
and
g
is of the form
g=(J,w,K^{\prime})
. We are to show that if
\Lambda_{s}\cap\Lambda_{g}\neq{\emptyset}
, then
\Lambda_{s}\subseteq\Lambda_{g}
. Equivalently, we have to show that if

\Phi_{\nu(\alpha,K)}\cap\Phi_{w}\neq{\emptyset}
, then

\Phi_{\nu(\alpha,K)}\subseteq\Phi_{w}
. Suppose that
\gamma\in\Phi_{\nu(\alpha,K)}\cap\Phi_{w}
.
Write
s^{-1}=(K,\nu(\beta,J),J)
. Then
