\gamma\in\Phi_{\nu(\alpha,K)}=\Phi_{\nu(\beta,J)^{-1}}=\Phi^{+}_{J\cup\{\beta\}}\setminus\Phi_{J}^{+}
. Hence
\gamma\in\Phi_{w}\cap\Phi^{+}_{J\cup\{\beta\}}
. By Lemma
3.12
, we have

\nu(\alpha,K)=\nu(\beta,J)^{-1}\leq w
. That is,
\Phi_{\nu(\alpha,K)}\subseteq\Phi_{w}
as required, proving (b).
∎

3.15.

We now discuss squares in the signed groupoid set
R=(W_{\bullet},\Phi_{\bullet})
, stating only the minimum needed for proofs in this section. For more extensive discussions of squares in various levels of generality, see
[
16
]
and
[
19
]
. In the following, we regard any subset of
W
as a poset in the order induced by weak order
\leq
on
W
.

Definition 3.16
.

A
square
(of
W
) is a quadruple
(w,x,y,z)
of elements of
W
such that
wx=yz
and
w(\Phi_{x})=\Phi_{y}
:

\scriptstyle{{w}}
\scriptstyle{x}
\scriptstyle{{z}}
\scriptstyle{y}

Proposition 3.17
.

Let
x
,
y
,
z
and
w
be elements of
W
. Then the following conditions are equivalent:

(i)

(w,x,y,z)
is a square. That is,
wx=yz
and
w(\Phi_{x})=\Phi_{y}
.

(ii)

wx=yz
and
\Phi_{wx}=\Phi_{w}\,\dot{\cup}\,\Phi_{y}
.

(iii)

(y,z,w,x)
is a square. That is,
yz=wx
and
y(\Phi_{z})=\Phi_{w}
.

(iv)

(w^{-1},y,x,z^{-1})
is a square. That is,
w^{-1}y=xz^{-1}
and
w^{-1}(\Phi_{y})=\Phi_{x}
.

(v)

wx=yz
and in weak order on
W
,
w\vee y=wx
and

w^{-1}\vee x=w^{-1}y
.

(vi)

wx=yz
and in weak order on
W
,
w\vee y\leq wx
and

w^{-1}\vee x\leq w^{-1}y
.

Remarks
.

(1) There is an action of the dihedral group of order
8
on
W^{4}
, in which the two simple reflections act by the maps
(w,x,y,z)\mapsto(y,z,w,x)
and

(w,x,y,z)\mapsto(w^{-1},y,x,z^{-1})
. Since (i)
\iff
(iii)
\iff
(iv), the set of squares of
W
is invariant under this action.

(2) By (1), the dihedral group of order
8
also acts naturally on the “characterizations” (of which (i)–(vi) provide examples) of squares
(w,x,y,z)
.

(3) A square
(w,x,y,z)
is uniquely determined by the ordered pair
(w,x)
(resp.,
(w,y)
,
(y,z)
,
(x,z)
).

Proof.

We show (i)
\implies
(ii). Suppose that (i) holds. Since
w(\Phi_{x})=\Phi_{y}\subseteq\Phi^{+}
, we have
\Phi_{x}\cap\Phi_{w^{-1}}={\emptyset}
and hence
\Phi_{wx}=\Phi_{w}\,\dot{\cup}\,w(\Phi_{x})=\Phi_{w}\,\dot{\cup}\,\Phi_{y}
, which implies (ii).

We prove (ii)
\implies
(i). Assume (ii) holds. Since
\Phi_{w}\subseteq\Phi_{wx}
, we have
w\leq wx
and so
\Phi_{wx}=\Phi_{w}\,\dot{\cup}\,w(\Phi_{x})
. By (ii). we get
\Phi_{y}=w(\Phi_{x})
, proving (i).

Condition (ii) is equivalent to
yz=wx
and
\Phi_{yz}=\Phi_{y}\,\dot{\cup}\,\Phi_{w}
, which by symmetry and (ii)
\iff
(i), is equivalent to (iii).

Condition (i) is equivalent to
y^{-1}w=zx^{-1}
and

w^{-1}(\Phi_{y})=\Phi_{x}
, which is (iv). Hence (i)
\iff
(iv).

We show (i)
\implies
(v). Suppose (i) holds. Then (ii) holds, and it implies
w\vee y=wx
. Similarly, since (iv) holds, we have

w^{-1}\vee x=w^{-1}y
. Hence (v) holds.

It is trivial that (v) implies (vi). Finally, we prove that (vi)
\implies
(i). Suppose (vi) holds.
Since
w\leq w\vee y\leq wx
, we have
\Phi_{w^{-1}}\cap\Phi_{x}={\emptyset}
. Similarly,
w^{-1}\leq w^{-1}\vee x\leq w^{-1}y
implies
\Phi_{w}\cap\Phi_{y}={\emptyset}
. These imply

\Phi_{w}\,\dot{\cup}\,\Phi_{y}\subseteq\Phi_{w\vee y}\subseteq\Phi_{wx}=\Phi_{w}\,\dot{\cup}\,w(\Phi_{x})

and so

\Phi_{y}\subseteq w(\Phi_{x})
. Similarly,

\Phi_{w^{-1}}\,\dot{\cup}\,\Phi_{x}\subseteq\Phi_{w^{-1}\vee x}\subseteq\Phi_{w^{-1}y}=\Phi_{w^{-1}}\,\dot{\cup}\,w^{-1}(\Phi_{y})

and thus
\Phi_{x}\subseteq w^{-1}(\Phi_{y})
i.e.
w(\Phi_{x})\subseteq\Phi_{y}
.
So we have
wx=yz
and
w(\Phi_{x})=\Phi_{y}
, proving (i).
∎

3.18.

Recall that a
complete meet semi-lattice
is a non-empty poset
(X,\preceq)
such that every non-empty subset of
X
has a meet (also called its glb or inf) in
X
.

Let
X
be a complete meet semilattice. Then any subset
A
of
X
which has an upper bound in
X
has a join (also called its lub or sup) in
X
;
that join is the meet of the set of upper bounds of
A
. We say that a non-empty subset
Y
of
X
is a
join-closed meet sub-semilattice
of
X
if for any non-empty subset
A
of
Y
, its meet
\bigwedge A
in
X
satisfies
\bigwedge A\in Y
, and further, if
A
has a join
\bigvee A\in X
, then
\bigvee A\in Y
.

We say a morphism
f\colon X_{1}\to X_{2}
of posets preserves existing joins (resp., existing meets) if for any subset

A
of
X_{1}
such that
\bigvee A
(resp.,
\bigwedge A
) exists in
X_{1}
, the join
\bigvee f(A)
(resp., the meet
\bigwedge f(A)
) exists in
X_{2}
and one has
f(\bigvee A)=\bigvee f(A)
(resp.,

f(\bigwedge A)=\bigvee f(A)
).

Proposition 3.19
.

Let
w\in W
. Define
A_{w}:=\{\,x\in W\mid\Phi_{x}\cap\Phi_{w^{-1}}={\emptyset}\,\}
and
B_{w}:=\{\,z\in W\mid\Phi_{w}\subseteq\Phi_{z}\,\}
, so
1_{W}\in A_{w}
and
w\in B_{w}
.

(a)

A_{w}
and
B_{w}
are join-closed meet sub-semilattices of
(W,\leq)
.

(b)

The map
\theta_{w}\colon A_{w}\to B_{w}
defined by
\theta_{w}(x):=wx
is an order isomorphism.

(c)

\theta_{w}
and
\theta_{w}^{-1}
each preserve all existing meets and joins of subsets of their domain.

Proof.

We prove (a). The fact
A_{w}
is closed under formation of meets in
W
of non-empty subsets of
A_{w}
is clear since
A_{w}
is a down-set of
W
. Also, if
A\subseteq A_{w}
and
\bigvee A
exists in
(W,\leq)
, then
\bigvee A\in A_{w}
by the JOP for
(W,\leq)
. The assertion for
B_{w}
is clear since
B_{w}
is the principal up-set of
W
generated by
w
.

Part (b) follows from basic properties of weak order (see
1.5
), and (c) is a direct consequence of (b).
∎

Proposition 3.20
.

Suppose that
(w,x_{i},y_{i},z_{i})
are squares, for
i\in I
.

(a)

If
I\neq{\emptyset}
, there is a unique square
(w,x,y,z)
with
x=\bigwedge_{i}x_{i}
and
y=\bigwedge_{i}y_{i}
.

(b)

The join
x=\bigvee_{i}x_{i}
exists in
(W,\leq)
if and only if the join
y=\bigvee_{i}y_{i}
exists. In that case, there exists a unique square
(w,x,y,z)
.

Proof.

We make extensive use of Propositions
3.19
and
3.17
in the proof, without further explicit reference to them. In both (a) and (b), there is at most one square
(w,x,y,z)
satisfying the indicated conditions, by Remark
3.17
(3).

We prove (a). We have
x_{i}\in A_{w}
for all
i\in I
, so
x=\bigwedge_{i}x_{i}\in A_{w}
.
For each
i\in I
,
y\leq y_{i}\leq wx_{i}=\theta_{w}(x_{i})
, so

y\leq\bigwedge_{i\in I}\theta_{w}(x_{i})=\theta_{w}(\bigwedge_{i\in I}x_{i})=\theta_{w}(x)=wx
. Also,
w\leq wx
since
x\in A_{w}
. Hence
w\vee y\leq wx
. By symmetry, we also have
w^{-1}\vee x\leq w^{-1}y
. Choose
z
so that
wx=yz
. Then
(w,x,y,z)
is a square, proving (a).

Now we prove (b). Suppose that
x=\bigvee_{i}x_{i}
exists in
W
. Since
x_{i}\in A_{w}
for each
i\in I
, we have
x\in A_{w}
.
For each
i\in I
, we have
y_{i}\leq w\vee y_{i}=wx_{i}=\theta_{w}(x_{i})\leq\theta_{w}(x)=wx
, so
y=\bigvee_{i}y_{i}
exists and
y\leq wx
. By symmetry, if
y=\bigvee_{i}y_{i}
exists, then
x=\bigvee_{i}x_{i}
exists and
x\leq w^{-1}y
.

Assume henceforward that both joins
x
and
y
exist.
Then from above, we have
y\leq wx
and
x\leq w^{-1}y
.
Since
x\in A_{w}
, we have
w\leq wx
, hence
w\vee y\leq wx
. By symmetry, we also have
w^{-1}\vee x\leq w^{-1}x
.
Choose
z
so that
wx=yz
. Then
(w,x,y,z)
is a square, proving (b).
∎

Proposition 3.21
.

Let
J
be an object of
G
and
w\in W
. Then there is a morphism
(J,w,K)
in
G
for some
K\subseteq\Pi
if and only if for each
\alpha\in J
, there exists a square

(w,x_{\alpha},s_{\alpha},z_{\alpha})
for some
x_{\alpha},z_{\alpha}\in W
. If so, then necessarily

K=w^{-1}J
and for all
\alpha\in J
,
z_{\alpha}=w
and

x_{\alpha}=s_{w^{-1}\alpha}
where
w^{-1}\alpha\in K
.

Proof.

If
(J,w,K)
is a morphisms in
J
, then
K=w^{-1}J\subseteq\Pi
and for each
\alpha\in J
, there exists a square

R:=(w,s_{w^{-1}\alpha},s_{\alpha},w)
since
ws_{w^{-1}\alpha}=s_{\alpha}w
and
w(\Phi_{s_{w^{-1}\alpha}})=w\{w^{-1}\alpha\}=\{\alpha\}=\Phi_{s_{\alpha}}
because
\alpha\in J\subseteq\Pi
and
w^{-1}\alpha\in K\subseteq\Pi
. By Remark
3.17
(3),

R
is the only square of the form
(w,x_{\alpha},s_{\alpha},z_{\alpha})
where
x_{\alpha},z_{\alpha}\in W
.

Conversely, suppose that for each
\alpha\in J
, there exists a square
(w,x_{\alpha},s_{\alpha},z_{\alpha})
where
x_{\alpha},z_{\alpha}\in W
. Let
\alpha\in J
. Then

w(\Phi_{x_{\alpha}})=\Phi_{s_{\alpha}}=\{\alpha\}
.
This implies that
\Phi_{x_{\alpha}}
is a singleton set,
so
x_{\alpha}\in S
, say
x_{\alpha}=s_{\alpha^{\prime}}
where
\alpha^{\prime}\in\Pi
. But then
\{\alpha\}=w(\Phi_{x_{\alpha}})=w(\Phi_{s_{\alpha^{\prime}}})=w\{\alpha^{\prime}\}=\{w\alpha^{\prime}\}
forcing

w^{-1}\alpha=\alpha^{\prime}\in\Pi
. This shows that
K:=w^{-1}J=\{\,w^{-1}\alpha\mid\alpha\in J\,\}\subseteq\Pi
and so
(J,w,K)
is a morphism of
G
.
∎

Proposition 3.22
.

Let
J\in\operatorname{ob}(G)
.

(a)

The map
F_{J}\colon(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-9.5255pt{J}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-9.5255pt{J}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-6.24115pt{J}\kern 3.85262pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-5.1404pt{J}\kern 2.75186pt}}},\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.82576pt{J}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.82576pt{J}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-5.69846pt\kern 6.08734pt}}_{{\kern-8.47588pt{J}\kern 6.08734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-4.60957pt\kern 4.99846pt}}_{{\kern-7.387pt{J}\kern 4.99846pt}}})\to(W,\leq)
in Proposition
3.11
preserves all meets of non-empty subsets of
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-9.5255pt{J}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-9.5255pt{J}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-6.24115pt{J}\kern 3.85262pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-5.1404pt{J}\kern 2.75186pt}}}
and all
existing joins of subsets of
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-9.5255pt{J}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-9.5255pt{J}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-6.24115pt{J}\kern 3.85262pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-5.1404pt{J}\kern 2.75186pt}}}
.

(b)

The image of
F_{J}
is a join-closed meet sub-semilattice of
(W,\leq)
.

Proof.

Consider an index set
I
and a family of morphisms
(g_{i})_{i\in I}
in
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-8.92719pt{a}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-5.81378pt{a}\kern 3.85262pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{a}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-4.71303pt{a}\kern 2.75186pt}}}
. Write
w_{i}:=F_{J}(g_{i})\in W
. Since
F_{J}
is an order embedding, it will suffice to prove the following two claims:

(1)

g:=\bigvee_{i}g_{i}
exists if and only if
w:=\bigvee_{i}w_{i}
exists, and then
w=F_{J}(g)
.

(2)

If
I\neq{\emptyset}
, then
g^{\prime}:=\bigwedge_{i}g_{i}
exists,

w^{\prime}:=\bigwedge_{i}w_{i}
exists and
w^{\prime}=F_{J}(g^{\prime}_{i})
.

Write
g_{i}=(J,w_{i},K_{i})
for
i\in I
. Then by Proposition
3.21
, for each
i\in I
and
\alpha\in J
, there is a square
(w_{i},x_{i,\alpha},s_{\alpha},z_{i,\alpha})
or equivalently, a square
(s_{\alpha},z_{i,\alpha},w_{i},x_{i,\alpha})
.

We prove (1). Suppose first that
g:=\bigvee_{i}g_{i}
exists.
Then
w_{i}=F_{J}(g_{i})\leq F_{J}(g)
, so
F_{J}(g)
is an upper bound for
\{w_{i}\mid i\in I\}
and
w:=\bigvee_{i\in I}w_{i}
exists. Hence to prove (1), we may assume that

w:=\bigvee_{i\in I}w_{i}
exists, and it will suffice to show show that
g=\bigvee_{i}g_{i}
exists and
F_{J}(g)=w
. By Proposition
3.20
, for each
\alpha\in J
, there is a square

(s_{\alpha},z_{\alpha},w,x_{\alpha})
. Hence there is a square
(w,x_{\alpha},s_{\alpha},z_{\alpha})
. By Proposition
3.21
, there is a morphism
g=(J,w,K)
in
G
, where

K:=w^{-1}J
. We check that
g=\bigvee_{i}g_{i}
. First,
g_{i}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.82576pt{J}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.82576pt{J}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-5.69846pt\kern 6.08734pt}}_{{\kern-8.47588pt{J}\kern 6.08734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-4.60957pt\kern 4.99846pt}}_{{\kern-7.387pt{J}\kern 4.99846pt}}}g
for all
i
since
F_{J}(g_{i})=w_{i}\leq w=F_{J}(g)
.
Second, if
h\in\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-9.5255pt{J}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-9.5255pt{J}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-6.24115pt{J}\kern 3.85262pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-5.1404pt{J}\kern 2.75186pt}}}
with
g_{i}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.82576pt{J}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.82576pt{J}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-5.69846pt\kern 6.08734pt}}_{{\kern-8.47588pt{J}\kern 6.08734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-4.60957pt\kern 4.99846pt}}_{{\kern-7.387pt{J}\kern 4.99846pt}}}h
for all
i
, then
w_{i}=F_{J}(g_{i})\leq F_{J}(h)
. This implies
F_{G}(g)=w=\bigvee_{i}w_{i}\leq F_{J}(h)
and so
g\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.82576pt{J}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.82576pt{J}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-5.69846pt\kern 6.08734pt}}_{{\kern-8.47588pt{J}\kern 6.08734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-4.60957pt\kern 4.99846pt}}_{{\kern-7.387pt{J}\kern 4.99846pt}}}h
.
This proves that
g=\bigvee_{i}g_{i}
. We have
F_{J}(g)=w
, proving (1).

Now we prove (2). Let
w^{\prime}:=\bigvee_{i}w_{i}
, which exists since
W
is a complete meet semilattice and
I\neq{\emptyset}
.
By Proposition
3.20
, for each
\alpha\in J
, there is a square

(s_{\alpha},z^{\prime}_{\alpha},w^{\prime},x^{\prime}_{\alpha})
. Hence there is a square
(w^{\prime},x^{\prime}_{\alpha},s_{\alpha},z^{\prime}_{\alpha})
. By Proposition
3.21
, there is a morphism
g^{\prime}=(J,w^{\prime},K^{\prime})
in
G
, where

K^{\prime}:=w^{\prime-1}J
. We check that
g^{\prime}=\bigwedge_{i}g_{i}
.
First,
g^{\prime}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.82576pt{J}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.82576pt{J}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-5.69846pt\kern 6.08734pt}}_{{\kern-8.47588pt{J}\kern 6.08734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-4.60957pt\kern 4.99846pt}}_{{\kern-7.387pt{J}\kern 4.99846pt}}}g_{i}
for all
i
since
F_{J}(g^{\prime})=w^{\prime}\leq w_{i}=F_{J}(g_{i})
.
Second, if
h^{\prime}\in\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-9.5255pt{J}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-9.5255pt{J}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-6.24115pt{J}\kern 3.85262pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-5.1404pt{J}\kern 2.75186pt}}}
with
h^{\prime}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.82576pt{J}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.82576pt{J}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-5.69846pt\kern 6.08734pt}}_{{\kern-8.47588pt{J}\kern 6.08734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-4.60957pt\kern 4.99846pt}}_{{\kern-7.387pt{J}\kern 4.99846pt}}}g_{i}
for all
i
, then
F_{J}(h^{\prime})\leq F_{J}(g_{i})=w_{i}
. This implies
F_{J}(h^{\prime})\leq\bigwedge_{i}w_{i}=w^{\prime}=F_{J}(g^{\prime})
and so
h^{\prime}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.82576pt{J}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.82576pt{J}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-5.69846pt\kern 6.08734pt}}_{{\kern-8.47588pt{J}\kern 6.08734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-4.60957pt\kern 4.99846pt}}_{{\kern-7.387pt{J}\kern 4.99846pt}}}g^{\prime}
.
This completes the proof that
g^{\prime}=\bigwedge_{i}g_{i}
.
Since
F_{J}(g^{\prime})=w^{\prime}
, this proves (2). ∎

Proof of Theorem
1.22
.

We prove Theorem
1.22
(a).
Propositions
1.5
and
3.14
prove that
(G,\Lambda)
is faithful and preprincipal and that its atomic morphisms are the Brink-Howlett generators. It remains to show that
(G,\Lambda)
is rootoidal. Let
J
be an object of
(G,\Lambda)
. Since
(W,\leq)
is a complete meet semilattice, the weak order
(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-9.5255pt{J}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-9.5255pt{J}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-6.24115pt{J}\kern 3.85262pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-5.1404pt{J}\kern 2.75186pt}}},\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.82576pt{J}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-8.13736pt\kern 8.52625pt}}_{{\kern-11.82576pt{J}\kern 8.52625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-5.69846pt\kern 6.08734pt}}_{{\kern-8.47588pt{J}\kern 6.08734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\leq^{{\kern-4.60957pt\kern 4.99846pt}}_{{\kern-7.387pt{J}\kern 4.99846pt}}})
at
J
is a complete meet semilattice by Proposition
3.22
(a). Finally, we prove the JOP, which is the assertion that if
(g_{i})_{i\in I}
and

h
are in
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-9.5255pt{J}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-5.8371pt\kern 6.22598pt}}_{{\kern-9.5255pt{J}\kern 6.22598pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-3.46373pt\kern 3.85262pt}}_{{\kern-6.24115pt{J}\kern 3.85262pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}G^{{\kern-2.36298pt\kern 2.75186pt}}_{{\kern-5.1404pt{J}\kern 2.75186pt}}}
are such that
\Lambda_{g_{i}}\cap\Lambda_{h}={\emptyset}
for all
i\in I
and
g:=\bigvee_{i\in I}g_{i}
exists, then
\Lambda_{g}\cap\Lambda_{h}={\emptyset}
.
Write
g_{i}=(J,w_{i},K_{i})
,
g=(J,w,K)
and
h=(J,v,L)
where
w_{i},w,v\in W
and
K_{i},K,L\in\operatorname{ob}(G)
. Then
w_{i}=F_{J}(g)
,
v=F_{J}(h)
and by Proposition
3.22
,

w=F_{J}(g)=\bigvee_{i}F_{J}(g_{i})=\bigvee_{i}w_{i}
.
We have

\Phi_{w_{i}}\cap\Phi_{v}=\Lambda_{g_{i}}\cap\Lambda_{h}={\emptyset}
.
Using the JOP for
(W_{\bullet},\Phi_{\bullet})
, we have

\Lambda_{g}\cap\Lambda_{h}=\Phi_{w}\cap\Phi_{v}={\emptyset}
,
proving the JOP for
(G,\Lambda)
.

Theorem
1.22
(b) is an immediate consequence of Theorem
1.22
(a) and
[
19
, Lemma 2.26(b)]
.
∎

4.
Abstract root systems of Brink-Howlett groupoids

Example 4.1
.

Suppose that
W
is finite. Let
J\subseteq S
. The unique
U^{\prime}\in M_{J}
containing

U\in R_{J}
is the parabolic closure of
U
, which is defined to be the intersection of all parabolic subgroups of
W
which contain
U
. One has
U^{\prime}\in P_{J}
and
\Phi_{U^{\prime}}=\operatorname{span}(\Phi_{U})\cap\Phi
. It follows that
M_{J}=P_{J}
(for finite
W
). In general,
P_{J}\neq R_{J}
; for instance, this is well known for
(W,S)
of type
B_{2}
.

Suppose instead that
W
is irreducible affine and
J=\{\alpha\}
where
\alpha\in\Pi
. Then
M_{J}
consists of the rank two, finite parabolic subgroups containing
s=s_{\alpha}\in S
, together with the unique
infinite maximal dihedral reflection subgroup
W^{\prime}
containing
s
. In the standard realization of
W
as Euclidean reflection group,
W^{\prime}
is generated by the reflections in the reflecting hyperplanes parallel (in the usual sense of Euclidean geometry) to the reflecting hyperplane for
s
. In particular,
M_{J}\supsetneq P_{J}
in this case unless
W
is of rank two (that is, type
\widetilde{A}_{1}
).

Proposition 4.2
.

(a)

P_{J}\subseteq M_{J}
and
Q_{J}=M_{J}\cap N_{J}
.

(b)

Inclusions as shown in the following Hasse diagram hold amongst the sets
Q_{J}
,
P_{J}
,
M_{J}
,

N_{J}
and
R_{J}
of corank one reflection overgroups of
W_{J}
:

\textstyle{R_{J}}
\textstyle{{M_{J}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{N_{J}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{P_{J}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{Q_{J}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Remarks
.

Additional inclusions may hold in particular cases. For instance, if
W
is finite, then
M_{J}=P_{J}
and
R_{J}=N_{J}
, while if
(W,S)
is of type
A
, then
R_{J}=P_{J}
.

Proof of Proposition
4.2
.

We prove (a). We have
P_{J}\subseteq M_{J}
by Theorem
1.30
(a). Hence
Q_{J}=P_{J}\cap N_{J}\subseteq M_{J}\cap N_{J}
. For the reverse inclusion, let
U\in M_{J}\cap N_{J}
. Since
(U\setminus W_{J})\cap T
is finite, Proposition
1.35
implies
[U:W_{J}]<\infty
. By Theorem
1.43
, there exists
U^{\prime}\in P_{J}\subseteq R_{J}
such that
U\subseteq U^{\prime}
. Since
U\in M_{J}
, we have
U^{\prime}=U
. Hence
U\in P_{J}\cap N_{J}
as required.

The inclusions diagrammed in (b) follow from (a) and the definitions.
∎

Proposition 4.3
.

Let
(K,w,J)
be a morphism in
G
and

U\in R_{J}
. Then:

(a)

r_{U,J}\not\in\Phi_{w^{-1}}
holds if and only if
((U\setminus W_{J})\cap T)\cap N(w^{-1})={\emptyset}
, and also if and only if
(\Phi^{+}_{U}\setminus\Phi^{+}_{J})\cap\Phi_{w^{-1}}={\emptyset}
.

(b)

r_{U,J}\in\Phi_{w^{-1}}
holds if and only if
((U\setminus W_{J})\cap T)\subseteq N(w^{-1})
, and also if and only if

\Phi^{+}_{U}\setminus\Phi^{+}_{J}\subseteq\Phi_{w^{-1}}
.

(c)

If
r_{U,J}\not\in\Phi_{w^{-1}}
, then
\Pi_{wUw^{-1}}=w\Pi_{U}
and

r_{wUw^{-1},K}=wr_{U,J}
.

(d)

Suppose
\alpha:=r_{U,J}\in\Phi_{w^{-1}}
. Let

L\dot{\cup}\{\alpha\}
be the component of
\Pi_{U}

containing
\alpha
, where
L\subseteq\Pi_{U}
. Then
u:=\nu(\alpha,J)=w_{L\cup\{\alpha\}}w_{L}\in U
is defined,

\Pi_{wUw^{-1}}=wu^{-1}\Pi_{U}
and

r_{wUw^{-1},K}=-ww_{L}r_{U,J}
.

Proof.

Make assumptions as in Proposition
4.3
. For the proof, there is no loss of generality in assuming that
\Pi
is linearly dependent. Then for any subset
I
of
\Pi
,
\Phi_{I}=\operatorname{span}(I)\cap\Phi
.

The map
\alpha\mapsto s_{\alpha}\colon\Phi^{+}\to T
is a bijection which maps
\Phi_{w^{-1}}
onto
N(w^{-1})
and

\Phi^{+}_{U}\setminus\Phi_{J}^{+}
onto
(U\setminus W_{J})\cap T
.
Hence
((U\setminus W_{J})\cap T)\cap N(w^{-1})={\emptyset}
if and only if
(\Phi^{+}_{U}\setminus\Phi^{+}_{J})\cap\Phi_{w^{-1}}={\emptyset}
.
Similarly,
((U\setminus W_{J})\cap T)\subseteq N(w^{-1})
if and only if

(\Phi^{+}_{U}\setminus\Phi^{+}_{J})\subseteq\Phi_{w^{-1}}
.

To prove (a)–(b), it will suffice, by the previous paragraph, to show the following three conditions (i)–(iii) are equivalent:

(i)

r_{U,J}\in\Phi_{w}^{-1}

(ii)

(\Phi^{+}_{U}\setminus\Phi^{+}_{J})\cap\Phi_{w^{-1}}\neq{\emptyset}

(iii)

\Phi_{U}^{+}\setminus\Phi_{J}^{+}\subseteq\Phi_{w^{-1}}

Since
r_{U,J}\in\Phi^{+}_{U}\setminus\Phi_{J}^{+}
, we have (i)
\implies
(ii) and also (iii)
\implies
(i) .

We prove (ii)
\implies
(i). Let
\beta\in(\Phi_{U}^{+}\setminus\Phi_{J}^{+})\cap\Phi_{w^{-1}}
. Then we may write

\beta=\sum_{\gamma\in\Pi_{U}}c_{\gamma}\gamma
where
c_{\gamma}\geq 0
for all
\gamma\in\Pi_{U}=J\cup\{r_{U,J}\}
and
c_{r_{U,J}}>0
. Since
w(\beta)=\sum_{\gamma\in\Pi_{U}}c_{\gamma}w(\gamma)\in\Phi^{-}
and
K:=w(J)\subseteq\Pi
, this implies that
w(r_{u,J})\in\Phi^{-}
. That is,
r_{U,J}\in\Phi_{w^{-1}}
as required.

Finally, we show that (i)
\implies
(iii). Assume that
r_{U,J}\in\Phi_{w^{-1}}
. For any
\beta^{\prime}\in(\Phi_{U}^{+}\setminus\Phi_{J}^{+})
, write

\beta^{\prime}=\sum_{\gamma\in\Pi_{U}}c^{\prime}_{\gamma}\gamma

where
c^{\prime}_{\gamma}\geq 0
for all
\gamma\in\Pi_{U}=J\cup\{r_{U,J}\}
and
c^{\prime}_{r_{U,J}}>0
. We have

w(J)\subseteq K
and
w(r_{U,J})\in\Phi^{-}\setminus w(\Phi_{J})=\Phi^{-}\setminus\Phi_{K}
, which implies that
w(\beta^{\prime})=\sum_{\gamma\in\Pi_{U}}c^{\prime}_{\gamma}\gamma\in\Phi^{-}
.
Hence
\beta^{\prime}\in\Phi_{w^{-1}}
, proving (iii).

This completes the proof of (a)–(b). We next prove (c).
Suppose that
r_{U,J}\not\in\Phi_{w^{-1}}
. It follows that

w\Pi_{U}=w(J\cup\{r_{J,K}\})=w(J)\cup\{w(r_{U,J})\}=K\cup\{w(r_{U,J})\}\subseteq\Phi^{+}.

This implies that
\Pi_{wUw^{-1}}=w\Pi_{U}=K\dot{\cup}\{wr_{U,J}\}
. Since
W_{K}=wW_{J}w^{-1}\subseteq wUw^{-1}
, it follows that
wUw^{-1}\in R_{K}
and so
\Pi_{wUw^{-1}}=K\dot{\cup}\{r_{wUw^{-1},K}\}
. Hence

r_{wUw^{-1},K}=w(r_{U,J})
, proving (c).

Finally, we prove (d). Assume
\alpha=r_{U,J}\in\Phi_{w^{-1}}
.
Since
|\Phi_{w^{-1}}|=\ell(w)<\infty
, (b) implies that
\Phi^{+}_{U}\setminus\Phi_{J}^{+}
is finite. By Propositions
1.25
and
1.35
, the component
L\cup\{\alpha\}
of
\Pi_{U}
is of finite type. Hence
u:=\nu(\alpha,L)=w_{L\cup\{\alpha\}}w_{L}
is defined in
(U,\chi(U))
. We have
\Pi_{U}=J\,\dot{\cup}\,\{\alpha\}=(J\setminus L)\cup(L\cup\{\alpha\})
.
where
(J\setminus L)\perp(L\cup\{\alpha\})
. Since
u\in W_{L\cup\{\alpha\}}
, we have
u^{-1}(J\setminus L)=J\setminus L
, and so

\begin{split}u^{-1}\Pi_{U}=&u^{-1}((J\setminus L)\cup(L\cup\{\alpha\}))=u^{-1}(J\setminus L)\cup u^{-1}(L\cup\{\alpha\})\\
=&(J\setminus L)\cup w_{L}w_{L\cup\{\alpha\}}(L\cup\{\alpha\})=(J\setminus L)\cup-w_{L}(L\cup\{\alpha\})\\
=&(J\setminus L)\cup(L\cup\{-w_{L}\alpha\})=J\cup\{-w_{L}\alpha\}.\end{split}

Now
-w_{L}\alpha\in-\alpha+\operatorname{span}(L)
.
Since
\alpha\not\in w_{-1}\Phi_{K}=\Phi_{J}
, it follows that
-w\alpha\in\Phi^{+}\setminus\Phi_{K}=\Phi^{+}\setminus\operatorname{span}(K)
. Since
w(\operatorname{span}(L))\subseteq w\operatorname{span}(J)=\operatorname{span}K
, this implies that
-ww_{L}\alpha\in\Phi^{+}
. Hence

wu^{-1}\Pi_{U}=wJ\cup\{-ww_{L}\alpha\}=K\cup\{-ww_{L}\alpha\}\subseteq\Phi^{+}
. This shows that
wu^{-1}\Pi_{U}=\Pi_{wu^{-1}U(wu^{-1})^{-1}}=\Pi_{wUw^{-1}}
. Since
U\in R_{J}
, we have
wUw^{-1}\in R_{wUw^{-1}}=R_{K}
and so

\Pi_{wUw^{-1}}=K\dot{\cup}\{r_{wUw^{-1},K}\}=K\cup\{-ww_{L}\alpha\}
. Therefore

r_{wUw^{-1},K}=-ww_{L}\alpha=-ww_{L}r_{U,J}
, which completes the proof of (d).
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.33
.

For each possible choice of
X
(i.e.
R
,
M
,
N
,
P
or
Q
) and each morphism
(K,w,J)
in
G
, and all
U\in X_{J}
, one has
wUw^{-1}\in X_{K}
.
Hence the formula for
\Upsilon_{X}(K,w,J)
in the statement of the theorem defines a function
X_{J}\times\{\pm\}\to X_{K}\times\{\pm\}
.
Straightforward calculation shows that the specified maps
\Upsilon_{X}
on objects and morphisms define a functor giving
a signed
G
-set
\Upsilon_{X}
provided that for any morphisms
(K,x,J)
and
(L,y,K)
of
G
and all
U\in X_{J}
, we have

\eta(yx,U,J)\equiv\eta(y,xUx^{-1},K)+\eta(x,U,J)\pmod{2}

Recall that
\eta(x,U,J)
is equal to
1
or
0
according as whether
(U\setminus W_{J})\cap T
is contained in, or disjoint from,

N(x^{-1})
(containment or disjointness are the only possibilities, by Proposition
4.3
). This implies similar descriptions of
\eta(y,xUx^{-1},K)
and
\eta(yx,U,J)
. Since
W_{K}=xW_{J}x^{-1}
, that description of
\eta(y,xUx^{-1},K)
implies that
\eta(y,xUx^{-1},K)
is equal to
1
or
0
according as whether
(U\setminus W_{J})\cap T
is contained in, or disjoint from,
x^{-1}N(y^{-1})x
. We relate
\eta(yx,U,J)
to

\eta(x,U,J)
and
\eta(y,xUx^{-1},K)
, by considering cases, using the
1
-cocycle formula

N((yx)^{-1})=N(x^{-1}y^{-1})=N(x^{-1})+x^{-1}N(y^{-1})x

(in which, recall,
+
denotes symmetric difference of sets:
A+B:=(A\cup B)\setminus(A\cap B)
).

Case 1
.

\eta(x,U,J)=0
and
\eta(y,xUx^{-1},K)=0
.

We have
(U\setminus W_{J})\cap N(x^{-1})={\emptyset}
and

(U\setminus W_{J})\cap x^{-1}N(y^{-1})x={\emptyset}
. The
1
-cocycle formula implies
(U\setminus W_{J})\cap N((yx)^{-1})={\emptyset}
, so

\eta(x,U,J)+\eta(y,xUx^{-1},K)=0=\eta(xy,U,J).

Case 2
.

\eta(x,U,J)=0
and
\eta(y,xUx^{-1},K)=1
.

We have
(U\setminus W_{J})\cap N(x^{-1})={\emptyset}
and

(U\setminus W_{J})\cap T\subseteq x^{-1}N(y^{-1})x
. The
1
-cocycle formula implies
(U\setminus W_{J})\cap T\subseteq N((yx)^{-1})
, so

\eta(x,U,J)+\eta(y,xUx^{-1},K)=1=\eta(xy,U,J).

Case 3
.

\eta(x,U,J)=1
and
\eta(y,xUx^{-1},K)=0
.

We have
(U\setminus W_{J})\cap T\subseteq N(x^{-1})
and

(U\setminus W_{J})\cap x^{-1}N(y^{-1})x={\emptyset}
. The
1
-cocycle formula implies
(U\setminus W_{J})\cap T\subseteq N((yx)^{-1})
, so

\eta(x,U,J)+\eta(y,xUx^{-1},K)=1=\eta(xy,U,J).

Case 4
.

\eta(x,U,J)=1
and
\eta(y,xUx^{-1},K)=1
.

We have
(U\setminus W_{J})\cap T\subseteq N(x^{-1})
and

(U\setminus W_{J})\cap T\subseteq x^{-1}N(y^{-1})x
. The
1
-cocycle formula implies
(U\setminus W_{J})\cap N((yx)^{-1})={\emptyset}
, so

\eta(x,U,J)+\eta(y,xUx^{-1},K)=2\equiv 0=\eta(xy,U,J)\pmod{2}.

This completes the proof that
\Upsilon_{X}
as defined in Theorem
1.33
is a signed
G
-set .

For the proof of
1.33
(a), let
J\in\operatorname{ob}(G)
and
(U,\epsilon)\in\Upsilon_{X}(J)
. Suppose first that
(U,\epsilon)
is a real root. This means that there is a morphism
(K,w,J)
in
G
such that

(\Upsilon_{X}(K,w,J))(U,\epsilon)=(wUw^{-1},-\epsilon)
. By definition of
\Upsilon_{X}
, we have
(U\setminus W_{J})\cap T\subseteq N(w^{-1})
. Since
N(w^{-1})
is finite,
(U\setminus W_{J})\cap T
is also finite.

Conversely, suppose
(U\setminus W_{J})\cap T
is finite. By Proposition
1.35
, the reflection subgroup
U
of
W
is a finite index overgroup of
W_{J}
. By Theorem
1.43
, there is a parabolic subgroup
U^{\prime}\subseteq U
of
W
such that
[U^{\prime}:W_{J}]
is finite and, moreover,

\corank_{U^{\prime}}(W_{J})=\corank_{U}(W_{J})=1
. We have
J\subseteq\Pi_{U^{\prime}}
since
W_{J}\subseteq U^{\prime}
. Since
U^{\prime}
is parabolic, there exists
w\in W
with
w(\Pi_{U^{\prime}})\subseteq\Pi
. Let
K=w(J)\subseteq\Pi
. Then
\Pi{\,\supseteq\,}\Pi_{wU^{\prime}w^{-1}}=w\Pi_{U^{\prime}}{\,\supseteq\,}wJ=K
.
Since
\corank_{wU^{\prime}w^{-1}}(W_{K})=\corank_{U^{\prime}}(W_{J})=1
, we may write
w(\Pi_{U^{\prime}})\setminus K=\{\alpha\}
where
\alpha\in\Pi
(so by definition,
\alpha=r_{wU^{\prime}w^{-1},K}
).

Note that
(K,w,J)
is a morphism of
G
. Now since
w(\Pi_{U^{\prime}})\subseteq\Pi
, we have

w\Phi^{+}_{U}\subseteq w\Phi^{+}_{U^{\prime}}=\Phi^{+}_{wU^{\prime}w^{-1}}\subseteq\Phi^{+}
.
This implies
(U\setminus W_{J})\cap N(w^{-1})={\emptyset}
and so

(\Upsilon_{X}(K,w,J))(U,\epsilon)=(wUw^{-1},\epsilon)\in X_{K}\times\{\pm\}
.

We have
[wU^{\prime}w^{-1}:W_{K}]=[U^{\prime}:W_{J}]<\infty
, so
\Phi^{+}_{K\cup\{\alpha\}}\setminus\Phi^{+}_{K}=\Phi^{+}_{wU^{\prime}w^{-1}}\setminus\Phi^{+}_{K}
is finite by Proposition
1.35
. Hence

y:=\nu(\alpha,K)\in W
is defined and there is a morphism

(L,y,K)
in
G
where
L:=yK\subseteq\Pi
.
Note that

N(y^{-1})=(W_{K\cup\{\alpha\}}\setminus W_{K})\cap T=(wU^{\prime}w^{-1}\setminus W_{K})\cap T{\,\supseteq\,}(wUw^{-1}\setminus W_{K})\cap T.

Hence
(\Upsilon_{X}(L,y,K))(wUw^{-1},\epsilon)=((yw)U(yw)^{-1},-\epsilon)\in X_{L}\times\{\pm\}
.
The composite morphism
(L,yw,J)=(L,y,K)(K,w,J)
of
G
satisfies
(\Upsilon_{X}(L,yw,J))(U,\epsilon)=(ywU(yw)^{-1},-\epsilon)
. By the definitions,
(U,\epsilon)
is a real root, proving (a).

Note that the functors
\Upsilon_{X}
are all subfunctors of
\Upsilon_{R}
(as set-valued functors). Theorem
1.33
(b) then follows from Proposition
4.2
(b).

We prove (c). The real roots of
(G,\Upsilon_{X})
based at
J
are, by part (a), the roots of the form
(U,\epsilon)
where
U\in X_{J}
,
\epsilon\in\{\pm\}
and
(U\setminus W_{J})\cap T
is finite. By definition of
N
, the only condition on
U
is just that
U\in N_{J}\cap X_{J}
. The assertions of (c) follow since
N_{J}\cap R_{J}=N_{J}
(recall that
N_{J}\subseteq R_{J}
),
N_{J}\cap M_{J}=Q_{J}
by Proposition
4.2
(a), and
N_{J}\cap P_{J}=Q_{J}
by definition.

For the proof of (d), we shall define a signed
G
-subset
(G,\Xi)
of
(G,\Lambda)
and morphisms of signed sets
\tau\colon(G,\Upsilon_{R})\to(G,\Upsilon_{M})
and
\rho\colon(G,\Xi)\to(G,\Upsilon_{M})
.

For a morphism
(K,w,J)
in
G
, we have
\Lambda(J)=\Phi{\,\supseteq\,}\Phi_{J}
and
(\Lambda(K,w,J))(\Phi_{J}^{\pm})=\Phi_{K}^{\pm}
.
This implies that there is a signed
G
-subset
(G,\Xi)

of
(G,\Lambda)
such that for any
J\in\operatorname{ob}(G)
, the
underlying set of
\Xi(J)
is
\Xi(J):=\Phi\setminus\Phi_{J}\subseteq\Phi=\Lambda(J)
. The above also shows that

\Phi_{J}^{\pm}\subseteq\Lambda^{{\mathrm{im}}}_{J}
for
J\in\operatorname{ob}(G)
and hence

(4.3.1)

(G,\Lambda^{{\mathrm{re}}})=(G,\Xi^{{\mathrm{re}}}),\qquad(G,\Lambda^{{\mathrm{rec}}})=(G,\Xi^{{\mathrm{rec}}}).

Define a morphism of signed
G
-sets
\rho\colon(G,\Xi)\to(G,\Upsilon_{M})
as follows. For any
J\in\operatorname{ob}(G)

and
\alpha\in\Xi(J)=\Phi\setminus\Phi_{J}
, Theorem

1.30
implies that there exist unique
U\in M_{J}
and

\epsilon\in\{\pm\}
such that
\alpha\in\epsilon(\Phi^{+}_{U}\setminus\Phi_{J}^{+})
.
Define
\rho_{J}\colon\Xi(J)\to\Upsilon_{M}(J)
by

\rho_{J}(\alpha)=(U,\epsilon)
. This defines a
morphism of signed
G
-sets, since, for a morphism
(K,w,J)
in
G
, and
\alpha,U,\epsilon

as above, we have

w\alpha\in w\epsilon(\Phi^{+}_{U}\setminus\Phi_{J}^{+})=(-1)^{\nu(w,U,J)}\epsilon(\Phi^{+}_{wUw^{-1}}\setminus\Phi_{K}^{+}),

where the equation holds as
\nu(w,U,J)=1
if
\Phi^{+}_{U}\setminus\Phi_{J}^{+}\subseteq\Phi_{w^{-1}}
and
\nu(w,U,J)=0
otherwise, in which case
(\Phi^{+}_{U}\setminus\Phi_{J}^{+})\cap\Phi_{w^{-1}}={\emptyset}
.

For each object
J
of
G
, the component
\rho_{J}
is surjective. From (
3.5
), we see that
\rho^{{\mathrm{c}}}\colon\Xi^{{\mathrm{c}}}\to\Upsilon_{M}^{{\mathrm{c}}}
is an isomorphism. Hence so is
\rho^{{\mathrm{rec}}}\colon\Xi^{{\mathrm{rec}}}\to\Upsilon_{M}^{{\mathrm{rec}}}
. By (
4.3.1
) and (c),

(4.3.2)

(G,\Lambda^{{\mathrm{rec}}})=(G,\Xi^{{\mathrm{rec}}})\cong(G,\Upsilon_{M}^{{\mathrm{rec}}})=(G,\Upsilon_{P}^{{\mathrm{rec}}})=(G,\Upsilon_{Q}^{{\mathrm{c}}}).

Define a morphism
\tau\colon(G,\Upsilon_{R})\to(G,\Upsilon_{M})
as follows. For any object
J
of
G
and element
U
of
R_{J}
, let
U_{[J]}\in M_{J}
denote the maximum corank-one reflection overgroup of
W_{J}
such that
U\subseteq U_{[J]}
, which exists by Theorem
1.30
.
Let
\tau_{J}\colon\Upsilon_{R}(J)\to\Upsilon_{M}(J)
denote the map
(U,\epsilon)\mapsto(U_{[J]},\epsilon)\colon R_{J}\times\{\pm\}\to(M_{J}\times\{\pm\})
. There is a natural transformation
\tau=(\tau_{J})_{J\in\operatorname{ob}(G)}\colon\Upsilon_{R}\to\Upsilon_{M}
(of underlying functors from
G
to the category of sets). To see this, one notes that for a morphism
(K,w,G)
in
G
, one has
(wUw^{-1})_{[K]}=w(U_{[J]})w^{-1}
and
\eta(w,U,J)=\eta(w,U_{[J]},J)
for
J\in\operatorname{ob}(G)
and
U\in R_{J}
. The components
\tau_{J}
of
\tau
are positivity preserving morphisms of signed sets, so

\tau
defines a morphism
(G,\Upsilon_{R})\to(G,\Upsilon_{M})
. The composite

(G,\Upsilon_{M})\hookrightarrow(G,\Upsilon_{R})\xrightarrow{\tau}(G,\Upsilon_{M})
is the identity morphism since for
J\in\operatorname{ob}(G)
and
U\in M_{J}
, one has
U_{[J]}=U
.
In particular, the (underlying maps of sets) of the components of (the natural transformation)
\tau
are surjective. From
3.5
, it follows that
\tau^{{\mathrm{c}}}\colon\Upsilon_{R}^{{\mathrm{c}}}\to\Upsilon_{M}^{{\mathrm{c}}}
is an isomorphism and hence there are morphisms

(4.3.3)

\tau^{{\mathrm{rec}}}\colon(G,\Upsilon_{R}^{{\mathrm{rec}}})\xrightarrow{\cong}(G,\Upsilon_{M}^{{\mathrm{rec}}}),\quad\tau^{{\mathrm{re}}}\colon(G,\Upsilon_{N})\to(G,\Upsilon_{Q}).

where in writing the domain and codomain of
\tau^{{\mathrm{re}}}
, we use (c).

By (
4.3.2
), we have
(G,\Lambda^{{\mathrm{rec}}})\cong(G,\Upsilon_{M}^{{\mathrm{rec}}})\cong(G,\Upsilon_{Q}^{{\mathrm{c}}})
. Since
(G,\Lambda)
is preprincipal, Proposition
3.7
implies that
(G,\Upsilon_{Q})
is real and preprincipal, with the same atomic generators (namely, the Brink-Howlett generators) as
(G,\Lambda)
. We show that
(G,\Upsilon_{Q})
is compressed. By Proposition
3.8
, this holds if and only if the inversion set

(\Upsilon_{Q})_{s}
of each Brink Howlett generator
s
of
G
is a singleton set.

Write
s=(J,w,K)
where
J\subseteq\Pi
,
w\in W
and
K=wJ
. There exists
\alpha\in\Pi\setminus J
such that
w=(\nu(\alpha,J))^{-1}
, and then
\Phi_{w}=\Phi_{J\cup\{\alpha\}}^{+}\setminus\Phi^{+}_{J}
.
Now a root
(U,\epsilon)\in(\Upsilon_{Q})(J)=Q_{J}\times\{\pm\}
lies in the inversion set
(\Upsilon_{Q})_{s}
if and only if it is positive and

(\Upsilon(J,w,K))(U,\epsilon)=(wUw^{-1},-\epsilon)
.
This holds if and only if
\epsilon=+
and
\Phi_{U}^{+}\setminus\Phi_{J}^{+}\subseteq\Phi_{w^{-1}}=\Phi_{J\cup\{\alpha\}}^{+}\setminus\Phi^{+}_{J}
, where the last condition implies
U\subseteq W_{J\cup\{\alpha\}}
. Since
U
is a parabolic subgroup which is a corank one reflection overgroup of
W_{J}
Theorem
1.30
(a) (for instance) implies
U=W_{J\cup\{\alpha\}}
. Hence

(\Upsilon_{Q})_{s}=\{(W_{J\cup\{\alpha\}},+)\}
is a singleton set, as required, and
(G,\Upsilon_{N})\cong(G,\Upsilon_{N}^{{\mathrm{c}}})
is compressed.

Using the fact that
(G,\Upsilon_{N})
is compressed, we obtain from (
4.3.2
) and (
4.3.3
) all the assertions of (d) except that
(G,\Upsilon_{N}^{{\mathrm{c}}})\cong(G,\Upsilon_{Q})
. But since the components of
\tau
are surjective, so are those of
\tau^{{\mathrm{re}}}
(because the components preserve both real and imaginary roots). From (
3.5
), we see that
\tau^{{\mathrm{rec}}}
induces an isomorphism
(G,\Upsilon_{N}^{{\mathrm{c}}})\to(G,\Upsilon_{Q}^{{\mathrm{c}}})\cong(G,\Upsilon_{Q})
. This completes the proof of the theorem. ∎

Remark 4.4
.

The fibers of the components of
\tau^{{\mathrm{re}}}
are the parallelism classes of
(G,\Upsilon_{N})
.
Concretely, for each object
J
of
G
, the parallelism classes
of
(G,\Upsilon_{N})
at the object
J
of
G
are the sets

\{\,(U^{\prime},\epsilon)\mid U^{\prime}\in N_{J},U^{\prime}\subseteq U\,\}
for
\epsilon\in\{\pm\}
and
U^{\prime}\in Q_{J}
.

Corollary 4.5
.

Any inversion set for a signed groupoid set
(G,\Upsilon_{X})
as in Theorem
1.33
is a finite set.

Proof.

It is sufficient to prove that the inversion set

\Upsilon_{X,g}=\Upsilon_{X}(K)^{+}\cap g(\Upsilon_{X}(J)^{-})
is finite, where
g=(K,w,J)
is a morphism in
G
. By Theorem
1.33
, this inversion set consists of all roots
(U,+)
where
U\in X_{J}
satisfies
(U\setminus W_{J})\cap T\subseteq N(w^{-1})
. Recall that
N(w^{-1})
is a finite set. Since
U=\langle\,U\cap T\,\rangle
and
U\cap T=(W_{J\cap T})\cup(U\setminus W_{J})\cap T
, it follows that there are only finitely many possibilities for
U
.
∎

4.6.

Proposition
4.7
below will be used in studying realizations (of root systems of Brink-Howlett groupoids) with particularly favorable properties. In formulating it, we use the notation of
1.39
.

Let
G
be a connected Brink-Howlett groupoid of
(W,S)
. For any object
J\subseteq\Pi
of
G
, let
\Sigma_{J}
denote the union of
J_{\mathrm{inf}}
with the set of all vertices of
\Pi\setminus J
which are joined (by an edge of the Coxeter graph of
(W,S)
) either to a vertex of
J_{\mathrm{inf}}
or to infinitely many vertices of
J_{\mathrm{fin}}
. Let
E_{J}
be the subspace of
V
which consists of all vectors
v
in
V
such that
B(v,\beta)=0
for all
\beta\in J_{\mathrm{inf}}
and
B(v,\beta)\neq 0
for only finitely many
\beta\in J_{\mathrm{fin}}
. Thus,
\Pi\setminus\Sigma_{J}=\Pi\cap E_{J}
.

Proposition 4.7
.

Let notation be as in
4.6
.

(a)

Let
J,K\in\operatorname{ob}(G)
. Then
J_{\mathrm{inf}}=K_{\mathrm{inf}}
, the symmetric difference
J_{\mathrm{fin}}+K_{\mathrm{fin}}
is finite,

\Sigma_{J}=\Sigma_{K}
and
E_{J}=E_{K}
. Then
\Sigma^{\prime}:=J_{\inf}
,
\Sigma:=\Sigma_{J}
and
E:=E_{J}
are well-defined (independent of
J
). Further,
\Sigma^{\prime}\subseteq\Sigma
and
E\perp\Sigma^{\prime}
.

(b)

\Phi^{\prime}:=E\cap\Phi
is a standard parabolic root subsystem of
\Phi
with canonical simple system
\Pi^{\prime}=E\cap\Pi=\Pi\setminus\Sigma
. Denote the corresponding Coxeter system as
(W^{\prime},S^{\prime})
. Then
W^{\prime}=W_{\Phi^{\prime}}=W_{\Pi^{\prime}}
,
\Phi^{\prime}=\Phi_{W^{\prime}}
,
\Pi^{\prime}=\Pi_{W^{\prime}}
and

S^{\prime}=\chi(W^{\prime})=\{\,s_{\beta}\mid\beta\in\Pi^{\prime}\,\}
.

(c)

There is a component
G^{\prime}
, with
\operatorname{ob}(G^{\prime})=\{\,J_{\mathrm{fin}}\mid J\in\operatorname{ob}(G)\,\}
, of the full Brink-Howlett groupoid of
(W^{\prime},S^{\prime})
, and a groupoid isomorphism

\theta\colon G\to G^{\prime}
given by
J\mapsto J_{\mathrm{fin}}
on objects and

(K,w,J)\mapsto(K_{\mathrm{fin}},w,J_{\mathrm{fin}})
on morphisms,
which induces a bijection between the set of Brink-Howlett generators of
G
and that of
G^{\prime}
.

(d)

Let
(G,\Lambda)
be as defined from
(W,S)
,
\Phi
and
G
as in
1.21
and let
(G^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime})
be defined analogously from
(W^{\prime},S^{\prime})
,
G^{\prime}
and
\Phi^{\prime}
. Consider the real compressions
(G,\Lambda^{{\mathrm{rec}}})
and
(G^{\prime},(\Lambda^{\prime})^{{\mathrm{rec}}})
. Then there is an equality of signed
G
-sets
(G,\Lambda^{{\mathrm{rec}}})=(G,(\Lambda^{\prime})^{{\mathrm{rec}}}\circ\theta)
.

Remark
.

For
K\in\operatorname{ob}(G^{\prime})
, no vertex of the Coxeter graph of
\Pi^{\prime}
is joined to infinitely many elements of
K
, and all components of the Coxeter graph of
K
are spherical.

Proof.

Since
G
is connected and generated by its
Brink-Howlett generators, induction reduces the proof of the assertions of (a) involving both
J
and
K
to the case in which there
exists a Brink-Howlett generator
(K,w,J)
of
G
, say with

w=\nu(\alpha,J)
. Then
w^{-1}=\nu(K,\beta)
where
J\,\dot{\cup}\,\{\alpha\}=K\,\dot{\cup}\,\{\beta\}
. Denote the
component of
J\cup\{\alpha\}
containing
\alpha
as
L\,\dot{\cup}\,\{\alpha\}
where
L\subseteq J
. Then

L\cup\{\alpha\}
is of finite type, so
L
is a union of finitely
many components of
J_{\mathrm{fin}}
. It follows that
\alpha\not\in\Sigma_{J}
and
L\subseteq J_{\mathrm{fin}}\subseteq\Pi\setminus\Sigma_{J}
.
Hence

(4.7.1)

w=w_{L\cup\{\alpha\}}w_{L}\in W_{L\cup\{\alpha\}}\subseteq W_{\Pi\setminus\Sigma_{J}}.

Since
J\setminus L\perp L\cup\{\alpha\}
, we have

K=w(J)=w(J\setminus L)\,\dot{\cup}\,w(L)=(J\setminus L)\,\dot{\cup}\,w(L)
where

w(L)\subseteq L\cup\{\alpha\}
is of finite type and
w(L)\perp(J\setminus L)
. This implies that
K_{\mathrm{inf}}=J_{\mathrm{inf}}
.
Using
+
for symmetric difference, we therefore have

\begin{split}J_{\mathrm{fin}}+K_{\mathrm{fin}}&=(J_{\mathrm{fin}}+J_{\mathrm{inf}})+(K+K_{\mathrm{inf}})=J+K\\
&=((J\,\dot{\cup}\,\{\alpha\})+\{\alpha\})+((K\,\dot{\cup}\,\{\beta\})+\{\beta\})=\{\alpha\}+\{\beta\}\end{split}

which is finite. This proves the assertions of (a) involving both
J
and
K
. The other assertions of (a) follow directly using the definitions.

The first assertion of (b) is proved by noting that

\Phi^{\prime\prime}:=\Phi\cap(J_{\mathrm{inf}})^{\perp}
is a standard parabolic root subsystem of
\Phi
by Proposition
1.41
(a), and that,
since
J_{\mathrm{fin}}\subseteq\Phi^{\prime\prime}\cap\Pi
,

\Phi^{\prime}=\{\,\beta\in\Phi^{\prime\prime}\mid B(\beta,\gamma)\neq 0\text{ \rm for only finitely many $\gamma\in J_{\mathrm{fin}}$}\,\}

is a standard parabolic root subsystem of
\Phi^{\prime\prime}
by Proposition
1.41
(b). The rest of (b) follows using basic facts about reflection subgroups.

Part (c) is from
[
9
]
, but we give a proof for completeness. We claim first that for any morphism

(K,w,J)
of
G
, we have
w\in W^{\prime}
. It suffices to prove the
claim in case
(K,w,J)
is a Brink-Howlett generator of
G
, say with
w=\nu(\alpha,J)
as in the proof of (a). In that proof, the component
L\cup\{\alpha\}
of
J\cup\{\alpha\}
is contained in

J_{\mathrm{fin}}\cup\{\alpha\}
, so
it is the component of
J_{\mathrm{fin}}\,\dot{\cup}\,\{\alpha\}
containing
\alpha
. Hence
\nu(\alpha,J\setminus\Sigma)
is defined in
(W^{\prime},S^{\prime})
and is equal to
w_{L\cup\{\alpha\}}w_{L}=w
,
by (
4.7.1
). This proves the claim. Note also that
w(J_{\mathrm{fin}})=K_{\mathrm{fin}}
since
w(J)=K
and
w=\nu(\alpha,J)=\nu(\alpha,L)=\nu(\alpha,J_{\mathrm{fin}})
, so
(K_{\mathrm{fin}},w,J_{\mathrm{fin}})
is a Brink-Howlett morphism of the full Brink-Howlett groupoid
H
of
(W^{\prime},S^{\prime})
.

In fact, for any morphism
(K,w,J)
of
G
, we have
w(J)=K
and therefore
w(J_{\mathrm{fin}})=K_{\mathrm{fin}}
and
w(J_{\inf})=K_{\inf}
(i.e.
w(\Sigma^{\prime})=\Sigma^{\prime}
). It follows from the claim that the maps

J\mapsto J_{\mathrm{fin}}
on objects and
(K,w,J)\mapsto(K_{\mathrm{fin}},w,J_{\mathrm{fin}})
on morphisms define a homomorphism
\theta^{\prime}
of groupoids from
G
to
H
. It is an embedding (that is,
\theta^{\prime}
is injective both on objects and on morphisms) since for
J\in\operatorname{ob}(G)
, we have
J=J_{\mathrm{fin}}\,\dot{\cup}\,J_{\inf}=J_{\mathrm{fin}}\,\dot{\cup}\,\Sigma^{\prime}
. Moreover, the proof of the claim shows that
\theta^{\prime}
sends Brink-Howlett generators of
G
to Brink-Howlett generators of
H
.

To complete the proof of (c), it will suffice to show that if

(K^{\prime},w,J^{\prime})
is a Brink-Howlett generator of
H
, where

J^{\prime}=\theta(J)=J_{\mathrm{fin}}
with
J\in\operatorname{ob}(G)
, then there exists

K
in
\operatorname{ob}(G)
such that
\theta^{\prime}(K)=K^{\prime}
,
(K,w,J)

is a Brink-Howlett generator of
G
and
\theta^{\prime}(K,w,J)=(K^{\prime},w,J^{\prime})
.
Write
w=\nu(\alpha,J^{\prime})
with
\alpha\in\Pi^{\prime}\setminus J^{\prime}
and let

L\,\dot{\cup}\,\{\alpha\}
, with
L\subseteq J^{\prime}
, denote the (spherical) component of
J^{\prime}\,\dot{\cup}\,\{\alpha\}
which contains
\alpha
. Now

J=J^{\prime}\,\dot{\cup}\,J_{\mathrm{inf}}
where
J^{\prime}=J_{\mathrm{fin}}\perp J_{\mathrm{inf}}
and
\alpha\in\Pi^{\prime}=\Pi\setminus\Sigma
so
\alpha\perp J_{\mathrm{inf}}
(in particular,
\alpha\not\in J_{\mathrm{inf}}
). Hence
J_{\mathrm{inf}}\perp L\,\dot{\cup}\,\{\alpha\}
. Thus,

L\,\dot{\cup}\,\{\alpha\}
is the component of
J\,\dot{\cup}\,\{\alpha\}
containing
\alpha
, and
\nu(\alpha,J)=\nu(\alpha,L)=\nu(\alpha,J^{\prime})=w
are all defined.
Let
K:=w(J)
, so
(K,w,J)
is a Brink-Howlett generator of
G
and
K\in\operatorname{ob}(G)
.
Then
\theta(K,w,J)=(\theta(K),w,J_{\mathrm{fin}})=(\theta(K),w,J^{\prime})
is a morphism of
H
. Since
(K^{\prime},w,J^{\prime})
is also a morphism of
H
, we get
K^{\prime}=\theta(K)
, completing the proof of (c).

The proof of (d) uses Proposition
3.8
(a).
For an object
J
of
G
, the result implies that the roots in
\Lambda^{{\mathrm{rec}}}(J)
are the subsets of
\Phi
of the form
w(\Phi_{s})
, where
(J,w,K)
is a morphism of
G
and
(K,s,L)
is an atomic morphism (i.e. Brink-Howlett generator) of
(G,\Lambda)
. Such a root is positive if
w(\Phi_{s})\subseteq\Phi^{+}
and negative otherwise i.e. if
w(\Phi_{s})\subseteq\Phi^{-}
. For a morphism
(L,u,J)
of
G
, one has

(\Lambda^{{\mathrm{rec}}}(L,u,J))(w(\Phi_{s}))=(uw)(\Phi_{s})
. The description for the signed
G
-set
(\Lambda^{\prime})^{{\mathrm{rec}}}\circ\theta
is essentially the same,
using inversion sets in the standard parabolic subsystem
\Phi^{\prime}
of
\Phi
instead of in
\Phi
. This gives the equality as desired since, for any morphism
(K,s,L)
of
G
, atomic or not, we have
s\in W^{\prime}
, and for any
s\in W^{\prime}
, we have
\Phi^{\prime}_{s}=\Phi_{s}
.
∎

Proposition 4.8
.

Let assumptions and notation be as in Proposition
4.7
. Let
X
denote either
R
,
M
,
N
,
P
or
Q
as in Theorem
1.33
.
For
J\subseteq\Pi
, let
X_{J}
denote the set of rank one reflection overgroups of
W_{J}
in
(W,S)
as in
4.7
, and for
J\subseteq\Pi^{\prime}
, let

X^{\prime}_{J}
denote the corresponding set of reflection overgroups of
W_{J}^{\prime}
in
(W^{\prime},S^{\prime})
. Let
(G,\Upsilon_{X})
and
(G^{\prime},\Upsilon^{\prime}_{X^{\prime}})
denote the signed
G
-set as in Theorem
1.33
and the analogously defined signed
G^{\prime}
-set, respectively. This gives two signed
G^{\prime}
-sets

(G^{\prime},\Upsilon_{X}\circ\theta^{-1})
and
(G^{\prime},\Upsilon^{\prime}_{X^{\prime}})
where
\theta\colon G\xrightarrow{\cong}G^{\prime}
is the isomorphism in Proposition
4.7
.

(a)

For
K
in
\operatorname{ob}(G^{\prime})
and
U^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}_{K}
, there is a
reflection subgroup
U=\iota_{K}(U^{\prime})\in X_{K\cup\Sigma}
such that
\Pi_{U}=\Pi^{\prime}_{U^{\prime}}\dot{\cup}\Sigma^{\prime}
.
One has

\Phi_{U}=\Phi^{\prime}_{U^{\prime}}\,\dot{\cup}\,\Phi_{W_{\Sigma^{\prime}}}
.

(b)

There is an embedding
\rho=\rho_{X}\colon(G^{\prime},\Upsilon^{\prime}_{X^{\prime}})\hookrightarrow(G^{\prime},\Upsilon_{X}\circ\theta^{-1})
of signed
G^{\prime}
-sets
where for
K\in\operatorname{ob}(G^{\prime})
the component

\rho_{K}\colon\Upsilon^{\prime}_{X^{\prime}}(K)\to\Upsilon_{X}(K\cup\Sigma^{\prime})
of
\rho
is
defined by
\rho_{K}(U^{\prime},\epsilon)=(\iota_{K}(U^{\prime}),\epsilon)

for all
U^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}_{K}
and
\epsilon\in\{\pm\}
.

(c)

If
X=N
or
X=Q
, then

\rho_{X}\colon(G^{\prime},\Upsilon^{\prime}_{X^{\prime}})\xrightarrow{\cong}(G^{\prime},\Upsilon_{X}\circ\theta^{-1})
is an isomorphism of signed
G^{\prime}
-sets.

Proof.

We prove (a). Let
K
in
\operatorname{ob}(G^{\prime})
and

U^{\prime}\in R^{\prime}_{K}
be any corank one
reflection overgroup of
W^{\prime}_{K}
in

W^{\prime}
. Then
\Pi^{\prime}_{U^{\prime}}\subseteq\Phi_{W^{\prime}}=\Phi^{\prime}
.
We have
\Pi^{\prime}_{U^{\prime}}\perp\Sigma^{\prime}
since
\Phi^{\prime}\subseteq E\perp\Sigma^{\prime}
. Since
\Pi^{\prime}\cup\Sigma^{\prime}\subseteq\Phi^{+}
, Theorem (
2.9
) implies that
there is a reflection subgroup
U
of
W
with
\Pi_{U}=\Pi^{\prime}_{U^{\prime}}\dot{\cup}\Sigma^{\prime}
. Since
\Pi^{\prime}_{U^{\prime}}\perp\Sigma^{\prime}
,
both
\Pi^{\prime}_{U^{\prime}}
and
\Sigma^{\prime}
are unions of components of

\Pi_{U}
, so
\Phi_{U}=\Phi^{\prime}_{U^{\prime}}\,\dot{\cup}\,\Phi_{W_{\Sigma^{\prime}}}

and
U=U^{\prime}W_{\Sigma^{\prime}}\cong U\times W_{\Sigma^{\prime}}
.
Also,
\Pi_{U}=\Pi^{\prime}_{U^{\prime}}\dot{\cup}\Sigma^{\prime}{\,\supseteq\,}K\,\dot{\cup}\,\Sigma^{\prime}

with
|\Pi_{U}\setminus(K\,\dot{\cup}\,\Sigma^{\prime})|=|\Pi^{\prime}_{U^{\prime}}\setminus K|=1
since
U^{\prime}\in R^{\prime}_{K}
is a corank one reflection overgroup of
W^{\prime}_{K}
. Hence
U
is a corank one reflection overgroup of
W_{K\cup\Sigma^{\prime}}
. That is,
U\in R_{K\cup\Sigma^{\prime}}
. To complete the proof of (a), it remains to show that if
U^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}_{K}\subseteq R^{\prime}_{K}

where
X
denotes
M
,
N
,
P
or
Q
, then

U\in X_{K\cup\Sigma^{\prime}}
(recall
1.32
).

Suppose first that
X=N
. Let
U^{\prime}\in N^{\prime}_{K}
. Then
\Phi^{\prime+}_{U^{\prime}}\setminus\Phi^{{}^{\prime}+}_{K}
is finite. By Proposition
1.35
,
we have
[U^{\prime}:W_{K}]<\infty
. Hence
[U:W_{K\cup\Sigma^{\prime}}]=[U^{\prime}\times W_{\Sigma^{\prime}}:W_{K}\times W_{\Sigma^{\prime}}]=[U^{\prime}:W_{K}]
is finite, and so

\Phi^{+}_{U}\setminus\Phi^{+}_{K\cup\Sigma^{\prime}}
is finite by Proposition
1.35
again. Hence
U\in N_{K\cup\Sigma^{\prime}}
.

Next, suppose that
X=P
. Let
U^{\prime}\in P^{\prime}_{K}
Then there is
w\in W^{\prime}
and
L\subseteq\Pi^{\prime}
such that
w(L)=\Pi^{\prime}_{U^{\prime}}{\,\supseteq\,}K
.
Let
J:=w^{-1}K\subseteq L
. Then
(K,w,J)
is a morphism in
G^{\prime}
.
Hence
(K\dot{\cup}\Sigma^{\prime},w,J\dot{\cup}\Sigma^{\prime})
is a morphism in
G
. Note that
W_{L\dot{\cup}\Sigma^{\prime}}
is a rank one reflection overgroup of
W_{J}
and it is a standard parabolic subgroup of
W
. Hence
W_{w(L\dot{\cup}\Sigma)}=W_{wL\dot{\cup}\Sigma}=W_{\Pi_{U^{\prime}}\dot{\cup}\Sigma}=U
is a parabolic subgroup of
W
, showing that
U\in P_{K\cup\Sigma}
.

The next case we consider is that when
X=Q
. Let
U^{\prime}\in Q^{\prime}_{K}
. That is,
U^{\prime}\in N^{\prime}_{K}
and
U^{\prime}\in P^{\prime}_{K}
. By the previous two paragraphs, we have
U\in N_{K}
and
U\in P_{K}
, so
U\in Q_{K}
as required.

The final case in the proof of (a) is that in which
X=M
.
Let
U^{\prime}\in M^{\prime}_{K}
. That is,
U^{\prime}
is a maximal corank one reflection overgroup of
W^{\prime}_{K}
in
W^{\prime}
. Write
\Pi^{\prime}_{U^{\prime}}=K\dot{\cup}\{\alpha\}
where
\alpha\in\Phi_{W^{\prime}}^{+}
. Then
\Pi_{U}=K\dot{\cup}\{\alpha\}\dot{\cup}\Sigma^{\prime}
, so

\alpha=r_{U,K\,\dot{\cup}\,\Sigma^{\prime}}
. Let

U^{\prime\prime}
be a corank one reflection overgroup of
W_{K\cup\Sigma^{\prime}}
in
W
such that
U^{\prime\prime}{\,\supseteq\,}U
. We have to show that
U^{\prime\prime}=U
. For this argument, we assume for simplicity that
\Pi
is linearly independent (this entails no loss of generality). Write
\Pi_{U^{\prime\prime}}=K\dot{\cup}\{\beta\}\dot{\cup}\Sigma^{\prime}

where
\beta=r_{U^{\prime\prime},K\dot{\cup}\Sigma^{\prime}}\in\Phi^{+}
.
Since
\alpha\in\Pi_{U}\subseteq\Phi_{U^{\prime\prime}}\subseteq\operatorname{cone}(\Pi_{U^{\prime\prime}})
, we may write

(4.8.1)

\alpha=c_{\beta}\beta+\sum_{\gamma\in K\cup\Sigma^{\prime}}c_{\gamma}\gamma,\qquad\text{\rm where $c_{\delta}\geq 0$ for all $\delta\in\Pi_{U^{\prime\prime}}$.}

We must have
c_{\beta}>0
since
s_{\alpha}\not\in W_{K\cup\Sigma^{\prime}}
. Hence

\beta=b_{\alpha}\alpha-\sum_{\gamma\in K\cup\Sigma^{\prime}}b_{\gamma}\gamma

where
b_{\alpha}=c_{\beta}^{-1}>0
and

b_{\gamma}=c_{\beta}^{-1}c_{\gamma}\geq 0
for all

\gamma\in K\cup\Sigma^{\prime}
. Define the finite subset
\Delta:=\{\,\gamma\in\Sigma^{\prime}\mid b_{\gamma}>0\,\}
of
\Sigma^{\prime}
.
For each
\delta\in\Sigma^{\prime}
, (
2.9
) implies that

0\leq-B(\beta,\delta)=\sum_{\gamma\in\Delta}b_{\gamma}B(\gamma,\delta)
since
B(K\cup\{\alpha\},\delta)\subseteq B(E,\Sigma^{\prime})\subseteq\{0\}
. Now
B(\gamma,\delta)\leq 0
for all distinct
\gamma,\delta\in\Sigma^{\prime}
. Hence if
\delta\in\Sigma^{\prime}\setminus\Delta
, we must have
B(\gamma,\delta)=0
for all
\gamma\in\Delta
. This implies that
\Delta
is a union of components of
\Sigma^{\prime}
. Moreover, for each component
\Delta^{\prime}
of
\Delta
, we have

\sum_{\gamma\in\Delta^{\prime}}b_{\gamma}B(\gamma,\delta)\geq 0
for all
\delta\in\Delta^{\prime}
, where all
b_{\gamma}>0
. Hence
the component
\Delta^{\prime}
must be of finite or affine type, by
[
32
, Ch 4]
.
But by construction,
\Sigma^{\prime}
has no components of finite type,
so
\Delta^{\prime}
is of affine type, and

\sum_{\gamma\in\Delta^{\prime}}b_{\gamma}B(\gamma,\delta)\geq 0
for all
\delta\in\Delta^{\prime}
with all
b_{\gamma}>0
implies that

\sum_{\gamma\in\Delta^{\prime}}b_{\gamma}B(\gamma,\delta)=0
for all
\delta\in\Delta^{\prime}
.

For each
\delta\in\Sigma^{\prime}
, the above proves that

B(\beta,\delta)=-\sum_{\Delta^{\prime}}\sum_{\gamma\in\Delta^{\prime}}b_{\gamma}B(\gamma,\delta)=0
, where
\Delta^{\prime}
runs over all components of
\Delta
. Thus,
\beta\in\Phi\cap\Sigma^{\prime\perp}
, which by Proposition
1.41
, is a standard parabolic root subsystem of
\Phi
with simple roots

\Pi\cap\Sigma^{\prime\perp}
.
We also have
\alpha\in\Phi_{W^{\prime}}=\Phi\cap E\subseteq\Phi\cap\Sigma^{\prime\perp}
.
Hence in (
4.8.1
),
\alpha-c_{\beta}\beta\in\operatorname{span}(\Pi\cap\Sigma^{\prime\perp})
. Linear independence of
\Pi
ensures that for
\gamma\in K\cup\Sigma^{\prime}
, we have
c_{\gamma}=0
unless
\gamma\in\Pi\cap\Sigma^{\prime\perp}
. In particular,
c_{\gamma}=0=b_{\gamma}
for
\gamma\in\Sigma^{\prime}
.
Hence
\beta=b_{\alpha}\alpha-\sum_{\gamma\in K}b_{\gamma}\gamma
.
Since
B(\alpha,\gamma)\neq 0
for only finitely many
\gamma\in K
, and all components of
K
are of finite type, this
implies that
B(\beta,\gamma)\neq 0
for only finitely many
\gamma\in K
. Since we have already seen
\beta\in\Sigma^{\prime\perp}
, it follows that
\beta\in\Phi^{+}\cap E=\Phi_{W^{\prime}}^{+}
. Let
U^{\prime\prime\prime}:=W^{\prime}_{K\cup\{\beta\}}
. Since
K\cup\{\beta\}\subseteq K\cup\{\beta\}\cup\Sigma^{\prime}=\Pi_{U^{\prime\prime}}
, we have
\Pi_{U^{\prime\prime\prime}}=K\cup\{\beta\}
, so
U^{\prime\prime\prime}
is a corank one overgroup of
W^{\prime}_{K}
.

Note that since
\Pi_{U^{\prime\prime}}=\Pi_{U^{\prime\prime\prime}}\cup\Sigma^{\prime}
where

\Pi_{U^{\prime\prime\prime}}=K\cup\{\beta\}\perp\Sigma^{\prime}
, we have

\Phi_{U^{\prime\prime}}=\Phi_{U^{\prime\prime\prime}}\dot{\cup}\Phi_{\Sigma^{\prime}}
.
Similarly,
\Pi_{U}=\Pi_{U^{\prime}}\cup\Sigma^{\prime}
where
\Pi_{U^{\prime}}=K\cup\{\alpha\}\perp\Sigma^{\prime}
and so

\Phi_{U}=\Phi_{U^{\prime}}\dot{\cup}\Phi_{\Sigma^{\prime}}
.
Since
U\subseteq U^{\prime\prime}
, we have
\Phi_{U}\subseteq\Phi_{U^{\prime\prime}}
and hence

\Phi_{U^{\prime}}\subseteq\Phi_{U^{\prime\prime\prime}}
. This implies
U^{\prime}\subseteq U^{\prime\prime\prime}
.
Since
U^{\prime\prime\prime}
is a corank one overgroup of
W^{\prime}_{K}
in
W^{\prime}

and
U^{\prime}
is a maximal corank one overgroup of
W^{\prime}_{K}
in
W^{\prime}
, this forces
U^{\prime\prime\prime}=U^{\prime}
,
\Pi_{U^{\prime\prime\prime}}=\Pi_{U^{\prime}}
,
\Pi_{U^{\prime\prime}}=\Pi_{U}
and hence
U^{\prime\prime}=U
, as required to complete the proof of (a).

We now prove (b). Let
K\in\operatorname{ob}(G)
. The function
\iota_{K}\colon X_{K}^{\prime}\to X_{\theta(K)}=X_{K\cup\Sigma^{\prime}}
is injective,
since if
U=\iota_{K}(U^{\prime})
with
U^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}_{K}
, then
\Pi_{U}=\Pi^{\prime}_{U^{\prime}}\,\dot{\cup}\,\Sigma^{\prime}
. Directly from its definition,
\rho_{K}
is positivity-preserving. It remains to show that

\rho
is a natural transformation (of the underlying set-valued functors). Consider a morphism

g=(J,w,K)
in
G^{\prime}
. Then
w^{\prime}\in W^{\prime}
. Let
(U^{\prime},\epsilon)\in\Upsilon^{\prime}_{X^{\prime}}(K)
.
We are required to show

(4.8.2)

\rho_{J}(((\Upsilon^{\prime}_{X^{\prime}}(g))(U^{\prime},\epsilon))=(\Upsilon_{X}(\theta^{-1}(g)))(\rho_{K}(U,\epsilon)).

In the left hand side of (
4.8.2
),
((\Upsilon^{\prime}_{X^{\prime}}(g))(U^{\prime},\epsilon)=(wU^{\prime}w^{-1},\eta\epsilon)
where
\eta=-1
if

\Phi^{\prime+}_{U^{\prime}}\setminus\Phi^{\prime+}_{W_{K^{\prime}}}\subseteq\Phi^{\prime}_{w}

and
\eta=1
otherwise. Hence the left hand side of (
4.8.2
) is equal to

(U^{\prime\prime},\eta\epsilon)
where
\Pi_{U^{\prime\prime}}=\Pi_{wU^{\prime}w^{-1}}\,\dot{\cup}\,\Sigma^{\prime}
.
In the right hand side of (
4.8.2
), we have
\theta^{-1}(g)=(J\dot{\cup}\Sigma^{\prime},w,K\dot{\cup}\Sigma^{\prime})
and
\rho_{K}(U^{\prime},\epsilon)=(U,\epsilon)
where
\Pi_{U}=\Pi_{U^{\prime}}\dot{\cup}\Sigma^{\prime}
. Hence the right hand side of (
4.8.2
)
is
(wUw^{-1},\eta^{\prime}\epsilon)
where

\eta^{\prime}=-1
if

\Phi^{+}_{U}\setminus\Phi^{+}_{W_{K\,\dot{\cup}\,\Sigma^{\prime}}}\subseteq\Phi_{w}

and
\eta^{\prime}=1
otherwise.

To complete the proof of (b), we therefore need only show that
wUw^{-1}=U^{\prime\prime}
and
\eta=\eta^{\prime}
.
Since
w\in W^{\prime}
where
\Pi_{W^{\prime}}\perp\Sigma^{\prime}
, we have

wUw^{-1}=w(U^{\prime}W_{\Sigma^{\prime}})w^{-1}=(wU^{\prime}w^{-1})W_{\Sigma^{\prime}}=U^{\prime\prime}.

Further,
\Phi^{\prime}_{w}:=\Phi^{\prime+}\cap w\Phi^{\prime+}=\Phi_{w}
since
W^{\prime}
is a standard parabolic subgroup of
W
and
w\in W^{\prime}
.
Now
\eta^{\prime}=\eta
follows because

\Phi^{+}_{U}\setminus\Phi^{+}_{W_{K\,\dot{\cup}\,\Sigma^{\prime}}}=(\Phi^{+}_{U^{\prime}}\,\dot{\cup}\,\Phi^{+}_{W_{\Sigma^{\prime}}})\setminus(\Phi^{+}_{W_{K}}\,\dot{\cup}\,\Phi^{+}_{W_{\Sigma^{\prime}}})=\Phi^{+}_{U^{\prime}}\setminus\Phi^{+}_{W_{K}}=\Phi^{\prime+}_{U^{\prime}}\setminus\Phi^{\prime+}_{W_{K}}.

We now turn to the proof of (c). First, let
X
denote either
N
or
Q
, and write

\rho:=\rho_{X}
. We have to show that for
K\in\operatorname{ob}(G^{\prime})
,
the component
\rho_{K}
of
\rho
is bijective as a function.
By (b), so we need only show this component is surjective.
By definition of
\rho
, it suffices to show that if
K\in\operatorname{ob}(G^{\prime})
and
U\in X_{K\cup\Sigma^{\prime}}
, there exists
U^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}_{K^{\prime}}
such that
U=\iota_{K}(U^{\prime})
. In either case
X=N
or
X=Q
, we have
U\in N_{K\cup\Sigma^{\prime}}
. That is,
U
is a rank one, finite index reflection overgroup of
W_{K\cup\Sigma^{\prime}}
in
W
.
Hence
\Pi_{U}=K\dot{\cup}\{\beta\}\dot{\cup}\Sigma^{\prime}
for some
\beta\in\Phi^{+}
. Since
W_{K\cup\Sigma^{\prime}}
has finite index in
U
, the component of
\Pi_{U}
containing
\beta
is of finite type, by Proposition
1.35
. Hence
\beta\perp\Sigma^{\prime}
(since each component of
\Sigma^{\prime}
is of infinite type)
and
B(\beta,\alpha)
is non-zero for only finitely many elements of
K
. By definition, we have
\beta\in\Phi^{+}\cap E=\Phi^{\prime+}
. Hence
U^{\prime}:=W^{\prime}_{K\cup\{\beta\}}
is a corank one reflection overgroup of
W^{\prime}_{K}
in
W^{\prime}
, with
\Pi^{\prime}_{U^{\prime}}=\Pi_{U^{\prime}}=K\cup\{\beta\}\subseteq\Pi_{U}
. In particular,
\beta\in\Phi_{W^{\prime}}
. Since
K\cup\{\beta\}\perp\Sigma^{\prime}
, we have
\Phi_{U}=\Phi^{\prime}_{U^{\prime}}\,\dot{\cup}\,\Phi_{\Sigma^{\prime}}
. Hence

\Phi^{\prime}_{U^{\prime}}\setminus\Phi^{\prime}_{K}=(\Phi^{\prime}_{U^{\prime}}\,\dot{\cup}\,\Phi_{\Sigma^{\prime}})\setminus(\Phi^{\prime}_{K}\,\dot{\cup}\,\Phi_{\Sigma^{\prime}})=\Phi_{U}\setminus\Phi_{K\cup\Sigma^{\prime}}

is finite, and so
U^{\prime}
is a finite index, corank one reflection overgroup of
W^{\prime}_{K}
in
W^{\prime}
. That is,
U^{\prime}\in N^{\prime}_{K^{\prime}}
. By the definitions,
\iota_{K}(U^{\prime})=U
, which completes the proof of (c) in case
X=N
.

If, instead,
X=Q
, then, additionally,
U
is a parabolic subgroup of
W
, and we have to show that
U^{\prime}
is a parabolic subgroup of
W^{\prime}
. Since
U
is parabolic in
W
, there exists
w\in W
such that
w\Pi_{U}\subseteq\Pi
. That is,

w(K\dot{\cup}\{\beta\}\dot{\cup}\Sigma^{\prime})\subseteq\Pi
.
Then
L:=w(K\dot{\cup}\Sigma^{\prime})\subseteq\Pi
, and

g:=(L,w,K\cup\Sigma^{\prime})
is a morphism in
G
since
K\cup\Sigma^{\prime}\in\operatorname{ob}(G)
.
Then
g^{\prime}:=\theta(g)=(J,w,K)\in\operatorname{mor}(G^{\prime})
where
J=L\setminus\Sigma^{\prime}
. We have
w\in W^{\prime}
,

w\Sigma^{\prime}=\Sigma^{\prime}
,
J=wK\subseteq\Pi^{\prime}
and

L=J\,\dot{\cup}\,\Sigma^{\prime}
. We have

w\Pi_{U^{\prime}}=w(K\dot{\cup}\{\beta\})\subseteq w\Pi_{U}\cap\Phi^{\prime}\subseteq\Pi\cap\Phi^{\prime}=\Pi^{\prime}=\Pi_{W^{\prime}}

and

w\in W^{\prime}
. Hence
U^{\prime}
is a parabolic subgroup of
W^{\prime}
, as
required.
∎

Proposition 4.9
.

Let notation and assumptions be as in Proposition
4.8
. Then
\rho_{X}
induces an isomorphism

(G^{\prime},(\Upsilon^{\prime}_{X^{\prime}})^{{\mathrm{re}}})\hookrightarrow(G^{\prime},\Upsilon_{X}^{{\mathrm{re}}}\circ\theta^{-1})
of signed
G^{\prime}
-sets

Proof.

This follows using Theorem
1.33
(c) and
Proposition
4.8
(c). Details are omitted. ∎

5.
Realized root systems of Brink-Howlett groupoids

Proof of Theorem
1.49
(a).

We show that
\iota\colon\Upsilon_{X}\rightarrow{\mathcal{V}}
is a natural transformation of set-valued functors on
G
, by checking that for any morphism
(K,w,J)
of
G
,

{\mathcal{V}}(K,w,J)\circ\iota_{J}=\iota_{K}\circ\Upsilon_{X}(K,w,J).

If
(U,\epsilon)\in\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Upsilon^{{\kern-5.75003pt\kern 6.13892pt}}_{{\kern-9.43843pt{J}\kern 6.13892pt{X}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Upsilon^{{\kern-5.75003pt\kern 6.13892pt}}_{{\kern-9.43843pt{J}\kern 6.13892pt{X}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Upsilon^{{\kern-3.42223pt\kern 3.81111pt}}_{{\kern-6.19965pt{J}\kern 3.81111pt{X}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Upsilon^{{\kern-2.33334pt\kern 2.72223pt}}_{{\kern-5.11076pt{J}\kern 2.72223pt{X}}}}
, then

({\mathcal{V}}(K,w,J)\circ\iota_{J})(U,\epsilon)={\mathcal{V}}(K,w,J)(\epsilon\pi_{J}(r_{U,J}))=\epsilon\pi_{K}(wr_{U,J})

and, since
w(J)=K
implies that
w\pi_{J}(v)=\pi_{K}(wv)
,

(\iota_{K}\circ\Upsilon_{X}(K,w,J))(U,\epsilon)=\iota_{K}(wUw^{-1},\epsilon\nu)=(-1)^{\eta(w,U,J)}\epsilon\pi_{K}(r_{wUw^{-1},K})

where
\eta(w,U,J)
is as defined in Theorem
1.33
.
Thus, we need to show that

\pi_{K}(wr_{U,J})=(-1)^{\eta(w,U,J)}\pi_{K}(r_{wUw^{-1},K}).

If
\eta(w,U,J)=0
, then
(U\setminus W_{J})\cap N(w^{-1})={\emptyset}

and Proposition
4.3
gives
wr_{U,J}=r_{wUw^{-1},K}
as required. Otherwise, we have
\eta(w,U,J)=1
and
(U\setminus W_{J})\cap T\subseteq N(w^{-1})
. Then
Proposition
4.3
gives
ww_{L}r_{U,J}=-r_{wUw^{-1},K}
where
L\,\dot{\cup}\,\{r_{U,J}\}
is the (spherical) component of
\Pi_{U}
containing
r_{U,J}
, with
L\subseteq J
. So to complete the proof of the above displayed equation, we have to show that

\pi_{K}(wr_{U,J})=\pi_{K}(ww_{L}r_{U,J})
.
This holds since
w_{L}r_{U,J}\in r_{U,J}+\operatorname{span}(L)
where

\pi_{K}(w\operatorname{span}(L))\subseteq\pi_{K}(w\operatorname{span}(J))=\pi_{K}(\operatorname{span}(K))=\{0\}
.

This competes the proof that
\iota
is a natural transformation.
It is obvious from the definitions that the components of
\iota
are
\{\pm\}
-equivariant maps. This proves that
F
is a weakly realized signed groupoid-set, as required.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.49
(b).

Suppose now that
\Pi
is linearly independent and that
X=P
. It suffices to show that each
\iota_{J}:\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Upsilon^{{\kern-5.75003pt\kern 6.13892pt}}_{{\kern-9.43843pt{J}\kern 6.13892pt{P}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Upsilon^{{\kern-5.75003pt\kern 6.13892pt}}_{{\kern-9.43843pt{J}\kern 6.13892pt{P}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Upsilon^{{\kern-3.42223pt\kern 3.81111pt}}_{{\kern-6.19965pt{J}\kern 3.81111pt{P}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Upsilon^{{\kern-2.33334pt\kern 2.72223pt}}_{{\kern-5.11076pt{J}\kern 2.72223pt{P}}}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{V}}(J)
is an injection for each object
J
of
G
. Let us suppose that
(U_{i},\epsilon_{i})\in\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Upsilon^{{\kern-5.75003pt\kern 6.13892pt}}_{{\kern-9.43843pt{J}\kern 6.13892pt{P}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Upsilon^{{\kern-5.75003pt\kern 6.13892pt}}_{{\kern-9.43843pt{J}\kern 6.13892pt{P}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Upsilon^{{\kern-3.42223pt\kern 3.81111pt}}_{{\kern-6.19965pt{J}\kern 3.81111pt{P}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Upsilon^{{\kern-2.33334pt\kern 2.72223pt}}_{{\kern-5.11076pt{J}\kern 2.72223pt{P}}}}
for
i=1,2
are such that

\iota_{J}(U_{1},\epsilon_{1})=\iota_{J}(U_{2},\epsilon_{2})
.
This means that

\epsilon_{1}\pi_{J}(r_{U_{1},J})=\epsilon_{2}\pi_{J}(r_{U_{2},J})
.
Hence
r_{U_{1},J}\in\operatorname{span}(\{r_{U_{2},J}\}\cup J_{\mathrm{fin}})
. Thus, we get that
\operatorname{span}(\Pi_{U_{1}})=\operatorname{span}(\Pi_{U_{2}})
where
\Pi_{U_{i}}=\{r_{U_{i},J}\}\cup J
for
i=1,2
. Since we are assuming that
U_{i}
is parabolic and that
\Pi
is linearly independent, we have that
\Phi_{U_{i}}=\operatorname{span}(\Pi_{U_{i}})\cap\Phi
for
i=1,2
. Thus,

\Phi_{U_{1}}=\operatorname{span}(\Pi_{U_{1}})\cap\Phi=\operatorname{span}(\Pi_{U_{2}})\cap\Phi=\Phi_{U_{2}}

and thus
U_{1}=U_{2}
. Note that if
\epsilon_{1}
and
\epsilon_{2}
had opposite signs, then one would get
2\pi_{J}(r_{U_{1},J})=0
and thus
\pi_{J}(r_{U_{1},J})=0
. But since
W_{J}{\,\subsetneq\,}U_{1}
, and because
\Phi_{J}=\operatorname{span}(J)\cap\Phi
, we must have that
\pi_{J}(r_{U_{1},J})\neq 0
, a contradiction. Therefore,
\epsilon_{1}=\epsilon_{2}
, and thus
\iota_{J}
is injective for any object
J
of
G
. This proves part (b).
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.49
(c).

Suppose now that
|J|=1
. Let
J=\{\alpha\}
. Let
(U_{i},\epsilon_{i})\in\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Upsilon^{{\kern-5.75003pt\kern 6.13892pt}}_{{\kern-9.43843pt{J}\kern 6.13892pt{X}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Upsilon^{{\kern-5.75003pt\kern 6.13892pt}}_{{\kern-9.43843pt{J}\kern 6.13892pt{X}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Upsilon^{{\kern-3.42223pt\kern 3.81111pt}}_{{\kern-6.19965pt{J}\kern 3.81111pt{X}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Upsilon^{{\kern-2.33334pt\kern 2.72223pt}}_{{\kern-5.11076pt{J}\kern 2.72223pt{X}}}}
for
i=1,2
. Suppose that

\iota_{J}(U_{1},\epsilon_{1})=\iota_{J}(U_{2},\epsilon_{2})
.
That is,

\epsilon_{1}\pi_{J}(r_{U_{1},J})=\epsilon_{2}\pi_{J}(r_{U_{2},J})
.
If
\epsilon_{1}\neq\epsilon_{2}
, then
\pi_{J}(r_{U_{1},J})=-\pi_{J}(r_{U_{2},J})
, so

\pi_{J}(r_{U_{1},J}+r_{U_{2},J})=0
,
meaning that
r_{U_{1},J}+r_{U_{2},J}=c\alpha

for some
c\in\mathbb{R}
. Note that if
c\leq 0
, then

r_{U_{1},J}+r_{U_{2},J}+(-c)\alpha=0

where the left hand side is a non-negative linear combination of positive roots, with at least one coefficient strictly positive. This contradicts the positive independence of
\Pi
. If
c>0
, then

c=cB(\alpha,\alpha)=B(\alpha,c\alpha)=B(\alpha,r_{U_{1},J}+r_{U_{2},J})=B(\alpha,r_{U_{1},J})+B(\alpha,r_{U_{2},J})

where the two terms on the right hand side above are non-positive since
\{\alpha,r_{U_{1},J}\}
and
\{\alpha,r_{U_{2},J}\}
are root bases. This contradicts
c>0
.
If
c=0
, then
r_{U_{1},J}=-r_{U_{2},J}
contrary to the fact
r_{U_{i},J}
is a positive root for
i=1,2
.

Thus, we must conclude that
\epsilon_{1}=\epsilon_{2}
and therefore
\pi_{J}(r_{U_{1},J})=\pi_{J}(r_{U_{2},J})
.
That is,
r_{U_{1},J}=r_{U_{2},J}+c\alpha

for some
c\in\mathbb{R}
. By Proposition
2.44
(a),
either
r_{U_{1},J}=r_{U_{2},J}
or
r_{U_{2},J}=s_{\alpha}(r_{U_{1},J})
. We show that
r_{U_{1},J}=r_{U_{2},J}
in either case. To see this, suppose that

s_{\alpha}(r_{U_{1},J})=r_{U_{2},J}
. Because
\{\alpha,r_{U_{i},J}\}=\Pi_{U_{i}}
, we have
B(\alpha,r_{U_{i},J})\leq 0
for
i=1,2
. Hence

0\geq B(\alpha,r_{U_{2},J})=B(\alpha,s_{\alpha}r_{U_{1},J})=B(s_{\alpha}(\alpha),r_{U_{1},J})=-B(\alpha,r_{U_{1},J})\geq 0.

Thus,
B(\alpha,r_{U_{2},J})=0
, and so
r_{U_{1},J}=s_{\alpha}(r_{U_{2},J})=r_{U_{2},J}
. Hence
r_{U_{1},J}=r_{U_{2},J}
, which implies that
(U_{1},\epsilon)=(U_{2},\epsilon)
. Thus, we have that
\iota_{J}
is injective for each object
J
with
|J|=1
. This proves
Theorem
1.49
(c).
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.49
(d).

Assume
X=N
or
X=Q
.
We need to establish that
\iota\colon\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Upsilon^{{\kern-5.75003pt\kern 6.13892pt}}_{{\kern-9.43843pt{J}\kern 6.13892pt{X}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Upsilon^{{\kern-5.75003pt\kern 6.13892pt}}_{{\kern-9.43843pt{J}\kern 6.13892pt{X}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Upsilon^{{\kern-3.42223pt\kern 3.81111pt}}_{{\kern-6.19965pt{J}\kern 3.81111pt{X}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Upsilon^{{\kern-2.33334pt\kern 2.72223pt}}_{{\kern-5.11076pt{J}\kern 2.72223pt{X}}}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{V}}(J)
is injective for each object
J
of
G
. Suppose that
(U_{i},\epsilon_{i})\in\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Upsilon^{{\kern-5.75003pt\kern 6.13892pt}}_{{\kern-9.43843pt{J}\kern 6.13892pt{X}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Upsilon^{{\kern-5.75003pt\kern 6.13892pt}}_{{\kern-9.43843pt{J}\kern 6.13892pt{X}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Upsilon^{{\kern-3.42223pt\kern 3.81111pt}}_{{\kern-6.19965pt{J}\kern 3.81111pt{X}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Upsilon^{{\kern-2.33334pt\kern 2.72223pt}}_{{\kern-5.11076pt{J}\kern 2.72223pt{X}}}}
for
i=1,2
with
\iota_{J}(U_{1},\epsilon_{1})=\iota_{J}(U_{2},\epsilon_{2})
. Then
\epsilon_{1}\pi_{J}(r_{U_{1},J})=\epsilon_{2}\pi_{J}(r_{U_{2},J})
.

Consider first the case in which
\epsilon_{1}=-\epsilon_{2}
. Then

\pi_{J}(r_{U_{1},J}+r_{U_{2},J})=0
.
That is,
r_{U_{1},J}+r_{U_{2},J}\in\operatorname{span}(J_{\mathrm{fin}})
.
Note that
B(r_{U_{i},J},\alpha)\leq 0
for all
\alpha\in J_{\mathrm{fin}}
and for
i=1,2
. Hence
B(r_{U_{1},J}+r_{U_{2},J},\alpha)\leq 0
for all
\alpha\in J_{\mathrm{fin}}
.
Since
r_{U_{1},J}+r_{U_{2},J}\in\operatorname{span}(J_{\mathrm{fin}})
, this gives

B(r_{U_{1},J}+r_{U_{2},J},r_{U_{1},J}+r_{U_{2},J})\leq 0
.
But the bilinear form
B(-,-)
is positive definite on
\operatorname{span}(J_{\mathrm{fin}})
, so this implies that
r_{U_{1},J}+r_{U_{2},J}=0
, meaning that
r_{U_{1},J}=-r_{U_{2},J}
. This gives a contradiction since
r_{U_{i},J}\in\Phi^{+}
for
i=1,2
, so this case cannot occur.

We must therefore have
\epsilon_{1}=\epsilon_{2}
, and so
\pi_{J}(r_{U_{1},J})=\pi_{J}(r_{U_{2},J})
.
Thus,
r_{U_{2},J}\in r_{U_{1},J}+\operatorname{span}(J_{\mathrm{fin}})
.
Since
U_{1}
is a finite index overgroup of
W_{J}
,
the component
L_{1}\,\dot{\cup}\,\{r_{U_{1},J}\}
of
\{r_{U_{1},J}\}\cup J
containing
r_{U_{1},J}
is of finite type, and
L_{1}\subseteq J_{\mathrm{fin}}
. Hence
L_{1}\,\dot{\cup}\,\{r_{U_{1},J}\}
is also the component of
J_{\mathrm{fin}}\,\dot{\cup}\,\{r_{U_{1},J}\}

containing
r_{U_{1},J}
. This shows that
W_{J_{\mathrm{fin}}\,\dot{\cup}\,\{r_{U_{1},J}\}}
is a finite index, corank one reflection overgroup of
W_{J_{\mathrm{fin}}}
.
By Theorem
1.43
, there is a corank one, finite index overgroup
U_{0}
of
W_{J_{\mathrm{fin}}}
which is parabolic in
W
and contains
W_{J_{\mathrm{fin}}\,\dot{\cup}\,\{r_{U_{1},J}\}}
. By Proposition
2.30
,
\Phi_{J_{\mathrm{fin}}}=\operatorname{span}(J_{\mathrm{fin}})\cap\Phi
and
J_{\mathrm{fin}}
is linearly independent.
It follows that
r_{U_{0},J_{\mathrm{fin}}}\not\in\operatorname{span}(J_{\mathrm{fin}})
and
\Pi_{U_{0}}=J_{\mathrm{fin}}\,\dot{\cup}\,\{r_{U_{0},J_{\mathrm{fin}}}\}
is linearly independent. Since
W_{J_{\mathrm{fin}}\,\dot{\cup}\,\{r_{U_{1},J}\}}\subseteq U_{0}
with
r_{U_{1},J}\not\in\Phi_{J_{\mathrm{fin}}}
, we see that

r_{U_{1},J}\in\operatorname{span}(\Pi_{U_{0}})\setminus\operatorname{span}(J_{\mathrm{fin}})
.
Since
r_{U_{2},J}\in r_{U_{1},J}+\operatorname{span}(J_{\mathrm{fin}})
, it follows that

r_{U_{2},J}\in\operatorname{span}(\Pi_{U_{0}})\setminus\operatorname{span}(J_{\mathrm{fin}})
, too.

Let
\alpha:=r_{U_{0},J_{\mathrm{fin}}}
and
M:=\{\alpha\}\,\dot{\cup}\,L_{0}
, where
L_{0}\subseteq J_{\mathrm{fin}}
, denote the (spherical) component of
\Pi_{U_{0}}
containing
\alpha
. Write
U:=W_{M}
, so
\Phi_{U}
is a component of
\Phi_{U_{0}}
with canonical simple system
\Pi_{U}=M
, and all other components of
\Phi_{U_{0}}
are contained in
\Phi_{J_{\mathrm{fin}}}
. We therefore have
r_{U_{i},J}\in\Phi_{U}\setminus\Phi_{L_{0}}
for
i=1,2
. Hence
r_{U_{2},J}-r_{U_{1},J}\in\operatorname{span}(M)\cap\operatorname{span}J_{\mathrm{fin}}=\operatorname{span}(L_{0})
, since
M\cup J_{\mathrm{fin}}=\Pi_{U_{0}}
, which is linearly independent, and

M\cap J_{\mathrm{fin}}=L_{0}
.

To summarize, we have a finite irreducible based root system
\Phi_{U}
with simple roots
\Pi_{U}=M
, a simple root
\alpha\in M
and roots
r_{U_{i},J}\in\Phi_{U}\setminus\Phi_{W_{L_{0}}}
, where
L_{0}:=M\setminus\{\alpha\}
, such that
r_{U_{2},J}\in r_{U_{1},J}+\operatorname{span}(L_{0})
. By Proposition
2.44
(c), it follows that

r_{U_{2},J}\in W_{L_{0}}r_{U_{1},J}
. But for
i=1,2
, we have
r_{U_{i},J}\,\dot{\cup}\,L_{0}\subseteq\Pi_{U_{i}}
and hence, by Theorem
2.9
,
r_{U_{i},J}\in-\mathcal{C}_{W_{L_{0}}}
.
By Proposition
2.18
, it follows that

r_{U_{1},J}=r_{U_{2},J}
. This implies that
U_{1}=U_{2}
and hence
(U_{1},\epsilon_{1})=(U_{2},\epsilon_{2})
as required.
∎

Proof of Proposition
1.50
.

We prove (a). Let
J
be an object of
G
. By Proposition
2.30
, we have
\operatorname{span}(J_{\mathrm{fin}})\cap\Phi=\Phi_{J_{\mathrm{fin}}}
. If
(U,\epsilon)\in\Upsilon_{X}(J)
, then
r_{U,J}\in\Phi_{U}\setminus\Phi_{J}\subseteq\Phi\setminus\Phi_{J_{\mathrm{fin}}}
and therefore
r_{U,J}\not\in\operatorname{span}(J_{\mathrm{fin}})=\ker(\pi_{J})
. Hence
\iota_{J}(U,\epsilon)=\pi_{J}(\epsilon r_{U,J})\neq 0
. This proves (a).

We prove (b). We have
\Pi=J_{\mathrm{fin}}\cup(\Pi\setminus J_{\mathrm{fin}})
where
J_{\mathrm{fin}}\subseteq\ker(\pi_{J})
. Since
\pi_{J}
is linear,

\begin{split}\pi_{J}(\operatorname{cone}(\Pi))&=\operatorname{cone}(\pi_{J}(\Pi))=\operatorname{cone}(\pi_{J}(J_{\mathrm{fin}}\cup(\Pi\setminus J_{\mathrm{fin}})))\\
&=\operatorname{cone}(\pi_{J}(\Pi\setminus J_{\mathrm{fin}}))=\operatorname{cone}(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-3.69446pt\kern 4.08334pt}}_{{\kern-6.47188pt{J}\kern 4.08334pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-2.52779pt\kern 2.91667pt}}_{{\kern-5.3052pt{J}\kern 2.91667pt}}}),\end{split}

proving (b).

We prove (c). Let
(U,+)\in{\Upsilon}_{X}^{+}(J)
.
Then
\iota_{J}(U,+)=\pi_{J}(r_{U,J})\subseteq\pi_{J}(\operatorname{cone}\Pi)=\operatorname{cone}(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-3.69446pt\kern 4.08334pt}}_{{\kern-6.47188pt{J}\kern 4.08334pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-2.52779pt\kern 2.91667pt}}_{{\kern-5.3052pt{J}\kern 2.91667pt}}})
by (b). We conclude that
\iota_{J}({\Upsilon}_{X}^{+}(J))\subseteq\operatorname{cone}(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-3.69446pt\kern 4.08334pt}}_{{\kern-6.47188pt{J}\kern 4.08334pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-2.52779pt\kern 2.91667pt}}_{{\kern-5.3052pt{J}\kern 2.91667pt}}})
, proving (c).

We prove (d).
Let
x\in\operatorname{cone}(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-3.69446pt\kern 4.08334pt}}_{{\kern-6.47188pt{J}\kern 4.08334pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-2.52779pt\kern 2.91667pt}}_{{\kern-5.3052pt{J}\kern 2.91667pt}}})\cap-\operatorname{cone}(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-3.69446pt\kern 4.08334pt}}_{{\kern-6.47188pt{J}\kern 4.08334pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-2.52779pt\kern 2.91667pt}}_{{\kern-5.3052pt{J}\kern 2.91667pt}}})
. Note that, by (b), we have
\operatorname{cone}(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-6.18059pt\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-9.86899pt{J}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-3.69446pt\kern 4.08334pt}}_{{\kern-6.47188pt{J}\kern 4.08334pt}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{J}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-2.52779pt\kern 2.91667pt}}_{{\kern-5.3052pt{J}\kern 2.91667pt}}})=\pi_{J}(\operatorname{cone}(\Pi\setminus J_{\mathrm{fin}}))
. Hence
x=\pi_{J}(y)=-\pi_{J}(z)
for some elements

y,z\in\operatorname{cone}(\Pi\setminus J_{\mathrm{fin}})
. Hence
y+z\in\operatorname{cone}(\Pi\setminus J_{\mathrm{fin}})
. We also have
y+z\in\ker(\pi_{J})=\operatorname{span}(J_{\mathrm{fin}})
since
\pi_{J}(y+z)=x-x=0
. Hence

y+z\in\operatorname{cone}(\Pi\setminus J_{\mathrm{fin}})\cap\operatorname{span}(J_{\mathrm{fin}})=\{0\}
, by
Proposition
2.30
(e). Since
y,z\in\operatorname{cone}(\Pi)
, this implies that
y=z=0
, by positive independence of
\Pi
. Hence
x=0
, completing the proof of (d).

Parts (e)–(f) follow from the definitions by elementary linear algebra.
∎

Proof of Corollary
1.51
.

By Theorem
1.33
,
(G,\Upsilon_{X}^{{\mathrm{re}}})

is equal to either
(G,\Upsilon_{N})
or
(G,\Upsilon_{Q})
, and

(G,\Upsilon_{\Lambda}^{{\mathrm{rec}}})
is isomorphic to
(G,\Upsilon_{Q})
. Hence parts (a)–(b) follow from Theorem
1.49
. The functor
\Upsilon_{\Lambda}^{{\mathrm{rec}}}
is faithful (since it determines the weak orders of
G
), and it is a subfunctor of
{\mathcal{V}}
(regarding both as set valued functors), so (c) follows also. Part (d) follows directly from Proposition
1.50
(a),(c),(d).
∎

6.
Bilinear forms of realized Brink-Howlett groupoids

6.1.

We say that the form
B
is positive definite on a subspace
U
of
V
if the restriction of
B
to
U
is positive definite i.e. if
B(v,v)>0
for all non-zero
v\in U
.

For any subset
L
of
\Pi
, define the
subspace
V_{L}
of
V
by

V_{L}:=\{\,v\in V\mid B(v,\alpha)\neq\{0\}\text{ \rm for only finitely many $\alpha\in L$}\,\}.

Proposition 6.2
.

Let
L\subseteq\Pi
.

(a)

If
L
is finite, then
V_{L}=V
.

(b)

If
\alpha\in\Phi\cap V_{L}
, then
B(\alpha,\beta)\neq 0
for only finitely many
\beta\in L
.

(c)
